ULA's Vulcan rocket is set to return to flight on Friday the 13th
2026-10-28

ULA's Vulcan rocket is set to return to flight on Friday the 13th

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
United Launch Alliance announced Wednesday it plans to return its Vulcan rocket to service on November 13, nine months after a solid rocket booster anomaly threw the company’s 2026 launch schedule into oblivion. The return-to-flight mission will launch 40 satellites for the Amazon Leo broadband constellation, the first of 38 Vulcan flights Amazon has booked to deploy the bulk of its network. The mission will lift off from Cape Canaveral Space Force Station, Florida, and ULA said the launch date is pending range approval. The launch is critical for ULA after its new flagship rocket suffered nozzle failures on its solid rocket boosters on two of its first four missions, most recently during a flight in February. In both cases, the rocket overcame the nozzle failure and completed its mission, but officials at the US Space Force, ULA’s primary customer, lost confidence in Vulcan. Space Force officials will closely scrutinize Vulcan’s return-to-flight mission. The Pentagon has entrusted ULA’s Vulcan rocket for roughly half of its major launches through the early 2030s. The Space Force certified the Vulcan rocket for national security launches in 2025, then flew two missions on Vulcan before its grounding after the February booster incident. In a statement, United Launch Alliance said engineers “made great strides” in understanding the booster anomaly that occurred in February. Engineers “have identified several potential contributors, which have been mitigated through design and process improvements to the nozzle,” ULA said. “To support our customers, this upcoming launch will utilize the improved nozzle design, and additional testing and analysis will continue in parallel with the mission,” ULA said. “By flying the improved design, ULA gains valuable flight data that validates these improvements and is the most beneficial path in support of all our customers.” ULA and Northrop Grumman, the booster manufacturer, have not publicly identified the root cause of the nozzle malfunction in February. Investigators blamed the nozzle problem’s first occurrence in 2024 on a manufacturing defect in an insulator meant to protect the nozzle’s metallic structure from the rocket’s superheated exhaust. Neither company responded to questions from Ars on Wednesday. The Space Force is counting on United Launch Alliance, a 50-50 joint venture between Boeing and Lockheed Martin, to deliver into orbit a backlog of the military’s communications, navigation, missile warning, and surveillance satellites. Amazon Leo, a potential competitor to SpaceX’s Starlink network, also has an inventory of broadband satellites in storage waiting for rides on the Vulcan rocket. With ULA’s delays, the Space Force has moved six missions off the Vulcan rocket to expedite their launch on SpaceX Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy rockets. ULA received six future missions originally assigned to SpaceX, the military’s other certified launch provider. The swaps included three GPS satellites that moved off ULA’s Vulcan rocket for launches last year and earlier this year on SpaceX Falcon 9s. Space Systems Command, which oversees military launch acquisition and procurement, also swapped one ULA launch for the Space Development Agency with another on SpaceX. A launch carrying the Air Force Research Laboratory’s Oracle-M lunar mission will now fly on SpaceX’s Falcon Heavy rocket instead of ULA’s Vulcan, which will receive a future SpaceX-assigned launch in compensation. Space Systems Command completed a similar swap to move a large strategic missile warning satellite from Vulcan to Falcon Heavy. So far the swaps have had no effect on the number of missions the Space Force has awarded to ULA and SpaceX. The trades are separate from the Space Force’s assignment of unallocated future missions to launch providers, which may already be hamstrung by SpaceX’s plans to retire the Falcon rocket family. “These swaps are executed in coordination with and with the support of both launch service providers,” a Space Systems Command spokesperson said. “We are grateful for the flexibility shown by our launch service providers during these swaps to help us ensure we can deliver capabilities to the warfighter when and where they are needed.” Brig. Gen. Eric Zarybnisky, the chief rocket buyer at Space Systems Command, said the military wants to see data from commercial flights on the Vulcan rocket before clearing ULA to resume national security launches. At least two Amazon Leo missions are on the Vulcan launch schedule before it flies another national security mission, perhaps early next year. “Those flights will have a significant number of solids,” Zarybnisky said. “It’ll depend a lot on what we see, not only on the engineering and tests that we’re doing on the ground, but also what we see from the flight data from the commercial missions they fly.” The Vulcan launch next month will be the first to fly with a full complement of six solid rocket boosters. The flight will also debut an extended payload fairing to accommodate Amazon’s satellite stack and a shortened Centaur upper stage optimized for low-Earth orbit missions.

As US ties fray, Europe plans first undersea cable link to Greenland
2026-10-28

As US ties fray, Europe plans first undersea cable link to Greenland

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
The European Union plans to create the first subsea Internet cable directly connecting Europe to Greenland. The Pan-Arctic Cable project could also extend to Canada and eventually link with Japan and South Korea, notably bypassing the United States. The Pan-Arctic Cable is described as a European Union flagship project in a European Commission joint communication published on October 20, 2026. The policy document describes a new European strategy for the Arctic that supports “a sovereign, secure and resilient submarine cable connection linking Greenland directly to the EU,” while also suggesting that “like-minded partners like Canada” could provide a stepping stone for future cable expansion toward Asia. “It is interesting that this project is even being considered, because five years ago it would have seemed like not a particularly commercially attractive proposition,” Elisabeth Braw, a senior fellow at the Atlantic Council who leads the Threats to the Global Maritime Order Initiative, told Ars. “But the fact that it’s being considered is very much on trend and a sign of our times.” The project has gained official support after repeated Trump administration attempts to claim Greenland were finally settled through a trilateral security agreement between the United States, Greenland and Denmark in September 2026. The agreement preserves Greenland’s autonomous territory status within the Kingdom of Denmark while expanding US military access. But relations have grown increasingly strained between the United States and its allies in Europe and Canada since President Trump initiated trade wars with multiple countries and has taken an increasingly pro-Russia stance on the war in Ukraine. Europe has also been eyeing new Internet cable routes to Asia because more than 90 percent of communications between Europe and Asia run through undersea cables in the Red Sea, according to TeleGeography. Maintaining such cables has become riskier since open warfare resumed between the Yemeni government and Houthi faction this year, with Houthi forces having fired missiles and drones at commercial ships in the Bab el-Mandeb Strait chokepoint and the broader Red Sea. The Pan-Arctic Cable took shape when European companies and research organizations joined forces with a Greenland telecom operator to sign an agreement on developing the project in 2025, according to Submarine Cable Networks. Since that time, the project has received European Union backing through the Connecting Europe Facility Digital program. The European Union has already invested $119 million (107 million euros) to “advance Greenland’s secure and sovereign connectivity” along with funding the Pan-Arctic Cable plan, according to the new European Commission policy document on Europe’s Arctic strategy. But the goal of eventually extending the undersea cable to Japan and South Korea by way of Canada faces considerable challenges, including requiring more European icebreaker ships to support the installation and maintenance of the cable in icy Arctic waters. Multiple pan-Arctic projects have been proposed in the past and some are still in development. One earlier project known as the Arctic Connect subsea cable aimed to connect Europe and Asia using the Northern Sea Route running along Russia’s Arctic coastline—but that was eventually halted in May 2021 before the full-scale Russian invasion of Ukraine led to growing Russian isolation from Europe. Still, the Pan-Arctic Cable is not the only subsea cable project that could strengthen Europe’s ties to friendlier nations. In September 2026, the Norwegian company Bulk Infrastructure Group AS began looking for a Canadian partner to take a majority ownership stake in a new transatlantic cable directly linking Europe to Canada, according to Bloomberg News. That proposed project has been named the Leif Erikson system. In previous decades, “geopolitics played virtually no role when companies considered where to put their cables and what countries to connect,” said Braw, whose book Undersea War explores why the world has become more dangerous for the Internet’s undersea backbone. But the focus on maximizing data connectivity and Internet traffic has given way to more complex planning that also accounts for international relations and the growing risk of adversaries carrying out surveillance or sabotage of cables. “We are going to see more cables that connect friendly countries, and then you have to figure out how you can connect friendly countries without going through unfriendly countries’ exclusive economic zones or territorial waters,” Braw told Ars. Growing international tensions have coincided with suspicious incidents of Russian- and Chinese-linked commercial ships severing European undersea cables in the Baltic Sea by dragging their anchors for long periods of time. In spring 2026, NATO countries’ forces also confronted Russian submarines that were practicing how to disable undersea cables in Arctic waters near Norway, Reuters reported. Europe’s new Arctic strategy document addresses such security threats by stating that the European Union will establish an Arctic Cable Hub to “monitor, protect and support the repair of submarine cables in the North Atlantic-Arctic.” It also says the European Union will invest in “smart technologies on cables that act as early warning systems for potential threats.” Such risks for undersea infrastructure are not limited to the Baltic Sea or Atlantic Ocean. In the Pacific Ocean, Chinese-linked commercial ships have also severed undersea cables near Taiwan. Analysts have documented Chinese research vessels spending unusual amounts of time near Pacific undersea cables and warned of the rising risks for US-linked subsea cable projects that are often owned by Big Tech companies such as Google and Meta. Meanwhile, Iran has threatened to charge fees to subsea cable operators whose cables run through the Strait of Hormuz chokepoint. That action comes as part of a broader regional war in the Middle East that began when the United States and Israel attacked Iran in February 2026—and Iran has drawn up plans to potentially attack undersea cables if hostilities ramp up once again.

Peptide peddlers cheer as RFK Jr. unilaterally grants access, overruling FDA
2026-10-28

Peptide peddlers cheer as RFK Jr. unilaterally grants access, overruling FDA

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
Anti-vaccine Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy has unilaterally passed a rule that will allow specialty pharmacies to sell six specific peptide drugs, overruling scientists at the Food and Drug Administration. The peptides are popular among members of Kennedy’s Make America Health Again movement and many prominent MAHA figures and Kennedy allies sell them. Kennedy said he uses some of the peptides and called himself a “big fan.” But FDA scientists continue to say there is insufficient evidence to show if they’re effective and they are potentially dangerous. In 2023, the safety concerns led the FDA to remove the six peptides from a list of drugs that can be made by specialty pharmacies called compounding pharmacies—which make customized formulations of drugs for patients, or, in cases such as this, specialty drugs in bulk. Kennedy’s new rule sidesteps the need to formally undo that FDA action. The rule is titled “Enforcement Discretion Policy Regarding 503A Compounding Using Certain Peptide Bulk Drug Substances” and was made available Wednesday on the Federal Register, with the final publication data set for October 30. The rule does not change the regulatory status of the peptide; instead, it states the Department of Health and Human Services (HHS) will no longer take enforcement action against compounding pharmacies for making the peptides, essentially giving them a green light to freely produce and sell them. Kennedy had quietly proposed this rule earlier this month. Along with the notice in the Federal Register, HHS also posted a more detailed explanation of the policy today that includes more of Kennedy’s rationale for easing enforcement of the six select peptides, which are: BPC-157, Epitalon, KPV, MOTS-c, Semax, and TB-500. One reason cited for all the peptides was that an FDA advisory panel voted in July to add these six peptides back to the list of drugs compound pharmacies can make. However, prior to the vote, Kennedy stacked that 12-member committee with eight new members. At least six of Kennedy’s additions have ties to peptide-related businesses and clinics, raising serious conflict-of-interest concerns. Another explanation for allowing the peptides is that the safety concerns are mainly hypothetical or “inferential” and not based on observed harm in humans. But this is mainly because there are few to no studies involving humans that show either benefits or harms from the peptides. Still, Kennedy argues in the policy that making the peptides more available through compounding pharmacies will improve safety because it will keep people from buying them on the gray or black market or buying formulas not intended for patient use. “The Secretary of HHS has determined that this limited enforcement-discretion policy will discourage the continued, widespread use of certain research-use-only peptides supplied by foreign manufacturers that pose a safety risk to US patients,” the HHS policy document states. Calling the compounded peptides “a more controlled alternative” to illegal versions of the peptides, the document says that “the Secretary has determined that the policy advances patient choice and freedom while appropriately protecting patient safety.” Peptide sellers online were quick to celebrate the news. Brigham Buhler, who sells peptides in a wellness clinic he runs, cheered the news on X, thanking Kennedy, President Trump, Calley Means, and Joe Rogan. “Medical freedom just took a major step forward!” he wrote. Zachary Shurtleff, a marketing executive for a wellness-focused compounding pharmacy, also celebrated, writing, ” Peptides are BACK like they never shoulda left!” Lee Rosebush, chair of the American Academy of Peptide Medicine, a group formed last year, released a statement thanking Kennedy and the Trump administration for “taking a forward-thinking, evidence-based approach to the regulation of peptides.” Critics have pushed back, noting that there are already reports of harms from peptides. In a statement released Tuesday, before the new rule was released, the Center for Science in the Public Interest laid out several reasons why making unproven peptides more accessible is dangerous. For one, reports of harms will “continue to mount,” CSPI said. And if any of the peptides actually showed promise of efficacy, reputable pharmaceutical companies will have less incentive to follow up with thorough clinical trials if the peptide is already widely available. Further, allowing access to peptides based on marketing demand could open the doors for other unapproved products, creating an incentive for illegal sales. Fred Mills, chief policy officer of Americans for Safe and Effective Medicines (ASEM), said in a statement Wednesday that the new rule “does not resolve problems with untested products, safety risks, questionable ingredients, or misleading claims. In fact, we believe this will worsen what we have already seen—the harms from mass compounding hitting communities hard due to a lack of oversight to rein in bad actors. We need to follow the science, and the reality is that FDA itself has continued to sound the alarm on lackluster data on these peptides. Safety and efficacy aren’t a nice-to-have; they are required.”

Trump admin sues Utah to save Kalshi from the state's total ban on gambling
2026-10-28

Trump admin sues Utah to save Kalshi from the state's total ban on gambling

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
The Trump administration is trying to stop Utah from enforcing its gambling ban against Kalshi, making Utah the 10th state sued by the US government agency that regulates prediction markets. Utah, which prohibits gambling, sued Kalshi in a state court last week. Utah’s lawsuit seeks an order prohibiting Kalshi from offering bets on sports, elections, politics, and other events. “Kalshi runs an online casino in Utah and calls it a prediction market. That violates Utah law,” Utah Attorney General Derek Brown, a Republican, said last week. “Not only do our state laws ban gambling but Utah is one of only two states where gambling is also prohibited under the state constitution. For 130 years, Utah has been clear: gambling has no place here, and we will enforce our laws.” Utah already defeated Kalshi in federal court after the prediction market filed a preemptive lawsuit that sought to prevent the state from enforcing its gambling laws. Now that Utah is moving ahead in its attempt to shut down Kalshi, it must defend against a lawsuit filed by the US Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC). The CFTC “has filed a lawsuit against Utah to block the state’s efforts to shut down CFTC-registered contract markets using state laws,” the commission said yesterday. “The lawsuit follows Utah’s decision to file civil enforcement actions in state court against CFTC-regulated markets and other businesses. Utah’s effort to restrict the functioning of CFTC-registered exchanges obstructs Congress’s decision to federally preempt state law.” If Utah can shut down Kalshi, that could spell trouble for Kalshi’s chief prediction-market rival, Polymarket, and for sports betting sites like DraftKings and FanDuel. DraftKings and FanDuel advertise availability in all US states via a mix of products, including prediction-market platforms in states like Utah. Both are registered with the CFTC. The Utah attorney general’s office declined to comment today on the CFTC lawsuit. The CFTC previously filed lawsuits against Arizona, Connecticut, Illinois, Kentucky, Minnesota, New Mexico, New York, Rhode Island, and Wisconsin. The federal agency also filed briefs supporting Kalshi in appeals court cases. “Utah seeks to shut down prediction markets across a wide variety of markets,” CFTC Chairman Michael Selig said. “Event contracts do not just serve hedging and investment purposes—they also provide the public with information about the outcome of future events. Congress entrusted the CFTC to be the federal regulator with sole responsibility to oversee these markets. As I’ve said before, the CFTC has the expertise and responsibility to defend its exclusive jurisdiction over commodity derivatives, and that’s exactly what we’ll do.” The US Commodity Exchange Act (CEA) gives the CFTC authority to regulate swaps. The key legal questions being grappled with by courts throughout the US are whether event contracts offered by prediction markets qualify as swaps, and whether the CFTC jurisdiction over swaps preempts states from applying their gambling laws. The CFTC is suing Utah in the same US district court that previously ruled in Utah’s favor. Kalshi sued Utah in February in an attempt to stop the state from bringing an enforcement action against the prediction market. In August, US District Judge Robert Shelby granted Utah’s motion for summary judgment and denied Kalshi’s motion for a preliminary injunction. Shelby wrote, “[T]he court concludes the federal law relied upon by Kalshi does not preempt Utah’s ability to enforce its anti-gambling laws.” Shelby determined the text of US law makes it clear that state regulation is not forbidden. US law states that CFTC jurisdiction does not “supersede or limit the jurisdiction conferred on courts of the United States or any State,” and “explicitly provides for State regulation of its gaming laws in all but a few specified cases,” he wrote. “Congress is aware that some States permit gambling while others do not, and the CEA explicitly provides for State jurisdiction,” Shelby wrote. Kalshi is now appealing its loss to the US Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit but was unable to obtain an injunction preventing enforcement of the state gambling ban while that litigation continues. On September 8, Tenth Circuit judges denied Kalshi’s emergency motion for an injunction pending appeal. Utah filed its lawsuit seeking a shutdown of Kalshi about six weeks later. The CFTC lawsuit against Utah makes arguments similar to the ones already rejected by the district court, although some other courts have ruled otherwise. “Utah is not the first State that has attempted to invade the Commission’s exclusive jurisdiction over swaps,” the CFTC lawsuit said. “Already, several federal courts—including the Third Circuit—have swiftly responded by issuing or affirming temporary restraining orders and preliminary injunctions barring the States from enforcing their gambling laws against CFTC-regulated exchanges that offer sports-related or other event contracts.” The Third Circuit appeals court ruled that New Jersey cannot regulate sports bets on prediction markets. But Kalshi lost similar cases in the Sixth Circuit and Ninth Circuit in rulings that went in favor of Ohio, Tennessee, and Nevada. Because circuit appeals courts are divided on the issue, it is likely that the Supreme Court will take up the case and determine whether states can apply their gambling laws to prediction markets. New Jersey petitioned the Supreme Court to resolve the circuit split, and 39 other states and the District of Columbia filed a brief supporting New Jersey’s petition.

The last, best hope for space tourism just rolled onto a runway in Arizona
2026-10-28

The last, best hope for space tourism just rolled onto a runway in Arizona

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
The private space tourism era looked so very promising five years ago. Blue Origin and Virgin Galactic had begun flying customers—real people with zero space experience—on suborbital missions. And at about the same time, private citizens were going to orbit for days at a time aboard SpaceX’s Crew Dragon. But that was then. Consider the state of play today: So we are now 10 months, and counting, without a space tourism mission in the United States. At the time of Unity‘s final flight, Virgin Galactic said it was moving on to the second generation of its suborbital spacecraft. It planned to bring two of these Delta-class ships into service by the year 2026. Of course, it is now 2026, and there are no Delta-class ships in service. But apparently we are getting closer to that goal. On Wednesday, the first Delta-class ship received a name: Apeiron. According to Virgin Galactic’s news release, the name is drawn from the ancient Greek concept of the boundless, which is an interesting choice for a vehicle that will forever be bound by Earth’s gravity. It was chosen through a public vote. “We’re proud that the lead ship in Virgin Galactic’s Spaceship fleet will carry a name chosen by the people who share our excitement for what comes next,” said Michael Colglazier, chief executive officer of Virgin Galactic. But more significantly, the company said Apeiron would be moved from its Spaceship Factory in Mesa, Arizona, to Spaceport America in New Mexico later on Wednesday. Apeiron won’t be making a free flight anytime soon. After being carried to New Mexico by the VMS Eve, aircraft engineers and technicians there will complete work on the vehicle’s avionics equipment and flight software. Following additional ground tests, Apeiron will be mated again to Eve for captive carry tests, followed by a drop test and ultimately a powered test flight above 80 km. Presently, the company is saying it now expects Apeiron’s first commercial spaceflight to take place “early” in the second quarter of 2027. As with every other projected date in aerospace, this should be taken with a grain of salt. But it seems plausible that Apeiron may begin flying paying customers on suborbital space tourism missions over the next 12 to 18 months. There is considerable skepticism about the commercial viability of Virgin Galactic’s space tourism ambitions. Success will require its vehicles to fly nearly flawlessly and with unprecedented durability to bring hundreds of people to space each year briefly. As this reality has dawned on investors, the markets have not been kind to the company in recent years. In 2021, at the height of space tourism euphoria, the company’s stock briefly reached a stratospheric valuation of $1,118 a share. More recently it has been trading in the vicinity $2.50 a share. Whatever you might think of Virgin Galactic, however, it seems clear that any hopes of expanding the aperture of space tourism in the next decade will be flying beneath its wings.
Ars Technica - All content
Touchscreen MacBook Pro hands-on: OLED looks great on a Mac
2026-10-28

Touchscreen MacBook Pro hands-on: OLED looks great on a Mac

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
LOS ANGELES—For about five minutes on an extremely crowded showroom floor, I got to take pictures of the new MacBook Pro and test out some of its new features. First impressions: It’s not as much of a departure from the previous design as I expected, but the outstanding OLED display steals the show regardless. The first thing I noticed was that it’s lighter. My daily driver is a 14-inch MacBook Pro (M1 Max), and it’s a bit weighty for a 14-incher. Picking up the new model, I could immediately tell it was lighter. It’s not like it’s in a different general weight class, but it’s a noticeable difference. The next thing I noticed was the Dynamic Island. Given the larger screen real estate of a laptop, it’s much less dominant on the screen than it is on an iPhone. I do worry about what will happen with fullscreen games, which I wasn’t able to test today. The notch MacBook Pro has an option to maximize apps or games to just below the notch, so it doesn’t cut into the image. I’m curious to test in the full review whether that remains the case with this little cutout. And lastly, the Apple logo on the back glows again! Some find this tacky, some find it essential. I just think it’s a little bit nostalgic. Of course, you might make the case that the most notable thing about this laptop is not its weight or the Dynamic Island. For years, people have debated whether Apple should introduce a touchscreen laptop. Steve Jobs was famously against it. A few years ago—there were reports that under Tim Cook and now John Ternus—Apple decided to move ahead with the idea. I played with it a bit, and it works just about exactly as you expect for a laptop touchscreen. A few of the things I did with the touchscreen during my brief time with the laptop: All of that is nice to have, I guess, but there’s not much you can do with it that you couldn’t already do with trackpad gestures, at least at the OS level; some creative apps, like finger painting on the screen, seemed to offer a bit more. When the credible reports emerged that the MacBook Pro would get a touchscreen, I imagined a convertible with Apple Pencil support—something you could use as a large iPad or drawing tablet, essentially. That’s not what this is; it only takes the usual laptop shape, and I was told it does not support the Apple Pencil. It’s just a MacBook Pro, in its normal form factor, but you can use your fingers on the screen to do things like drag files around, swipe between desktop spaces, or pinch-to-zoom on maps or images. But while it’s not about the touchscreen capability for me, I was impressed by the OLED screen itself—and I’m quite the display snob, if I’m being honest. The peak brightness, absolute blacks, well-balanced color, and dynamic range were genuinely remarkable. It’s an enormous upgrade over the MiniLED screen in the previous MacBook Pro. It took far too long for OLED to come to the MacBook Pro, but unsurprisingly, Apple spared no expense and cut no immediately obvious corners on quality. I was also happy to see that the cutout near the top of the screen that has replaced the camera notch is what Apple calls a Dynamic Island; it can resize to show live activities and other information on the fly. That’s one of my favorite features on the iPhone, as I’ve been impressed with Apple’s ability to turn something that should be an albatross into an appealing feature. Based on my time with the device and today’s announcement, the screen seems to be the main draw (though it’s nice that it noticeably weighs less, too), but the price is the real barrier. A 16-inch model with an M5 Max, 64 GB of RAM, and 2 TB of storage is currently priced at $5,799. Sure, that’s a high-end spec, but that’s some serious cash. Much of that is due to the storage and memory; upgrading from 64GB to 128GB adds a whopping $1,600 to that. Sure, most people don’t need anywhere near that much RAM—but some might for future-proofing for local AI workflows. I’m looking forward to spending more time with the new MacBook Pro, but for right now, it seems to follow in the lineup’s existing tradition: impressively high-end components like an outstanding OLED panel, but a very steep price to match. No surprises here! Though after well over a decade of speculation and back-and-forth, you’d be forgiven for being surprised that the touchscreen thing actually finally happened.

‘I get more meetings because of the brand’: BlackBerry’s CEO on keeping a famous name
2026-10-29

‘I get more meetings because of the brand’: BlackBerry’s CEO on keeping a famous name

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Good morning. Interbrand came out with its 2026 Best Global Brands list yesterday. Apple occupies the top spot. It first hit No. 1 in 2013, the year smartphone pioneer BlackBerry fell off the list. I wrote a lot of stories about BlackBerry over the years, from its stunning rise to its precipitous fall. There have been books, case studies, even a “comedy” about co-founders Jim Balsillie and Mike Lazaridis that you can now stream on Netflix. And there’s also a serious company that continues to proudly boast the name BlackBerry, even though it no longer makes BlackBerry handsets and went by the name Research In Motion when it did. So when I met with BlackBerry CEO John Giamatteo recently, the first question I asked was why he stays attached to such a notorious name. After all, the company now makes its money—more than $600 million and growing this year, compared with close to $20 billion at its height—from a business called BlackBerry Secure Communications and the very popular QNX software that powers more than 275 million smart vehicles and is expanding into robotaxis, medical devices, and robots. The main reason is that having a recognized brand makes the company stand out in the crowd. “I get more meetings because of the brand,” he told me. “If I’m just ‘John from QNX,’ it’s like, ‘QNX what?’ I love the fact that it gets you in the door almost across the board.” The cost? “I have to spend the first 10 minutes of every conversation, whether it’s an investor, whether it’s a media, whether it’s a customer, recalibrating people on who we are and what we’re doing,” he said. “But more times than not, people are like, ‘Oh, great, I like them. Glad to hear you guys are doing so well.’” That said, a little attention can go a long way. “Being a public company, you have to have a proctology exam every quarterly result,” says Giamatteo, who joined the company as president in 2021, becoming CEO in 2023. “But I net out that it’s probably better to be public.” To him, the BlackBerry brand is less a cautionary tale about missing market shifts than a testament to resilience and reinvention. Balsillie and Lazaridis also deserve credit for having the foresight to buy QNX software for $200 million back in 2010. Says Giamatteo: “BlackBerry will endure.” Certainly, it’s got a sticky name.Contact CEO Daily via Diane Brady at diane.brady@fortune.com Alphabet shares fall despite Google Cloud revenue doubling Alphabet shares dropped more than 11% after hours as slower search growth and heavy AI spending overshadowed Google Cloud’s revenue, which more than doubled to $30.7 billion. Shares were up more than 4% in initial after-hours trading. Capital spending rose to $45.2 billion as Microsoft continued building capacity to meet demand for AI services. In updated guidelines, the company encouraged engineers to explain when AI doesn’t help their work. S&P 500 futures are down 0.19% this morning. The last session closed up 0.28%. ‘The assertion that the AI is an autonomous being is insane’: Cyber expert says companies should face criminal charges when their AI hacks
2026-10-29

‘The assertion that the AI is an autonomous being is insane’: Cyber expert says companies should face criminal charges when their AI hacks

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Cybersecurity experts are calling this month “Black October,” and unfortunately, the name has nothing to do with Halloween. Over the past several weeks, AI developers, government agencies, and private companies have disclosed a wave of cybersecurity incidents. One of the most significant: a pair of decade-old flaws in PHP, the programming language behind an estimated 70% of websites, which researchers uncovered with the help of AI models. Some of this month’s incidents involved hackers using AI tools to hammer an organization’s digital defenses at machine speed; others resulted from plain human error. But many cybersecurity experts agree that this is the beginning of a new era, one in which AI can be used both to defend people’s cybersecurity and to attack it. “AI is making the old vulnerabilities cheaper to discover and exploit, but on the other side, the defense side… that’s not sped up,” Junfeng Yang, a computer science professor at Columbia University, told Fortune. “That is the asymmetry right now.” That asymmetry, Yang explained, is what leaves internet users and organizations vulnerable. In many of these incidents, the remedy is a patch, a software update engineered to close the vulnerability. But it takes time to properly test a patch and make sure everyone affected can deploy it. That struggle was on display Monday, when the U.S. Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency added last week’s PHP vulnerabilities, known collectively as “php2shell,” to its catalog of flaws being actively exploited by attackers and ordered civilian federal agencies to fix affected systems by Oct. 29. Five days earlier, CISA had said it had not yet seen exploitation. Still, Yang and other cybersecurity experts believe the asymmetry could be temporary. “Maybe in the future, AI can help us with the patching process by updating automatically whenever a high-security vulnerability is discovered,” Yang said. “Like your AI, running together with your website, could automatically apply these patches, without interrupting normal operations.” Some incidents involved AI models themselves taking actions nobody authorized after being given access to the live internet. Over the weekend, Anthropic confirmed that a Claude model used internally for a statistics project accessed aggregate vote-count data from a Georgia state data hub without being asked. Anthropic initially told Axios the model had gotten around a paywall, but Georgia’s secretary of state’s office said Monday that Anthropic had confirmed the model had not bypassed a paywall and had used the state’s public Data Hub. Both say the model never accessed the state’s voter registration system or individual voter records. In some cases, AI agents targeted real people. During cyber testing by the UK AI Security Institute in July, a Claude Mythos 5 agent that couldn’t solve a test challenge within the rules tried to slip malicious code into an unrelated open-source GitHub project, then used fake accounts to pressure the project’s human maintainers into accepting it. The maintainers refused, and AISI said the incident caused no real-world harm; Anthropic called it a serious alignment failure. In another peculiar case, an OpenAI research agent took cryptocurrency from a wallet it didn’t own to pay the fees needed to finish a coding task, though the tokens were on an Ethereum test network and had little real-world value. Joseph Steinberg, a cybersecurity expert and consultant, believes many incidents involving AI models on the internet are easily avoidable. He says all AI testing should happen on air-gapped servers, which are not connected to the internet, to keep models from causing harm outside the test. AISI has since said it will run such tests without internet access until it has stronger controls in place. “I think that if an AI goes out and hacks, the parties that own it should be held responsible criminally,” Steinberg said. “The assertion that the AI is an autonomous being is insane.” The profits, he argued, go to the AI’s owners, not the AI. “It’s their entity.” In several of this month’s incidents, AI was a weapon in human hands. In one major case, from late September to early October, a hacker used ARTEX, an agentic penetration-testing tool developed in China, to steal data from South Korean financial institutions, according to CrowdStrike. At one bank, the attacker breached a loan progress inquiry service used by financial brokers; at another, an employee mobile work-support system. ARTEX’s developer took the previously open-source tool private on Oct. 8 after linking it to the attacks. (Fortune has reported on the growing wave of AI-powered cyberattacks.) The same tool surfaced again last week, when Microsoft said a China-aligned hacking group it tracks as Storm-0940 used ARTEX after compromising ConnectWise’s Cloud Backup service. Using stolen credentials, the group had ARTEX attempt to sign in to more than 2,250 Microsoft 365 customer environments, known as tenants, at a rate of more than 2,900 attempts per minute, a pace no human team could match. Most of those attempts failed, but Microsoft said the attackers did gain privileged access at some organizations, in multiple cases less than three hours after first targeting them. Yang said it is important to take the Black October incidents seriously, but he pointed out that some of the most impactful ones had nothing to do with AI. For example, a major flaw disclosed earlier this month at eMudhra, an Indian certificate authority, came down to old-fashioned human error. Certificate authorities issue the digital certificates that let browsers verify a website is what it claims to be. On Oct. 17, a researcher reported that the private keys behind eight eMudhra intermediate certificates, generated in April 2025, were so weak they could be recovered almost instantly. One was simply the number 4. Anyone who found the keys could have forged trusted certificates and impersonated legitimate websites, from banks to government portals. Browser makers blocked the certificates, and eMudhra revoked them, saying it found no evidence they had been misused. The company has since said it will exit the global SSL/TLS certificate business. Steinberg said new technology has historically created new problems, but experts eventually find ways to solve them. Further down the road, he thinks, Black October might not be seen as significant. “It sounds scary, but for those of us who’ve been around in the field for a few decades… this is not something new,” Steinberg said. “It fits the pattern. AI is something new. It’s qualitatively different from general information systems. But we’ve had other qualitative changes that have also happened, and we’ve managed.” © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Spirit Halloween’s CEO says Trump’s tariffs were ‘going to put us out of business’—they even set up a war room. Then it had its best year ever
2026-10-29

Spirit Halloween’s CEO says Trump’s tariffs were ‘going to put us out of business’—they even set up a war room. Then it had its best year ever

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Standing in the White House Rose Garden, President Donald Trump declared April 2, 2025, would be now known as “Liberation Day.” That day, he announced tariffs on imports from nearly every country on earth, and promised “jobs and factories will come roaring back” to the U.S. Within days, after China retaliated, the U.S. tariff on Chinese goods rose to 145%. The increase came just as Spirit Halloween’s goods began shipping from Asia. “This was going to put us out of business,” Steven Silverstein, CEO of Spirit’s parent company, Spencer Spirit Holdings, told Fortune. Silverstein said the tariffs were worse for Spirit than COVID. In March 2020, every Spencer’s store closed just as Spirit was choosing locations and signing leases for the fall. The company decided to go ahead with Halloween anyway and opened all of its stores that September. In spite of all that went down that year, he said the business has more than doubled since 2019. But the tariffs were harder because they hit in the middle of shipping season. “They were implemented immediately without giving business the opportunity to really react in any kind of logical way,” he said. “We were being ripped around like a yo-yo.” About 75% to 80% of Spirit’s merchandise is designed and sourced by the company, mostly in Asia, and it moves on a tight schedule through distribution centers and 63 third-party logistics sites before reaching more than 1,550 stores, a process Silverstein says runs all year. “It skyrocketed right in the middle of our shipping season,” he said. “Every day is critical to us.” Spirit had to stop the ships. A group of executives set up what Silverstein called a war room. “You start burning the furniture,” he said. “You have to preserve your cash.” The company suspended all capital spending and cut costs across its teams. Silverstein called a meeting of the whole company. “I said, look, this is going to be really rough seas, but this is what we’ve got to do to get from here to there,” he said. On May 12, the U.S. and China agreed to cut the tariff to 30%. Spirit restarted shipping, but its goods were six weeks behind. Silverstein restored everything the company had cut. Spirit didn’t raise prices to cover the tariffs. “I thought it would be irresponsible, if nothing else,” he said. “All we do is Halloween. Without knowing what the outcome would be, that could be really dangerous. And so we ate the tariffs.” To make up for lost time, Spirit hired even more workers, not fewer. Stores opened about two weeks to two and a half weeks late—but even then, Silverstein said it was the company’s best year ever. Silverstein said critics see a chain that rents empty stores for a few weeks as opportunists. “They’re carpetbaggers,” he said, describing that view. “They just come in, they occupy and then they go away, and they take their circus on the road. We don’t think of ourselves that way at all.” In 2006, three years after he became CEO, Spirit threw a Halloween party for children in a hospital, with costumes, music, and art supplies. It started with eight hospitals in New Jersey, where the company is still based today. “These kids lit up,” he said. “They got into their Spider-Man costume, and they were Spider-Man.” The company later added fundraising at the register so shoppers can round up their purchase or add a fixed donation, and all of the money goes to the child life department at a local hospital. That pays for staff, playrooms and therapy beyond medical care. So far, Spirit of Children has raised more than $164 million since 2006, at an average of about $1.25 per donation, Silverstein said. “This is a story of microphilanthropy,” he said. “You take millions and millions of people, and you give a little amount each.” Silverstein said the program is one reason seasonal workers return. “When I speak to associates, why are you coming back to Spirit? Because I love Spirit of Children so much,” he said. This year, the program’s 20th anniversary, Spirit aims to raise $20 million. “Every year it grows,” Silverstein said. “I think we’re going to get there.” Catherina covers markets, the economy, energy, tech, and AI. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. Anthropic spent $2.75 million guarding Dario Amodei last year—more than 4 times his salary, but a fraction of Mark Zuckerberg’s security bill
2026-10-29

Anthropic spent $2.75 million guarding Dario Amodei last year—more than 4 times his salary, but a fraction of Mark Zuckerberg’s security bill

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Anthropic spent $2.75 million protecting cofounder and CEO Dario Amodei in 2025—more than four times his $654,167 base salary, according to the AI company’s newly public IPO filing. The security bill accounted for more than 15% of Amodei’s $17.96 million in total reported compensation for 2025, which also included stock and option awards. And Amodei wasn’t the only executive Anthropic paid to protect. The company spent another $1.29 million on personal protection, home security, and transportation for his sister, president and cofounder Daniela Amodei, plus $97,055 on private-aircraft travel for Dario that it said was required under his security program. Anthropic has maintained round-the-clock security since 2024, The Wall Street Journal reported in July. According to the S-1, Anthropic’s board determined in 2025 that enhanced protection for the Amodeis and their families was necessary, citing security studies, increased media attention, and what it called “bona fide business-related, heightened security concerns.” The measures included personal protection, home surveillance, and private cars with drivers. Anthropic did not disclose how much it spent on executive security in 2024, making it impossible to determine whether costs rose sharply in 2025. The filing acknowledges threats to employee safety but does not specify when they occurred. “Heightened public attention on AI has increased physical and informational security risks,” the company wrote, “and we have experienced threats to employee safety and attempts to obtain confidential information through physical or digital means.” This year, several of those threats became public. In April, days after an attack on OpenAI CEO Sam Altman’s home, a man slipped into the lobby of Anthropic’s San Francisco office behind a badge-swiping employee and told a security guard that a top executive was “going to be killed,” according to the Journal. That same month, a man who had applied for a job at Anthropic under a false identity allegedly posted online threats to harm employees’ children, which he framed as punishment for the company stealing his work. Police classified it as a terroristic threat but made no arrest. In June, a customer demanding a refund wrote that he would come to Anthropic’s office with his pistol. In August, Amodei himself was named in an alleged threat. A Claude user claimed in a chat to have purchased an AR-15 rifle and to have the CEO “in his sights,” according to a police report obtained by The San Francisco Standard. Anthropic reported the chat to police and banned the account. The man later told the Standard he had been joking and was embarrassed. Digital threats targeting AI executives and data centers surged sevenfold from late February to May 2026, according to security intelligence firm Liferaft. “What has surprised me is how bad it’s gotten over such a short period of time,” its CEO, Jonathan Graff, told the Journal. The threats have also moved offline. In April, a Texas man was charged after allegedly throwing a Molotov cocktail at Altman’s San Francisco home, then going to OpenAI’s headquarters, where he threatened to burn down the building. Federal prosecutors said he was carrying an anti-AI document that advocated killing AI executives and investors and listed names and addresses. He has pleaded not guilty. Anthropic said in its IPO filing that the security measures it provides senior executives are comparable to those at other high-profile technology companies. By that measure, Amodei’s protection is far from the priciest in tech. Meta remains in a league of its own. The company disclosed approximately $8.5 million in direct security costs for CEO Mark Zuckerberg in 2025, plus a $14 million annual allowance for additional protection for him and his family—about $22.5 million in total, or $25.1 million including private-aircraft costs. That’s more than eight times what Anthropic spent on Amodei. Last year, Fortune reported that Meta spent more guarding Zuckerberg than Apple, Nvidia, Microsoft, Amazon, and Alphabet did for their own CEOs—combined. Here’s how disclosed CEO security spending compares: Compared with the multimillion-dollar security bills of other high-profile tech executives, Anthropic’s spending may not stand out. But against the broader corporate world, the gap is stark. ISS-Corporate, a corporate governance research firm, told Fortune that a recent study shows the median reported CEO security cost was $93,818 among S&P 500 companies and $401,336 among software and services companies. Amodei’s $2.75 million security bill was nearly seven times the latter figure. Valeriano Saucedo, an associate director at ISS-Corporate, said the better comparison may have less to do with the company itself and more to do with the person running it: How high-profile is the CEO? AI promises to transform the world. But even some of the people building it warn that it could destroy humanity. In September, Anthropic alignment science lead Evan Hubinger said he personally believed there was a greater than 10% chance AI could kill all humans within the next decade. Anthropic’s own IPO filing warns that advanced AI systems could pose “catastrophic or existential risks to humanity” without adequate safeguards. As AI companies become the focus of heated public debate, their CEOs are also becoming the faces of the technology. Meta has long warned that public anger toward a company can become personal. In its proxy filings, the Facebook parent has described Zuckerberg as “synonymous with Meta,” saying negative sentiment toward the company is often transferred to him. The 2024 killing of UnitedHealthcare CEO Brian Thompson showed how corporate leaders can become targets amid broader resentment toward their industries, and prompted many companies to reexamine how they protect their top executives. Executive security benefits are becoming more common. According to ISS-Corporate, 28.3% of S&P 500 companies reported providing security benefits to their CEOs in 2025, more than double the 13.4% in 2021. The technology sector has seen a particularly sharp rise: A separate study of the 500 largest U.S. public companies by executive data firm Equilar found that tech saw the biggest increase in the prevalence of executive security benefits, up 73.5% between 2020 and 2024. Anthropic’s security program has since expanded beyond its founding siblings. In 2026, the company began providing security services to CFO Krishna Rao and commissioned an independent assessment of the risks he faces. It has not disclosed how much that protection costs. ISS-Corporate said boards should consider whether security expenses address legitimate business risks, whether the costs are reasonable, and whether the arrangements are regularly reassessed. Anthropic said in its filing that its board regularly reviews the security measures provided to the Amodeis and their costs. The firm added that private-aircraft use can be justified as part of an executive security program, but boards should also consider whether less costly alternatives would be appropriate. “The dollar amount alone does not determine whether an expense is appropriate,” Saucedo said. Meet the Gen Zers fighting back against AI data centers—70% of young adults don’t want one in their town
2026-10-29

Meet the Gen Zers fighting back against AI data centers—70% of young adults don’t want one in their town

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Elijah Lee has spent years listening to Virginia lawmakers talk about data centers. At 18, he’s done listening. Lee is high school chapters coordinator for Voters of Tomorrow, a Gen Z–led political advocacy group. He lives in the state often called the data center capital of the world, since Virginia has more data centers than anywhere else on the planet. Now he’s helping young people across the country push back against them. “It’s difficult to break the data center issue into one piece, but for young people, we know what data centers mean long-term,” Lee said. He has plenty of company. A University of Pennsylvania Annenberg Public Policy Center survey conducted this summer found that 70% of adults under 30 oppose new data centers in their area. That compares with 61% of all adults and 57% of those 65 and older. The researchers called it a reversal of the usual pattern, in which younger Americans are quicker to embrace new technology. In Pennsylvania, the gap is wider still: 83% of likely voters ages 18 to 29 opposed data center development, according to a poll reported by The Philadelphia Inquirer. Fortune has reported that Gen Z has become an organizing force behind the national backlash, and that opposition has spread well beyond the small share of Americans who actually live near a data center. Voters of Tomorrow chapters are meeting with local officials, hosting voter registration drives on campuses and holding information sessions at high schools. When they ask young people what concerns them, Lee said, data centers come up again and again. In Spartanburg, S.C., members of the group’s high school chapter spoke up about a proposed data center. Amid heavy public turnout, Spartanburg County Council delayed a final vote on a one-year moratorium in August. It then passed the pause 5-1 in September, though the ban doesn’t cover a data center already under construction there. Scenes like that are playing out nationwide. On Monday night, a vote on a proposed Google data center in Hermantown, Minn., was postponed after two hours of protests. With the Nov. 3 midterms less than a week away, campaigns have spent $45 million this year on ads mentioning data centers, most of them in opposition. For Lee, the fight is about money first. He called out the old promise that if young people went to college, they would land a good job and eventually afford a house. “This isn’t true for young people anymore, and data centers are making that much worse,” he said. Data centers are far from the only reason. Fortune has reported that the facilities once helped hold power costs down before the current buildout threatened that trend. Still, their enormous appetite for power strains the grid. A 2025 Bloomberg News analysis found that in some areas near significant data center activity, wholesale electricity costs were as much as 267% higher for a single month than they were five years earlier. Wholesale prices are only one part of a household bill, typically 30% to 50%, according to PolitiFact, so residential bills haven’t risen nearly that much. But experts told NPR that how much households pay varies by utility and state, and that residential customers will ultimately cover a share of the AI buildout. Economists have warned that lower-income households will feel it most. Lee also worries that the industry’s demand for large tracts of land will make it harder for his generation to buy property. “The idea of now having to grow up and compete in terms of water prices and energy prices with a tech company is not something I can say I’m excited about,” he said. Not all of the concerns are about money. Many data centers use large amounts of water to cool their servers. U.S. data centers directly used about 17 billion gallons of water in 2023, and that figure could reach nearly 73 billion gallons by 2028, according to Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory estimates. In Spartanburg County, the developer of one project recently raised its projected water use from 459,000 gallons a day to as much as 1.6 million. “Think about what having a million data centers does to the energy grid in 30 years,” Lee said. “Think about what it will do to our water and our ability to access clean water.” Residents near some facilities also complain of constant noise from cooling equipment and backup generators. Civil rights groups have warned that data centers are often built in communities already burdened by pollution and climate risk. “Young people know that data centers are going to destroy our communities, flat out,” Lee said. The young people leading the backlash aren’t anti-technology. In Deloitte’s 2025 Gen Z and Millennial Survey, more than half of respondents said they already use generative AI in their day-to-day work. Nearly three-quarters of Gen Z said they expect it to change how they work within a year. The Annenberg researchers found that how often someone uses AI didn’t predict whether they opposed a local data center. Lee doesn’t see a contradiction. “We are not willing to take advantage of the tool that is AI so long as it compromises the livelihood of our fellow citizens,” he said. He wants regulations that make tech companies build in ways communities can live with. Several politicians who once wooed the industry have reversed course. Pennsylvania Gov. Josh Shapiro championed a $20 billion Amazon data center investment last year. In August, he signed an executive order blocking new projects unless they meet stricter limits on electricity and water use, and removing them from the state’s fast-track permitting program. Shapiro faces Republican state Treasurer Stacy Garrity next week, and she has accused him of flip-flopping. Texas Gov. Greg Abbott once called his state “the epicenter of AI development.” He has since paused environmental permits and grid connections for new data centers. On ABC’s This Week, he said developers had “dug their own grave” by failing to work with state and local governments. In July, New York Gov. Kathy Hochul signed the nation’s first statewide data center moratorium. In September, California Gov. Gavin Newsom, who is term-limited, signed seven bills aimed at keeping data centers from passing their energy costs on to other ratepayers. Even President Donald Trump, a champion of the buildout, wrote on Truth Social last week that large new data centers must generate their own electricity. He also said developers should pay host communities a “dividend.” Whether the anger turns into votes is another question. In a recent Targoz poll, young voters ranked the cost of living and health care as their top issues. Electricity bills and data centers accounted for just 6% combined. Lee argues those are the same fight. “Young people cannot afford to live this way,” he said. “Building a future where we can actually live means backing down on data centers.” © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. Women astronauts outnumber men in NASA’s new class—they’re now halfway through training and set to earn around $150K with no overtime or hazard pay
2026-10-29

Women astronauts outnumber men in NASA’s new class—they’re now halfway through training and set to earn around $150K with no overtime or hazard pay

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Women astronauts outnumber men in NASA’s new class—they’re now half way through training, and set to earn around $150K with no overtime or hazard pay If billionaires like Elon Musk, Jeff Bezos and Sam Altman are right, your next job could be on the Moon. And the people lined up to get there first are already in training. NASA’s newest astronaut class, announced last year, is now just past the halfway point of its two-year training—and it’s doing the rounds on social media again for making history. For the first time, women outnumber men: six of the 10 candidates, chosen from more than 8,000 applicants. They’re currently learning “basic astronaut skills,” according to NASA’s website, like spacewalking, operating the space station, flying jet planes and controlling a robotic arm. Once qualified, they could be assigned to future missions in low Earth orbit and to the Moon, with some potentially playing a role in NASA’s longer-term plans for Mars. But there’s a catch: they’ll earn on par with a mid-career desk job or a skilled trade like electricians—and they won’t get paid overtime or hazard pay. The out-of-this-world job comes with a very down-to-earth paycheck. Candidates earn a government salary during training, and if they qualify, NASA’s website states the pay is around $152,000, as of 2024. NASA didn’t respond to Fortune’s request for comment on their updated pay. In comparison, psychotherapists, project managers and air traffic controllers can earn similar wages—without the added risk of leaving planet Earth. “When NASA astronauts are aboard the International Space Station, they receive regular 40-hour workweek salaries,” NASA previously told Fortune. “While in space, NASA astronauts are on official travel orders as federal employees, so their transportation, lodging, and meals are provided.” Not that they can clock off—the office is orbiting Earth. Astronauts Suni Williams and Butch Wilmore found that out the hard way. Their eight-day trip stretched to 286 days after problems with Boeing’s Starliner spacecraft, and they received just $5 a day in incidentals, about $1,430 each in extra pay. First, the odds. With an acceptance rate of about 0.125%, it’s harder to get into NASA’s astronaut class than Harvard, which accepts around 4%. The qualifications themselves are relatively straightforward. Applicants must: Military experience isn’t required, and neither is flying experience. There’s no age limit, with the average candidate being 34. But applicants must be free of any medical conditions that could impair space flight—and NASA physicians have the final say, with no waivers for medical requirements. And if you want a taste of what it takes to become an astronaut, look no further than Sara Sabry. She doesn’t work for NASA, but she became the first Egyptian, and the first Arab and African woman, to fly to space on Jeff Bezos’s Blue Origin in 2022, beating more than 7,000 other applicants. To get there, she woke at 4:30 a.m. to squeeze in training and research before her full-time job as CTO of a Berlin-based startup. “Back then it was, it was really, really, it was really tough,” she told Fortune. “You would wake up at night, and then you would go back at night, so you barely see the daylight ever.” “I see a lot of young people now; they’re wanting to take the easy route without working so hard. But the truth is, you have to make sacrifices. You have to put yourself through a lot of discomfort,” Sabry added. “Of course, it’s not easy to wake up at 4:30 a.m. every morning and be completely isolated from the world, right? But it goes to show that you can really transform your life—and you have so much control over your life.” Orianna Rosa Royle is the Success associate editor at Fortune, overseeing careers, leadership, and company culture coverage. She was previously the senior reporter at Management Today, Britain's longest-running publication for CEOs. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Dropbox cutting 200 global roles
2026-10-29

Dropbox cutting 200 global roles

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
The company also laid off around 500 people two years ago. File storage platform Dropbox is laying off around 200 people, or 9pc of its global workforce, across its engineering, product, design and administrative functions. SiliconRepublic.com has contacted Dropbox to ask if Ireland-based employees will be impacted. In a memo to staff yesterday (28 October), co-CEO Ashraf Alkarmi said the company was “making difficult choices about where we invest and how we organise Dropbox to achieve the product and growth priorities that will matter most for our long-term strategy”. “Our business is profitable and we’ve built real momentum this year. Our two-year strategy sets a clear direction for growth,” he said. “Delivering on that strategy requires concentrating our resources on the priorities that will have the greatest potential for customer impact and growth.” He added: “In order to be successful, we can’t take the same work and spread it across fewer people. We’ll streamline teams and concentrate support and resources against product areas that have the highest impact on our customers and Dropbox as a business.” In a filing with the US Securities and Exchange Commission yesterday, Dropbox noted the layoffs would cost it “approximately $27m to $33m”. In his staff memo, Alkarmi said that impacted staff members would receive 16 weeks’ pay plus one additional week for each completed year at Dropbox, while country-specific practices and legal requirements would also be followed. Other aspects of compensation are also included in Dropbox’s offering to affected staff. The SEC filing noted that that the company “expects its third quarter 2026 financial results to be in line with or above its guidance ranges for revenue, constant currency revenue and non-GAAP operating margin”. Alkarmi’s memo also noted that the company’s CTO Ali Dasdan would be leaving in November. On this, the SEC filing said: “Mr Dasdan will receive a pro rata bonus equal to $515,000 as well as a lump sum cash payment equal to approximately $1.3m, as well as certain additional benefits consistent with those received by other departing employees.” Two years ago, Dropbox laid off more than 500 employees, while in April 2023, it cut a similar number of roles. Rise of AI not deterring parents from backing their child’s STEM interests
2026-10-29

Rise of AI not deterring parents from backing their child’s STEM interests

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
The research found that a range of factors can impact why a parent may support a child’s STEM ambitions, including their own career and level of AI use. A report published today (29 October) by STEM South West has found that, despite the concerns around artificial intelligence, eight out of 10 (81pc) parents are still encouraging their school-aged children to pursue careers in science, technology, engineering and maths (STEM). STEM South West is an industry-led initiative that brings together Irish tech, pharmaceutical, science and engineering companies, alongside SMEs and stakeholders, to identify and address problems facing the STEM sector in the south-west. The report gathered information from 1,000 adults nationwide and discovered that, of those who contributed to the research, almost one-quarter are likely to encourage a child to engage with STEM even more than they would have prior to the rise of AI. Comparatively, only 8pc said they are less likely to and only 12pc said they wouldn’t encourage a child into STEM at all. According to the report, people’s own use of AI had some bearing on how likely they would be to support their child’s foray into the AI space. Life-changing career opportunities for you Join a culture that offers a world of possibilities Among frequent or daily AI users, 36pc said they would encourage a child towards STEM more than before, compared to 18pc of non-AI users. In contrast, 25pc of non-AI users said they wouldn’t encourage a child towards STEM, compared to 7pc of frequent or daily users. ICT professionals were also found to be particularly supportive, as 41pc said they would encourage a child towards STEM more than before, while 94pc said they would encourage a child at least as much as they would have before recent advances in AI. Commenting on the research, Aine McCarthy, the chair of STEM South West said: “It’s really encouraging that people continue to believe strongly in the opportunities a career in STEM can offer young people. There has understandably been a lot of discussion about what AI could mean for future jobs, but these findings show that confidence in STEM remains strong. “What is particularly interesting is the difference we see among people who use AI regularly, where greater familiarity with the technology is associated with a more positive outlook on STEM careers. That reinforces the importance of giving young people opportunities not simply to use AI, but to understand it and develop the skills to work alongside it.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Ireland’s Vectigo unveils new road safety platform in the US

Ireland’s Vectigo unveils new road safety platform in the US

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Image: © David S. Swierczek/Stock.adobe.com Vectigo’s platform is ‘designed to reduce crashes and save lives for everyday drivers first’, company chair Brendan Lundy said. Irish start-up Vectigo’s US division is launching a new safety platform for autonomous vehicles that can be embedded directly onto roadways. Vectigo, headquartered out of Dublin, was founded in 2024 by Sinéad O’Sullivan and Brendan Lundy. The business is heading towards a $2.2m raise this year after securing $1.9m in total funds since its founding. Vectigo’s technology offers solar-powered and grid-independent on-road sensors designed to improve road safety and support human and autonomous driving. The data infrastructure company uses AI to conduct predictive analysis to help manage traffic, and detect any potential anomalies on the road. Its technology has been shown to reduce human-driver accidents by 70pc, the company said. “This platform is designed to reduce crashes and save lives for everyday drivers first,” said Lundy, the company’s executive chairperson. “Every winter, Colorado drivers face black ice they cannot see, wrong-way drivers they cannot anticipate and closures they cannot avoid. “Vectigo detects those dangers in the lane and gets that information to the drivers, first responders and truckers who need it, in time to change course. The same data gives autonomous vehicles the redundancy they need to operate safely.” The company’s Colorado-based US division delivered its first in-ground pilot with RoadSafe Traffic Systems earlier this year. According to the company, the sensors light up in heavy snow, send warnings before black ice and alert drivers about a wrong-way driver. The platform works with every vehicle on the road today, with no special equipment, app or new car required, Vectigo said. The technology is particularly useful for autonomous vehicles, which use advanced sensors to detect obstacles on the road, though they cannot directly sense surface conditions such as ice or standing water and can be degraded by snow, glare and faded lane markings. 10 Ireland-based quantum experts you should know about
2026-10-29

10 Ireland-based quantum experts you should know about

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Ireland’s new ‘Quantum 2030’ has major ambitions to focus research capacity toward competitive growth. Although artificial intelligence continues to grab headlines, it isn’t the only advanced technology worth watching. Researchers have spent years developing quantum computing, which utilises the unique behaviours of quantum physics to its advantage, giving it exponential power to compute at a scale much faster than what we can achieve now. The technology has applications across industries. Logistics companies, for examples, use quantum computing to optimise supply chains, while pharmaceutical developers are able to run more detailed simulations while screening drug compounds. With a month of quantum and deep-tech-focused coverage at SiliconRepublic.com almost behind us, here are 10 quantum experts in Ireland you should know about. McCollam has been serving as a senior professor of quantum technology at University College Cork (UCC) since 2023, where she recently helped establish Ireland’s first dedicated quantum sensing laboratory. The €1.56m project is expected to help enable cutting-edge quantum physics and chemistry research across UCC. McCollam completed her bachelor’s degree in natural sciences in Trinity College Dublin (TCD), alongside a PhD at the Cavendish Laboratory in Cambridge. Following this, she worked as a postdoctoral fellow in the physics department at the University of Toronto before moving to the Netherlands to take up a number of positions, including a Marie Curie fellowship and as a faculty member at Radboud University. Dr Mjeda is a cybersecurity lecturer in Cork’s Munster Technical University, where she also serves as its quantum computing pillar lead. She began her academic career as a theoretical physicist, with a bachelors and masters in computer science, and a PhD in safety-critical systems at the University of Limerick (UL). Mjeda’s research spans across cybersecurity topics including secure software development and application security, as well as quantum software, autonomous systems, and innovations in software engineering education. She is a founding member of Lero, the UL-based Research Ireland centre for software. A University College Dublin (UCD) associate professor in quantum, Prof Blokhina has worked with Irish quantum computing start-up Equal1 for nearly a decade. She has been serving as its chief scientific officer since 2022. Blokhina is a member of the IoP and a senior member of the Institute of Electrical and Electronics Engineers, having previously served on the chair of several committees in the group. She completed her PhD in physical and mathematical sciences from Russia’s Saratov State University and received a postdoctoral degree in electronic engineering from the University of Sorbonne in Paris. Lynch leads Equal1 as its CEO. Founded in 2017, the company builds cost-effective, scalable quantum processing units (QPU). The start-up unveiled Ireland’s first home-grown QPU last year and announced a $60m raise in January. Previously the company’s chief operating officer, Lynch – prior to Equal1 – served as the director of IoT at Analog Devices for a number of years. He holds a masters in engineering from UCC. Lynch recently joined the Government’s industry-led advisory council on the semiconductor sector. Dr Fitzsimons served as a senior research fellow in the materials department at the University of Oxford between 2007 and 2010. During this time there, he co-invented universal blind quantum computation, now recognised as a key technology for securing cloud-based quantum computation. In 2018, Fitzsimons established Horizon Quantum, which recently went public on the Nasdaq. The Singapore-based company specialises in software infrastructure for quantum applications. Before starting his business, Fitzsimons also served as a principal investigator at the Centre for Quantum Technologies in Singapore. Presently, aside from his position as CEO of Horizon, Fitzsimons also leads the Southeast Asia Quantum Industry Association as its president and serves on the board of the Irish Chamber of Commerce in the country. A professor of physics at Trinity College Dublin (TCD), Dr Goold co-directs Rinn Quantum (the new Research Ireland centre for quantum based in the university) alongside another one of our list’s features. Goold completed his PhD in physics from UCC in 2010, the same year he received an INSPIRE Marie Curie Fellowship, which he undertook at the University of Oxford. He is also the recipient of an SFI Royal Society University fellowship for his project on thermodynamics for quantum technologies, and was awarded a European Research Council Starting Grant, which he used to found the QuSys research group. The VP of quantum technology at Fidelity Investments, and the founder and chair of Quantum Ireland, Dr Dascal is a tenured academic with a PhD from the University of Maryland. Dascal leads quantum algorithm, post-quantum security research and product strategy at Fidelity, meanwhile his work with the national industry-led consortium, Quantum Ireland, helps to advance enterprise initiatives to adopt the technology. In 2023, he co-founded DiviQ, a non-profit that works towards diversifying the quantum workforce through travel grants for underrepresented researchers. Hess is the chair professor of quantum nanophotonics and a Research Ireland professor at Trinity’s School of Physics and the CRANN Institute, where he also serves as the director of research. The tenured physicist has been co-running Rinn Quantum with Goold since its formation earlier this year, where his focus lies around quantum communication and its close links with quantum sensing, quantum materials and quantum simulation. Hess is also the inaugural editor-in-chief of the journal APL Quantum, an elected member of the Royal Irish Academy and a fellow of the Institute of Physics (IoP). Prof Davis, one of Ireland’s most renowned quantum experts, is set to receive the prestigious Michael Faraday Medal and Prize by the IoP next week for his contributions to the study of macroscopic quantum physics in emergent quantum matter. Davis is a quantum physics professor at UCC and holds external positions at the University of Oxford and Cornell University. He also sits on the membership of the National Advisory Forum for Quantum Technologies, a key group central to the Irish Government’s ‘Quantum 2030’ strategy. Davis spoke to SiliconRepublic.com in 2023 shortly after receiving the Buckly Prize for his work in the field. Chormaic is an adjunct professor at Trinity’s School of Physics, where she also contributes to the Rinn Network. A full time professor at the Okinawa Institute of Science and Technology Graduate University in Japan, Chormaic previously worked in UCC as a lecturer for a number of years. She holds a masters in atomic physics from Maynooth University and a PhD from the Sorbonne University Paris. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed.
