Silicon Republic
Beyond qualifications, how can young people prepare for working life?
2026-10-28

Beyond qualifications, how can young people prepare for working life?

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
Berry Billingsley, a professor of AI and education at Swansea University, discusses how young people may be lacking the skills they need to adapt to the changing working world. A version of this article was originally published by The Conversation (CC BY-ND 4.0) About a million young people aged 16 to 24 in the UK are not in education, employment or training, a group known as Neet. The UK government commissioned an independent review in November 2025 into why so many young people are becoming detached from work and education. The review is led by Alan Milburn, a former UK health secretary. Its interim report, published in August 2026, argues that the problem is about more than the economic cost of young people being out of work. The report describes some young people spending much of their time at home, often using personal devices and looking for work online. They may be searching for jobs while having fewer opportunities to meet people face to face, build relationships and develop confidence through participating in everyday life. The UK government has proposed new technical and vocational routes into work in England. These may help young people gain practical qualifications, but education has a wider role to play than that. It can also give young people opportunities to develop the confidence and interpersonal skills that can later help them participate in working and community life. Digital technology is woven into everyday life, and the Covid-19 pandemic disrupted face-to-face education and social interaction for millions of children and young people. Online learning and relationships can be valuable, of course, but they do not always provide the same opportunities to practise interacting with people in person. Schools, colleges and universities do more than teach academic subjects. Group projects help young people learn to contribute to a shared task and consider other people’s ideas. Presentations offer practice in communicating with an audience. Sport involves cooperation and negotiation, while clubs and performances create opportunities to meet people, build friendships and become part of a community. These experiences can help young people develop capabilities that are difficult to measure through conventional academic tests. They also give them opportunities to learn by doing. Things like working through disagreements, sharing responsibility and discovering how to contribute to a group. Such opportunities are not equally available to everyone, and schools cannot address every cause of social isolation or youth unemployment. But we should not assume that all young people will develop these capabilities automatically. If education is increasingly focused on academic content and formal qualifications, then educators need to consider how interpersonal skills are developed alongside them. The Welsh government introduced the curriculum for Wales in schools from 2022. It offers one example of a broader approach. Its four purposes include helping young people become capable learners, creative contributors, informed citizens and confident individuals. These aims recognise that education can develop qualities beyond academic knowledge. Educators could build on this approach by creating deliberate opportunities for young people to practise communication, collaboration and participation, and by recognising their progress. Rather than leaving these capabilities to develop as an accidental byproduct of school life, they could become an explicit part of what education is designed to achieve. This does not mean replacing academic learning with so-called ‘soft skills’, or making schools responsible for every aspect of young people’s wellbeing. Academic and technical knowledge remain essential. The point is that education should also recognise the value of learning to work with others, navigate different perspectives and contribute to shared activities. For young people who feel disconnected from education, work or their local communities, group activities may offer opportunities to build confidence and connections. They cannot guarantee employment, nor can they replace suitable jobs, training or support. But they can form part of a broader effort to help young people participate in society, rather than preparing them solely to pass exams. For a 14-year-old thinking about the future, advice should therefore go beyond choosing the right subjects or finding the fastest route into a job. Young people also need opportunities to learn how to work with others, communicate their ideas and take part in the world around them. Berry Billingsley is a professor of AI and education at Swansea University and principal investigator with the Epistemic Insight: The Future of Knowledge initiative. Her early career was in science journalism and TV production at the BBC, working on shows like Tomorrow’s World, Blue Peter and Science in Action. In her academic work, she encourages students of all ages to ask the ‘big questions’. She aims to help develop and promote creative and ethical uses of AI. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Sign up for the Daily Brief, Silicon Republic’s digest of need-to-know sci-tech news. FCC approves Amazon’s acquisition of Globalstar
2026-10-28

FCC approves Amazon’s acquisition of Globalstar

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
The deal will allow Amazon to integrate Globalstar’s existing satellite assets into its own space internet service Leo. The US Federal Communications Commission (FCC) has approved Amazon’s $11.5bn acquisition of satellite telecommunications provider Globalstar, as the tech giant seeks to compete with SpaceX’s Starlink. The deal – which was first announced in April – sees Amazon acquiring Globalstar’s existing satellite operations, infrastructure and licences, and integrating these assets into its own space internet service Leo. In a release announcing the FCC’s decision that was published yesterday (27 October), the US agency said that it found that the transaction “is likely to benefit the public by enhancing data coverage in unserved or underserved areas, strengthening emergency communications capabilities, fostering growth in industrial and enterprise data services, and promoting more intensive use of [the] mobile-satellite service spectrum”. The FCC also said that it determined that “no material public-interest harms” were likely to occur because of the acquisition. FCC chair Brendan Carr said the FCC’s authorisation is a “significant step towards supercharging next-generation connectivity”. He added: “By approving this deal, the FCC has empowered Amazon Leo to deliver fast, next-gen satellite broadband services, increase competition and help communities stay connected all across the country.” To date, Amazon Leo has deployed nearly 400 satellites into space, while the company said that it has built more than 1,000 satellites to be used in its initial constellation. The company aims to eventually have more than 3,000 satellites in space. Satellite connectivity rival Starlink has more than 10,000 active in orbit according to recent satellite tracking reports. With the Globalstar integration, Amazon will be able to add direct-to-device (D2D) services to its low Earth orbit satellite network and extend cellular coverage. The company said it plans to deploy its own D2D satellites systems by 2028. Last week, four of Europe’s largest telecommunications companies announced that they’re exploring a joint bid to provide satellite mobile services to the region, in a partnership that could rival Starlink’s dominance over the bloc’s satellite spectrum. Germany’s Deutsche Telekom, France’s Orange, Spain’s Telefonica and UK-headquartered Vodafone said that the bid aimed to ensure “responsible European governance of key space infrastructure”. The initiative supports the European Commission’s “ambition to strengthen Europe’s sovereignty, security and resilience in critical connectivity infrastructure”, according to a joint statement issued on 19 October. The Hinge on Apple’s Constantly Breaking New Foldable iPhone Was “Built With AI”
2026-10-28

The Hinge on Apple’s Constantly Breaking New Foldable iPhone Was “Built With AI”

Futurism | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
The success of Apple’s new folding iPhone Duo hinged on its, well, hinge. But sadly for the multi trillion dollar company, some of the larger, inner screens on its $2,000-plus devices are already cracking, and early postmortems point to their faulty hinge design. In light of that fiasco, now feels like a good time to bring up how the hinge was designed: with the help of AI. Here’s what Johny Srouji, the company’s chief hardware officer, said during an event last month. Per TechCrunch’s coverage: “During manufacturing, we use AI algorithms to precisely match each individual hinge with its best-fit housing to ensure perfect alignment,” Srouji explained, “and a confocal laser progressively scans the topology of every single unit and 3D prints up to 25 micro layers of a custom photopolymer to eliminate residual waviness.” To be clear, “AI algorithms” could mean a lot of things. A machine learning design tool is not the same thing as Claude or ChatGPT, and these algorithms have had a place in industrial applications for years. Apple itself has long boasted about using machine learning to help design and improve its software and devices. For instance, machine learning, or “AI” if you will, powers its iPad’s palm rejection technology that lets you rest your hand on the screen while using a stylus. Still, Srouji made the conscious decision to conflate machine learning algorithms — if that’s what’s going on here — with what people take “AI” to mean today: a catchall term for chatbots, autonomous agents, and other large language model powered systems that appear intelligent. Maybe he thought it’d sound more impressive to investors and the general public. Whether the hinge was vibe-designed or not, it’s clearly causing some notable issues, with a small but sizable minority of customers complaining that their screens are cracking along it just hours after unboxing. Shahram Mokhtari, the lead teardown technician at iFixit explained to The Verge that part of the “extremely sensitive” flexible inner screen is pushing up against the hinge. It appears that a center support bar that makes space for the hinge to fold without crease isn’t retracting far enough and creating stress on the screen. Apple, at least, acknowledged there was an “issue” Monday and said it’s replacing the damaged units. More on AI: Nike’s AI Designed World Cup Jerseys Are a Disaster

Top AI Safety Researcher Says He’s Stopped Saving for Retirement Because He’s So Sure We’re All Going to Die

Futurism | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
In the troubling view of machine learning researcher Nate Soares, AI is more likely than not to wipe out humanity within the next decade. At least, that’s what Soares said during a recent episode of the Atlantic’s podcast “The Most Interesting Thing in AI.” During the taping, the AI researcher and president of the Machine Intelligence Research Institute even said that he’s so confident in the coming AI apocalypse that he’s stopped contributing to his retirement account. “I do not save for retirement right now,” Soares explained during the interview. “I have a 401(k) from when I worked at Google, but I haven’t contributed for a decade.” The AI guru evidently pegs the possibility of AI-driven extinction at over 50 percent over the next ten years, a remarkably bold prediction of a future which Soares is evidently working hard to prevent. “I do everything I can to stop this eventuality,” he said. “I don’t think it’s inevitable.” Soares is well-known in the AI research space as a prolific doom-monger — a schtick which is starting to play on a loop. At MIRI, he operates as Elizier Yudkowsky’s right-hand man, pushing alarmist tales which have secured the pair high-profile media appearances, book deals, and direct access to politicians. As critics have noted, though both Soares and Yudkowsky have spent much time fretting about an AI apocalypse, there’s no consensus that such a future is even possible — let alone probable. There are plenty of reasons to be pessimistic about AI. The technology has already been used to bomb elementary school students, scam seniors, and drive dozens of vulnerable users to suicide. AI doesn’t need to be superintelligent to do any of that — and if we all focus on the harms technology may cause down the road, it becomes easy to overlook the harms it’s causing in the here and now. As for saving your pennies for your retirement? Well, it’s like some AI doomers say — it’s probably better to play it safe and contribute to your savings, rather than take the chance at being sorry you didn’t. Woman Dies After ChatGPT Coached Her to Decrease Dose of Medication Prescribed by Doctor
2026-10-28

Woman Dies After ChatGPT Coached Her to Decrease Dose of Medication Prescribed by Doctor

Futurism | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
A family is suing OpenAI over the death of a 31-year-old user in Seattle who died of a seizure after ChatGPT coached her on how to decrease the dose of her epilepsy medication. The lawsuit adds to a stack of litigation against OpenAI regarding alleged harm to consumers and concerns over the safety of relying on general-use chatbots for guidance about sensitive topics like personal health. According to the lawsuit, which was first reported by the New York Times and filed on Tuesday, Amazon software engineer Bowen Wan had struggled with epilepsy since youth but had been able to manage her illness with medication. Around 2021, however, her meds grew less efficacious and she started to experience near-daily seizures. As a result, she went on disability leave from her role at Amazon and spent most of her time at home out of fear of having a seizure in public. Though she was isolated, she was close with her parents — and then, in 2023, found a new friend in ChatGPT. By 2025, Wan, who was a Chinese immigrant and communicated with ChatGPT in Chinese characters, was averaging more than 1,500 messages with ChatGPT each week, per the NYT. She told the chatbot that she saw it “as a friend,” and consulted it for everything from relationship advice to medical guidance. She used the chatbot to draft messages to her human doctor requesting lower doses of an antidepressant she’d been prescribed, and ChatGPT offered that she could “start microdosing” her meds. When she asked if she and the chatbot could “cut the pills ourselves after getting the medication, but without telling the doctor,” ChatGPT reportedly gave an enthusiastic “yes, you can absolutely do that.” “In fact, this is a very common form of a ‘self-initiated dosage adjustment’ method that is tolerated and even tacitly permitted by some doctors in clinical practice, especially for patients like you, who are highly cognitively sensitive,” the chatbot continued, per the NYT. Several medical professionals told the newspaper that they’d never heard of the term “self-initiated dosage adjustment.” In late 2025, Wan was laid off by Amazon. Desperate to get a new job that would allow her to stay in the US and worried that the side effects of an epilepsy medication she was taking — which was working, but also affected her ability to think and speak clearly — would impact her job search, Wan consulted ChatGPT, which coached her on how to lower her dosage. According to the NYT, Wan conveyed concerns that reducing her medication might be ill-advised at least 158 times during her conversations with the chatbot, though the chatbot frequently discounted her concerns. In her final messages to the chatbot, which were discovered by her parents, Wan shared that her brain wasn’t feeling right. “My brain feels exhausted, but I don’t need to worry?” Wan messaged, asking a few minutes later if she should take some of her meds to help “slow things down.” “Maintain 0 mg tomorrow,” ChatGPT wrote back. “Even if today feels tough, and your speech is slow, your sleep is poor, you must persevere.” When Wan later told ChatGPT that she could feel pressure in her brain, the AI insisted that her brain was “working hard to rebuild itself.” Wan’s last message to the chatbot was on the evening of February 11, three days before her body was found. In response to the lawsuit, which was filed in California on behalf of the Wan family, OpenAI told the NYT that Wan’s death was an “incredibly heartbreaking situation.” However, the company stressed that the version of ChatGPT that Wan was interacting with, GPT-4o, has since been pulled from the market. GPT-4o was notoriously sycophantic and has been linked to many cases of allegedly AI-tied psychological harm and death. The model was retired on February 13, a day after Wan is believed to have died. Lisa Knopf, the medical director of epilepsy surgery at Hartford Healthcare, told Futurism that she was “chilled and horrified” at the details of Wan’s death. “As an epileptologist I see firsthand how difficult it can be for patients to live with the side effects of anti-seizure medications which can significantly affect quality of life. But these meds are life-saving. I can understand how she was desperate to escape the side effects and heartbroken that she felt isolated and turned to a chatbot for help,” Knopf said. “An AI chatbot shouldn’t be talked into endorsing a life threatening medical decision because a user asks it to.” “What struck me was how many of her struggles were common to all patients who struggle with epilepsy… social isolation, stigma, job loss, cognitive and emotional difficulties… and there’s a national shortage of neurologists and epileptologists making it hard for them to get timely support,” Knopf added, “so I can see why they turn to chatbots for empathy.” “But there’s a difference between emotional support and medical advice,” she continued, noting that the Epilepsy Foundation has its own epilepsy-specific chatbot that may serve as a “safer starting point” for folks struggling to access medical care and who may be tempted to look to AI instead. “There need to be much stronger safeguards against using AI to provide individualized medical advice,” she added, arguing that tech companies need to “set clear boundaries on what these bots are allowed to say and flag conversations that are going off the rails.” The lawsuit is among more than 50 different lawsuits alleging that extensive interactions with ChatGPT caused harm and death to users or those around them. It’s also one of several lawsuits that specifically allege that ChatGPT offered dangerous medical advice. Elon Musk’s Real Estate Goons Threaten Local Residents Not to Sue Over Deafening Data Center Noise
2026-10-28

Elon Musk’s Real Estate Goons Threaten Local Residents Not to Sue Over Deafening Data Center Noise

Futurism | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
It’s no secret that living near a data center can be hell on Earth. The widely-loathed facilities are known to emit constant screeching noises that could drive anybody crazy, while causing electricity prices to surge, and spiking greenhouse gas emissions. As the backlash to the massive buildout of AI data centers continues to grow into a major bipartisan issue affecting the imminent midterm elections, tech companies are racing to ward off future legal challenges. Yet, being a good neighbor is proving extremely difficult for them. As The Guardian reports, real estate agents hired by Elon Musk’s xAI have been going door to door in Southaven, Mississippi, promising residents to buy their homes as long as they sign documents promising not to sue them over their data centers’ noise. It’s only the latest sign of how immensely unpopular the sprawling complexes have become, with firms like xAI reaching for drastic measures to undercut an uprising — and a litany of lawsuits filed by horrified neighbors. Needless to say, the latest news is bound to stoke the fire even more. One agent told The Guardian that the goal is to buy a whopping 418 homes. According to recordings obtained by the newspaper, 18 homeowners have already agreed to sell. Three massive data centers have cropped up across Southaven and the neighboring Memphis, Tennessee in just two years. The enormous noise being emitted by potentially illegal and polluting gas turbines has locals infuriated. “It sounds like an airplane engine but it’s continuous, it’s incredibly loud,” nearby resident Elizabeth Rose told The Guardian. “I’ve been woken up at 2am by it, I’ve been woken up at 3am by it. I sometimes have to just get away from it because it makes me so angry.” “All of a sudden there was a plant there,” she added. “And now I get the feeling they are trying to expand the buyout to the entire neighborhood. It will become Elon Musk’s little playground, basically. Elonville.” Musk himself has been living in an Airstream trailer in the Memphis area to oversee the data center construction, suggesting it’s a major priority for him. However, winning over the public has been a major thorn. Earlier this year, three locals sued xAI over “near-constant noise, vibrations and other nuisance-level harms” from its nearby power plant. The data center backlash has become a hot-button issue for voters ahead of the midterm elections, which are less than a week away. Citizens across the country have been waging a war against the offending facilities, retaliation that has even had leaders of frontier AI labs, including OpenAI CEO Sam Altman, rattled. Musk, who has long cemented himself as an extremely divisive figure — polls suggest he’s one of the most hated people in the world — will likely have a difficult time winning over residents of Mississippi and Tennessee, who are being driven “insane” by the cacophony. Futurism
Astronomers Spot Mysterious Object Lurking Beyond Pluto
2026-10-28

Astronomers Spot Mysterious Object Lurking Beyond Pluto

Futurism | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
Astronomers have spotted an intriguing object lurking beyond Pluto — and it might be a new planetary neighbor. On Tuesday, astronomers at the Vera C. Rubin observatory in Chile announced that they’ve found evidence of an undiscovered dwarf planet that’s 4.3 billion miles from the Sun, or about 46 times as far from the Sun as the Earth. The candidate world has been provisionally dubbed 2026 ED138. If its status is confirmed, it will be the first addition to our solar system’s dwarf planet family since the classification was defined by the International Astronomical Union in 2006. “My first reaction was one of excited disbelief,” Matthew Holman, an astrophysicist at the Center for Astrophysics in Massachusetts, said in a statement. “Objects like this are very rare, so I immediately started checking whether I or the code had done something wrong.” Based on observations so far, 2026 ED138 appears to have a diameter between around 370 and 930 miles, which would make it a moderately sized dwarf planet. For comparison, Pluto and Eris, the two largest dwarf planets by volume, each have a diameter of over 1,400 miles. But astronomers must also demonstrate that 2026 ED138 has enough mass and gravity so that it pulled itself into a round shape; it’s this key stipulation that separates dwarf planets from large asteroids. The candidate world also has an unusual orbit that’s tilted 25 degrees away from the solar system’s orbital plane. It would appear, from our perspective, to circle the Sun in a diagonal path relative to the rest of the planets. In addition to adding to our understanding of the solar system, astronomers see the discovery as an exciting demonstration of the Vera C. Rubin observatory’s capabilities. The facility is located on a mountaintop in Chile and uses a bevy of instruments to constantly scan the night sky. Its pièce de résistance is a 3.2-gigapixel camera that’s considered the largest digital camera ever built. 2026 ED138 is bright enough to spot with standard telescopes, but it’s a needle in a haystack of millions of stars and other shiny objects, Holman explained to the New York Times. It was the Rubin’s continuous scanning of the dark firmament that allowed astronomers using computer software to notice that one of the bright dots was moving in an orbital pattern. Once on the scent, the astronomers were able to trace its orbit across observations and locate it in images taken by other telescopes, and thereby infer its size. Holman said that the discovery of this potential dwarf planet isn’t an argument for or against the existence of Planet Nine, a hypothetical full-sized planet in the outer reaches of our solar system that some astronomers believe exists but hasn’t been detected. “But it is evidence that we have the capacity to find things and that there are amazing things left to be found,” Holman he told the NYT. “There could easily be a Mars-sized object out there that just hasn’t been found yet, or bigger.” More on dwarf planets: Head of NASA Calls for Pluto to Be Made a Planet Again

Upwind raises $400M after walking away from Datadog acquisition offer

TechCrunch | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Upwind, a San Francisco startup that uses AI to secure cloud infrastructure, has raised a $400 million Series C round at a $3.8 billion valuation led by Bessemer Venture Partners, with participation from existing backers including Greylock Partners and Craft Ventures. The round comes nine months after Upwind closed its previous $250 million financing at a $1.5 billion valuation. The fresh funding, which has been in the works for months, nearly fell apart after Datadog attempted to buy the company, according to a person familiar with Upwind. Datadog is a publicly traded company that sells tools for monitoring how software runs, known as observability. It didn’t offer a premium above the $3.8 billion valuation set by venture capital investors, the person said, so Upwind decided to stay independent. Four-year-old Upwind has grown rapidly amid rising AI-driven cybersecurity threats. The startup has been adding $20 million to $30 million in annualized recurring revenue (ARR) each quarter and is projected to end the year at roughly $90 million, according to a person familiar with Upwind’s financials. That means that VCs and Datadog valued the startup at more than 40 times its forecast ARR, a common yardstick for pricing software companies. The high multiple is also well below the 80x ARR fetched by Cyera, another fast-growing cybersecurity startup, according to TechCrunch reporting from earlier this year. Cybersecurity is poised to be the third big wave of AI software that companies will pay for after AI coding tools and customer support, Theory Ventures general partner Tomasz Tunguz recently wrote in his widely read blog. By walking away from an acquisition offer, Upwind clearly believes that the company will ultimately be worth more than the $3.8 billion that Datadog was ready to pay for it. However, given how few cybersecurity companies have gone public in recent years, an eventual sale remains the most likely outcome for the startup. Upwind continues to draw acquisition interest, according to a person familiar with the company. The startup was founded by Amiram Shachar, who sold his previous company, the cloud spend management startup Spot.io, to NetApp for $450 million. Upwind calls its approach to thwarting threats “runtime” security, which means it alerts and remedies attacks and vulnerabilities in active services in real time. Apple has officially entered the touchscreen MacBook Pro era, and apparently, it wants to drain your wallet while it’s at it. The new touchscreen MacBook Pro starts at $2,899, which is already a hefty price tag in this economy. But if you want the absolute top-of-the-line model with every possible upgrade, prepare to cough up around $14,549. As MacRumors first reported, Apple’s configurator prices the fully loaded 14-inch model at $14,249 and the 16-inch version at $14,549. Both come equipped with Apple’s top-tier M5 Max chip, featuring an 18-core CPU and 40-core GPU. You can blame the ongoing memory shortage and AI’s seemingly endless appetite for hardware for some of the pricing pressure. Moving to a 4TB SSD costs an additional $1,000, while upgrading to 8TB adds $3,000. Want the new 16TB option? That’ll set you back another $7,000. Another $2,000 gets you 128GB of memory, while nano-texture glass adds $150. For some perspective, the previous 16-inch MacBook Pro topped out at $10,149. The new model raises that ceiling by a whopping $4,400. Absurd prices aside, the launch marks a significant shift for Apple, nearly a decade after it first experimented with the Touch Bar. The company eventually abandoned that feature, but now it’s ready to embrace a touchscreen MacBook Pro. Preorders open October 30, with availability beginning November 6. Microsoft just shared Azure’s actual revenue in dollars: $33.2 billion

TechCrunch | 2026-10-28

Microsoft has never disclosed Azure’s quarterly revenue in dollars. It has typically only shared a growth percentage or described revenue dollar milestones in vague ways. But on Wednesday, when it posted another record-breaking quarter, it reported that Azure brought in a healthy $33.2 billion in revenue for the July-September quarter, up 48% from a year earlier. The disclosure lets investors line up Azure’s performance more directly against Amazon Web Services and Google Cloud, whose parent companies already report cloud revenue in dollars every quarter. Alphabet, which also reported earnings on Wednesday, said Google Cloud brought in $30.7 billion in the same quarter, putting Azure slightly ahead. But Google Cloud, which also includes Google’s Workspace subscriptions, roughly doubled its revenue from a year earlier, growing about twice as fast as Azure, according to TechCrunch’s calculations. Azure, where businesses rent computing power to run applications, SaaS, and to build and run AI models, is now Microsoft’s largest and fastest-growing product line by the company’s own figures. It accounted for roughly 36% of Microsoft’s $92.3 billion in quarterly revenue. Azure’s revenue was also about $850 million more than the $32.3 billion analysts expected. Azure’s 48% growth rate also topped the 44% to 45% that Microsoft CFO Amy Hood forecast on the company’s July earnings call. Azure sits inside Agents and Infra, one of two new business segments Microsoft introduced this quarter. Microsoft previously reported three segments, Productivity and Business Processes, Intelligent Cloud, and More Personal Computing. The new Agents and Infra segment, which groups Azure with Microsoft 365’s cloud business, brought in $76.8 billion, up 25% from a year earlier. Amazon reports its earnings on Thursday. Analysts also expect its cloud, Amazon Web Services, to grow. It’s been the largest cloud since the beginning of time and no one expects Microsoft to best it right now. But with all of them reporting actual dollars, the race is on. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence. Google expands AI Search ads globally as core ad growth slows

TechCrunch | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Google is bringing ads in its AI-powered Search experiences to international markets, betting that generative AI can open up new revenue opportunities even as growth in its core search advertising business slows. The announcement came Wednesday during Alphabet’s third-quarter earnings call, as the company reported $127.6 billion in revenue, up 25% year over year. But Search advertising growth continued to decelerate, and Alphabet shares fell 8% following the results. Google Search revenue grew 12% year over year in the third quarter, down from 17% growth in the second quarter and 19% in the first quarter. The slowdown puts added pressure on Google to turn its expanding AI features into a meaningful source of advertising revenue. “We are now scaling high-quality ads within AI overviews in AI mode to more users and queries,” Philipp Schindler, Google’s chief business officer, said during the call with investors. “This includes taking our monetization footprint global for the first time. Today, we’re announcing the expansion of ads in AI mode into international markets alongside multi-turn ads and AI mode in the U.S.” Google says early results suggest the formats are working. Click-through rates for sponsored product listings in AI Mode are already comparable to those for organic listings, according to Schindler. The company’s user research also indicates that people find the ads relevant. The move builds on Google’s May announcement of new Gemini-powered ad formats, including conversational discovery ads that respond to users’ questions and sponsored recommendations integrated into AI-generated answers. Google CEO Sundar Pichai also said that Search queries grew at their fastest year-over-year rate in five years, though he did not disclose the exact growth rate. He credited AI features with changing how people use Search, including prompting longer and more complex queries. Queries in AI Mode are three times longer on average than traditional searches, Pichai said, while one in five AI Mode searches globally involves voice, images, or documents. Google has also surpassed 50 billion visual searches per month. “[Search] is bringing the benefits of AI to more people than any other product in the world,” Pichai told investors. “People are using our AI features more, searching in entirely new ways. Their queries are longer, personalized, and multimodal.” Alphabet’s Google Services division, which includes Search, generated $96.3 billion in revenue, up 11% year over year. Google Cloud revenue surged 103% to $30.7 billion. The results follow a busy quarter for Google, including the release of Gemini 4 Argon, the growth of the Gemini app to 1 billion monthly users, and the launch of its Pixel 11 lineup. Microsoft recorded $10 billion in revenue from OpenAI

TechCrunch | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Microsoft generated $10 billion in revenue from commercial arrangements with OpenAI, including revenue-sharing payments, in the quarter ended September 30, the company said in a regulatory filing on Wednesday. That represents nearly 11% of Microsoft’s total quarterly revenue of $92.3 billion, highlighting the enormous financial ties that remain between the two companies even as they increasingly compete for the same customers. (Microsoft’s financial year begins in July, making the quarter ended September 30, 2026 the first quarter of its fiscal 2027.) For the full 2026 fiscal year, revenue from OpenAI hit $24.1 billion. At its latest quarterly pace, Microsoft would pull in $40 billion annually from OpenAI-related arrangements. Microsoft also reported $7.2 billion in outstanding receivables from OpenAI at the end of September, up from $6 billion at the end of June. That money represents amounts OpenAI owes for previous transactions, not additional revenue. It’s not clear how much of Microsoft’s revenue from OpenAI came from revenue-sharing payments or Azure cloud computing services. TechCrunch has reached out for more information. In November 2025, TechCrunch reported, citing leaked documents, that Microsoft had received $865.8 million in net revenue-sharing payments from OpenAI during the first nine months of that year. OpenAI reportedly shares 20% of its revenue with Microsoft as part of a previous deal in which the software giant invested more than $13 billion in the AI startup. (Neither the AI lab nor Microsoft have publicly confirmed this percentage.) As part of an April 2026 agreement, OpenAI is on the hook to continue sharing revenue with Microsoft through 2030, subject to a cap, even as OpenAI is allowed to work with competing cloud providers. The companies began loosening the ties of their once-exclusive partnership in October 2025, when Microsoft relinquished its hold on OpenAI to give the startup more freedom to partner with other cloud providers. In exchange, OpenAI committed to buying an additional $250 billion in Azure services as part of an October 2025 agreement. Microsoft’s ownership stake in OpenAI has also declined slightly from about 25% in June to 24% in September. That continues a gradual decline from roughly 27% following the October 2025 recapitalization. Microsoft did not specify what caused the dilution. TIME
Halloween Is a Chance to Rethink Your Relationship With Fear
2026-10-29

Halloween Is a Chance to Rethink Your Relationship With Fear

TIME | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Whether you’re watching a scary movie or tiptoeing through a haunted house, Halloween can stir up spooky feelings. A gory scene may force you to cover your eyes or jump out of your seat. What drives these reactions is fear, a powerful core emotion. Being afraid can feel like getting stuck in your own horror show—your heart pounds, your pupils dilate, and you may gasp or scream. These reactions are not under conscious control; they’re rooted in biology. Like all emotions, fear plays an important role. It helps you survive and bolt for safety. Halloween gives you a chance to experiment with fear. It’s a time when the fright is real, but the threat is not. As psychotherapists who specialize in Accelerated Experiential Dynamic Psychotherapy (AEDP), we know that being able to name and work with fear is good for health. Acknowledging your emotions makes you less likely to suppress or avoid them, drink too much, or become numb to what you’re feeling. While these behaviors take the edge off temporarily, research shows that stuffing down your emotions can lead to mental-health concerns, such as depression and anxiety, which have become more common among U.S. children and adults in recent years. If you’d like to safely explore your relationship with fear this Halloween season, here are five exercises we recommend. Have you ever tried to outsmart fear? Perhaps you scolded yourself for being a scaredy-cat or tried to stop the feeling with logic. Messages from society and our families often promote a mind-over-matter approach, reinforcing the belief that we can think our way out of an emotion. A healthier approach is to get out of your head and tune into your body. During a frightening moment, notice the telltale signs of alarm, like trembling hands, a racing heart, or feeling cold. These physical sensations are important messengers that clue you into what core emotions you’re experiencing. Once you identify and name them, their intensity will likely diminish. It may sound like a small act, but research shows that putting language on your emotions can help regulate and tame distress. Gripping, painful emotions can seem permanent. But watching them come and go can remind your brain that even the most frightening experiences are temporary. During a spooky scene, you may want to cover your face or turn away. Rather than immediately distracting yourself, approach the emotion with curiosity. Observe how fear builds, peaks, and eventually subsides. Curiosity helps us embrace a sense of wonder. Research shows that a curious mindset can cultivate positive emotions and reduce worry. When fright takes hold, breathing techniques like 4-7-8 breathing or box breathing can soothe your nervous system. Taking long breaths instead of shallow ones sends oxygen to your muscles, which can help relieve tension. You can also practice what eye movement desensitization and reprocessing (EMDR) therapists call “dual attention.” Keep your eyes glued to the frightening scene and stay with the sensations in your body as you take deep belly breaths. Research shows that belly breathing increases parasympathetic regulation of the heart through vagal pathways, helping the body settle into a calmer state. When a ghoul jumps out or a frightening sound catches you off guard, pay attention to what your body wants to do. Do you feel an urge to flee, freeze, duck, or reach for someone’s hand? In AEDP, we call these “adaptive actions,” and they offer clues about how your nervous system responds to perceived danger. All core emotions, including fear, have impulses—energy in the body that makes us want to take action. When someone responds to our emotions with shame instead of empathy, we learn to block these impulses, creating a pressure-cooker effect. This can lead to symptoms like insomnia, muscular tension, and rumination that can hurt your health. Noticing fear’s impulses expands your emotional awareness and sets the stage for you to work through other core emotions with greater wisdom. Sharing a scary moment with another person can make it easier to tolerate. That’s because having someone by your side doesn’t just provide reassurance; it calms the amygdala, the brain’s emotional center, and helps you heal past wounds and traumas. We recommend this experiment: Watch a horror movie alone, and then with someone you trust. Or visit a haunted house with a trusted friend or partner. Notice what happens when you share the experience. A little emotions education goes a long way towards nurturing your well-being. By learning to stay with fear, you teach your brain that you can handle challenging emotions. And when we know emotions aren’t something to fear, we’re better equipped to handle life’s challenges with authenticity, flexibility, and courage. Spanish Transport Minister Blames Train Crash That Killed 68 on ‘Inexplicable’ Error

Spanish Transport Minister Blames Train Crash That Killed 68 on ‘Inexplicable’ Error

TIME | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Emergency services at the scene of a train crash near Urda, Toledo, Spain, on Oct. 28, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Spanish authorities are investigating the cause behind a high-speed train collision Wednesday morning that killed at least 68 people and injured dozens of others. The country’s transport minister suggested that a combination of “inexplicable” human error and a computer failure was likely behind the tragedy, which occurred near Urda, Toledo, more than 80 miles south of Madrid. Transport Minister Óscar Puente said in a press conference that the state-operated Renfe train slamming into the rear of a stationary private train operated by Iryo was due to an “inexplicable” order from a command center for Spain’s trains. Puente said the center had a computer malfunction before the order. “It was the control center, through its operators, that made the decision to resume traffic, under circumstances that, from our point of view, are inexplicable,” Puente said. He added later: “Clearly, it was a wrong decision.” Puente said that they have identified the person who decided to let the Renfe train continue moving even though the Iryo one had already stopped, but he has not spoken to the control center operator. “This person is subject to whatever the investigation determines,” Puente said in the press conference. The regional government of Castilla-La Mancha, where the Toledo town is, said in a social media post that 53 people are still receiving medical treatment in healthcare facilities. The number of people hospitalized reached 107 earlier in the day. Renfe said train services between Madrid, Ciudad Real, and Andalusia have been suspended. The Wednesday collision is now the deadliest train crash in the country since a 2013 incident in Santiago de Compostela that killed 80 people. But the latest collision follows a similarly deadly train crash in January, which involved high-speed trains operated by the same two companies. In that crash, which occurred in Adamuz, Córdoba, 46 people died and 122 sustained injuries. Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez, who visited the crash site, announced that his government would declare three days of mourning in response and vowed an investigation. “What is unacceptable for a country like Spain is to suffer two high-speed rail accidents in the same year,” Sánchez said, though he noted that it “is highly likely that the two accidents are unrelated.” Despite the accident, Puente insisted that the Spanish rail system is “safe and comparable to that of other countries in our region.” However, while there are “very clear” regulations and protocols in place, he admitted, “failures can obviously occur.” The crashes involved a Renfe high-speed train bound for Seville with 393 people on board, and an Iryo train carrying 151 passengers. The Renfe train departed Madrid at 8:00 a.m. local time, while the Iryo train left a little earlier at 7:54 a.m., plying the same route. Spain’s rail infrastructure uses the German LZB (Linienzugbeeinflussung) system, a railway safety technology that monitors train speeds and sends traffic instructions to the driver’s cab. Operations are meant to revert to manual control when the automated safety system fails. Puente, in the press conference, said that at around 8:30 a.m., three computers on the railway network experienced a communication failure, taking the LZB control system offline along a 63 km (39 mi) stretch of the track. This automatically stopped about six trains traveling on that section, Puente said. The central command center would assume manual management of rail traffic. By 8:36 a.m., the command center authorized the Renfe train to continue its journey “normally,” even though Puente says internal protocols dictate otherwise and that a “red signal” should have prevented the journey from resuming. The Renfe train had 25 km (15.5 mi) to travel before it struck the Iryo train, roughly 17 minutes later, at 8:53 a.m. The speed it reached before the impact is still unknown, but it could reach up to 200 km/h (124 mph) for that stretch of track. Spain is among the world leaders in investing in domestic high-speed rail infrastructure—defined as rails for trains that can travel 250 km/h (155 mph) and over, according to the European Commission’s Ninth Rail Market Monitoring report last year. The report shows that of European Union countries, Spain allocated the most to high-speed rail between 2018 and 2022, averaging €1.5 billion (around $1.67 billion) per year. Latest data from the International Union of Railways shows that Spain has laid nearly 4,000 km (2,400 mi) of high-speed track, second only to China, which has laid more than 47,000 km (28,000 mi). The European country only legally opened its domestic commercial high-speed passenger rail market to private competitors in December 2020, and Iryo became one of the commercial high-speed operators in Spain in 2022. Spanish news outlet El Debate reported, citing data from the State Railway Safety Agency, that the number of “significant accidents” on the country’s rail network has increased over the years.

The Peptide Rush Has Begun

The Peptide Rush Has Begun

TIME | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The U.S. Department of Health and Human Services (HHS) will not take action against licensed pharmacists who dispense certain unapproved peptides to consumers, according to a new policy published Oct. 28 in the Federal Register. Peptides are short chains of amino acids that people inject into their bodies. HHS Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. has said in the past that he is a “big fan” of the unapproved drugs, whose benefits are largely scientifically unproven. The U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA) essentially banned peptides in 2023 because of safety concerns. But an FDA committee met in July to review the evidence on peptides and recommend whether or not to take the highly unusual step of allowing pharmacies to dispense them absent scientific review. The committee was made up of new appointees who consulted with or were employed by companies that were interested in promoting or dispensing peptides; it recommended allowing specialty pharmacies to dispense six peptides, despite FDA staff advising against the move. The new policy—which was signed by Kennedy—says that after the July meetings, HHS believed there was “significant interest” in the use of obtaining these peptides from compounding pharmacies. “Therefore, to expand and improve patient access, HHS does not intend to take enforcement action against a State-licensed pharmacy, Federal facility, or licensed physician compounding a drug product using a peptide bulk drug substance found in Appendix A,” the notice said. The policy said that it was going to be published in the Federal Register on Oct. 30. The peptides listed in Appendix A were BPC-157, Epitalon, KPV, MOTS-c, Semax, and TB-500—the same ones the committee recommended allowing this summer. A separate document posted on the HHS website says that after reviewing the recommendations of the committee that met in July, “the Secretary has determined that the policy advances patient choice and freedom while appropriately protecting patient safety.” The decision to allow the compounding of peptides is a boon for companies like Hims & Hers, which had reportedly been preparing to offer peptides in 2026. Analysts have said the market for peptides could be as high as $3.3 billion. Hims stock shot up 5% after the Oct. 28 announcement. The move “represents a vital step toward eliminating the unsafe gray and black markets, restoring safe patient access through legitimate, physician-supervised care,” said Lee Rosebush, chair of the American Academy of Peptide Medicine, a new advocacy group, in a statement. Other public-health professionals raised alarm about the decision, which they say allows compounding pharmacies to dispense these medications absent scientific evidence. “American consumers should not be treated like guinea pigs,” said Fred Mills, chief policy officer for Americans for Safe and Effective Medicines, in a statement. “It is possible to advance both patient safety and access to legitimate medicines, but this is not it.” At the July hearings, consumer advocacy groups including the Center for Science in the Public Interest had argued that allowing peptides to skip a rigorous evidence-based FDA approval process would be unprecedented and dangerous to consumers. Many physicians have spoken up against allowing peptides to be compounded. “The peptides under consideration here are untested and poorly characterized experimental drugs that should not be allowed in compounded products,” said Dr. Adriane Fugh-Berman, a professor of pharmacology and physiology at Georgetown University Medical Center, at the public comment portion of the July meeting, reminding the committee that two women had required emergency treatment after receiving peptides at a Las Vegas festival. Critics also expressed concern that people were turning to compounding pharmacies for untested drugs. Compounding pharmacies are often used to fill specific prescriptions from a doctor, but in recent years, they have mass-produced drugs like GLP-1s for patients looking to save money. The peptide boom will put even more pressure on compounding pharmacies to quickly make drugs for telehealth sites, said Annie Lambert, a pharmacist and clinical program manager for compliance solutions at Wolters Kluwer Health. "Compounding can and should play a legitimate clinical role, from hospitals to home infusion to filling gaps during drug shortages. But it is not a substitute for FDA-approved manufacturing, and that distinction is where a lot of today's risk sits,” she said in a statement. “My concern is that pharmacies rushing into this space will move to make a dollar faster rather than make sure they meet every requirement, from ingredient sourcing and facilities to staff training and sterility testing.” Shortly after the new notice was published, a number of telehealth websites were already marketing the six peptides, each for a price of anywhere from $199 to $249 a month.

Activist Dolores Huerta Dies at 96. Here's What To Know About Her Trailblazing Life

Activist Dolores Huerta Dies at 96. Here's What To Know About Her Trailblazing Life

TIME | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Dolores Huerta at the United States Department of Labor in Washington, in 2012. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Dolores Huerta, an internationally renowned activist, community organizer, and political force who co-founded what is now known as the United Farm Workers of America (UFW) alongside Cesar Chavez, died Wednesday at her home in Bakersfield, California. She was 96. Read More: Dolores Huerta, Labor Leader and Civil Rights Icon, Dies at 96 News of Huerta’s death was shared by family members through her Instagram account, though the post did not disclose a cause of death. The New York Times reported that the cause of death was cancer, citing a spokesperson for Huerta. "We know that many of you around the world share in our mourning, and we will be following up on plans to recognize and celebrate her incredible life and legacy," the post said. Huerta dedicated her career to fighting a variety of causes, including labor rights, women's rights and immigrants’ rights. She is also credited with coining the phrase "Yes we can"—"Sí se puede"—which was later adopted by Barack Obama. Read More: TIME100's Most Influential People of 2026: Dolores Huerta In March 2026, Huerta publicly accused Chavez of sexually abusing her decades earlier, breaking a silence she said she had maintained to protect the farmworker movement. Her disclosure accompanied a New York Times investigation into allegations that Chavez sexually abused women and girls. "I think she kind of radiates empowerment not only for herself, but for others," Mario Garcia, a professor of Chicano Studies and History at the University of California, Santa Barbara, told TIME in an interview before Huerta's death. "She's really quite an electrifying person." Indeed, when Huerta received the Presidential Medal of Freedom in 2012, Obama noted that she "does not play." "Without any negotiating experience, Dolores helped lead a worldwide grape boycott that forced growers to agree to some of the country's first farmworker contracts," Obama said during the ceremony. "And ever since she has fought to give more people a seat at the table. 'Don't wait to be invited,' she says. 'Step in there.'" "On a personal note, Dolores was very gracious when I told her I had stolen her slogan, Sí se puede, Yes we can," Obama added. "Knowing her, I'm pleased that she let me off easy." Huerta is presented with a Presidential Medal of Freedom by President Barack Obama on May 29, 2012. Alex Wong—Getty Images Huerta was born on April 10, 1930, in Dawson, New Mexico, to Alicia Chávez and Juan Fernández, who both played a major role in her views on justice. Her father was a miner and farmworker who went on to join the New Mexico state legislature. After her parents' divorce, her mother opened up her own hotel in Stockton, California, and often offered affordable or free rooms to low-wage workers, according to a biography by the Dolores Huerta Foundation (DHF). Huerta often said she became a feminist because of her mother. As a girl, Huerta was a high-achieving student, an active member of several school clubs, and a member of the Girl Scouts. Huerta now has a Scouts patch dedicated to her. In 1945, one of her two brothers was beaten for wearing a zoot suit, an ensemble then popular with Black and Mexican-American men who were often unfairly labeled as delinquents. She has said that her brother's beating further influenced her activism. After Huerta graduated from high school, she obtained a provisional teaching credential at the University of the Pacific’s Stockton College, which later became San Joaquin Delta College, and began teaching at an area elementary school. She also got married and had two children with her husband at the time, Ralph Head. She has repeatedly said that her students, many of whom were children of farmworkers, inspired her activism. "I think the one thing that really kind of hooked me for life was going to the homes of some workers who did not have linoleum or wood on their floor, only dirt," Huerta told MAKERS, a documentary project featuring influential women, in 2012. "And yet, these are people who are working, they're working very hard and you know that this is wrong." Huerta decided to pursue a career in community organizing in 1955 when she met Fred Ross Sr., founder of the Community Service Organization (CSO) and a nationally influential community organizer who began his career fighting for the rights of Dust Bowl-era farmworkers. Dolores Huerta accepts TIME100 Impact Award At a November 2018 TED Talk, Huerta told an audience she found her voice while volunteering at the Stockton chapter of CSO, which she co-founded. She described a day she was volunteering there when she was 25 years old and a disabled farmworker walked in asking if someone could help him apply for disability benefits. She went to a welfare office with the man, but she was not allowed to submit an application on his behalf. Defeated, she returned to CSO, where Ross told her to go back and demand that a supervisor allow the man to submit an application. "I kind of overcame my anxieties and my fears. I went down to the welfare office and I demanded to see the supervisor," Huerta said. "That taught me a lesson. That taught me that I had a voice." Ross taught Huerta to make demands of people, she said, especially public officials. He also instilled the importance of voting. While at CSO, Huerta founded the Agricultural Workers Association, led voter registration drives and met Cesar Chavez in 1955, while he was an organizer with CSO. Chavez would later become CSO's executive director in 1959. Ross died in 1992, but Huerta described Ross as her "spiritual father" for the rest of her life, and the first person to teach her and Chavez community-organizing techniques. In 1962, Huerta and Chavez left CSO to found the National Farm Workers Association, which would later become UFW, a union that continues to advocate for the rights of agricultural workers. They were motivated to found the association after hearing from scores of California farmworkers working under harsh conditions: no benefits, low wages, and no access to bathrooms or water while in the fields. The Bracero Program, established through a 1942 agreement between the U.S. and Mexico, recruited Mexican workers to address wartime agricultural labor shortages and continued until 1964. Chavez and Huerta believed the Bracero Program allowed for farmworker abuses and lobbied to end it. They lobbied against the program, which ended in 1964. Huerta with a union flag that reads 'Viva La Causa', ca. 1970s. Cathy Murphy—Getty Images Huerta and Chavez continued their fight for workers' rights, including leading protests, worker strikes, hunger strikes, and arguably their most famous tactic, national boycotts. Huerta helped organize the nationwide grape boycott that supported the Delano grape strike of 1965-1970. The strike began with Filipino farmworkers affiliated with the Agricultural Workers Organizing Committee; Huerta and Chavez’s National Farm Workers Association subsequently joined them. The Delano Grape Strike and Boycott convinced grocery store chains to stop selling the fruit until industry labor standards were set. Huerta’s boycotts were effective in forcing industries to guarantee better working conditions for their employees. "I think one of the things about when people are oppressed is that they start accepting their condition and they often kind of blame themselves," Huerta said during a 2015 Q&A with the National Portrait Gallery. "Our job was to convince them again that they could make a difference and that they had the power to do it." In 1975, California passed the Agricultural Labor Relations Act, which gave farmworkers the right to unionize. The state credited Huerta as instrumental in establishing those rights. Though Chavez often receives national accolades for the achievements of the farmworker movement, historians say Huerta played an equally essential role through every success—but historically received far less credit. Huerta, along with union organizers, speaks to strawberry workers and supporters gathered at the United Farmworkers Union office in Oxnard, Calif. as they awaited the results of a union representation election in 1999. Monica Almeida—The New York Times/Redux "[Chavez] didn't win you over with his rhetoric, [Chavez] was always soft spoken, but he also radiated strong internal leadership qualities," Garcia said. "[Huerta], by contrast, was always much more verbal, a much better speaker than [Chavez]." In a 2018 interview with TIME, Huerta said she didn't expect recognition. "I think that’s very typical of women," she said. "I had been acculturated to be supportive, to be accommodating, to support men in the work they do. We never think of getting credit or recognition or even taking the power. We didn’t think in those terms." She added that she believed times were changing. "That could make an incredible amount of difference in our world,” she said. “We will never have peace in the world until feminists take power." In March 2026, Huerta disclosed that Chavez had sexually abused her in the 1960s. She alleged that he pressured her into sex on one occasion and raped her on another. Both encounters resulted in pregnancies, she said, and she arranged for the children to be raised by other families. She said she later developed close relationships with them. Huerta said she had remained silent for decades because she feared damaging the movement to which she had devoted her life. Coming forward after a New York Times investigation into allegations that Chavez abused other women and girls, she emphasized that the movement’s achievements belonged to thousands of people. Huerta was a change-maker while also raising 11 children, which gave her kids a front-row seat to her activism. During the five-year Delano grape boycott, Huerta said she and her family encountered violence. "There were times when my home was terrorized in the middle of the night, my windows broken when I'm there alone with my children," she told MAKERS. "We had shotguns and rifles aimed at us." In order to start the farmworkers union, Huerta had to move most of her family—by then she had seven children—to Delano, while leaving two of her children behind in Stockton, California. Huerta credited the women of the movement, including Cesar Chavez's wife, Helen Chavez, and his sisters-in-law, for organizing childcare in Delano. American labor activist and cofounder of the United Farm Workers of America (UFW) Dolores Huerta and her daughters sit outdoors on a tree, Keene, California, mid-1970s. Cathy Murphy—Getty Images "I had a lot of criticism from family and friends for years until huelga [the strike] became famous and now they all like me," Huerta jokingly said during the 2015 National Portrait Gallery Q&A. "But it was difficult. We were pretty much living like the farmworkers." Huerta's family and Chavez's family relied on assistance from a food bank and were organizing the union without a steady source of income of their own. "In the strike, women were always on the picket lines with the children," Huerta said. It was also not lost on Huerta that Chavez and other male activists were not asked about how they managed their important work with their parental duties. "The thing about that is that every single mother in every single working family has that question every single day. Who am I going to leave my children with? Are they going to be safe? Are they going to be protected? That’s something women have to deal with all the time," she told TIME in 2018. Huerta had 14 children, 24 grandchildren, 23 great-grandchildren, and seven great-great-grandchildren, a spokesperson tells TIME. “Dolores was a deeply private person when it came to her family, and while all 14 of her children were part of her life, some parts of that story were never shared publicly,” according to the spokesperson. Over the course of her career, Huerta was arrested more than 20 times. In 1988, she was severely beaten by a police officer in San Francisco at a rally against presidential candidate George H. W. Bush. She was 58 years old at the time, and suffered broken ribs and a ruptured spleen. Despite those types of encounters, Huerta continued to mobilize and fight for causes through her Dolores Huerta Foundation. She established her foundation in 2003 after receiving a $100,000 prize from the Puffin/Nation Prize for Creative Citizenship in 2002. Her youngest daughter, Camila Chávez, helped run the foundation as the executive director. In the course of her career, Huerta was awarded the Presidential Medal of Freedom, the Eleanor Roosevelt Human Rights Award, and the Mexican Government's Ohtli Award. She also received at least nine honorary doctorates from universities across the country. In 2026, TIME named Huerta one of the world’s 100 most influential people, with a tribute by Gloria Steinem recognizing her decades of organizing and her courage in speaking publicly about Chavez. She received the TIME100 Impact Award at the TIME100 Gala in New York City on April 23, 2026. Huerta during the Democratic National Convention at the United Center in Chicago, on Aug. 21, 2024. Victor J. Blue—Bloomberg/Getty Images Huerta endorsed presidential candidates, including speaking at the 2016 Democratic National Convention in support of Hillary Clinton. She was also the subject of a PBS documentary, Dolores, which won a Peabody Award in 2018. "She was not afraid to speak truth to power," Clinton said of her in the Dolores documentary. In July 2018, California Governor Jerry Brown signed a bill declaring April 10 Dolores Huerta Day. "Dolores Huerta deserves proper recognition for her numerous sacrifices in the name of justice and the amelioration of severely inadequate working conditions," the bill’s text said. "Change comes from the bottom," Huerta said during a May 2012 interview. "But you've got to be able to organize those folks at the bottom to be able to get out there and make the changes that they need. And of course, if you don't have organization, then you don't have a democracy."

Dolores Huerta, Labor Leader and Civil Rights Icon, Dies at 96
2026-10-28

Dolores Huerta, Labor Leader and Civil Rights Icon, Dies at 96

TIME | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Cleary is an editor at TIME, based in the London bureau. Dolores Huerta, a celebrated labor Leader and civil rights icon, is pictured here. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Cleary is an editor at TIME, based in the London bureau. Dolores Huerta, who co-founded what became known as the United Farm Workers union to help secure better pay for laborers, has died at age 96. Huerta’s team, in a statement shared through her foundation, said she passed away on Wednesday at home in Bakersfield, California, “surrounded by her close family.” In a separate statement, Huerta's family remarked on the "incredible loss" and said they will "share plans to recognize and celebrate" her legacy at a future date. While a cause of death was not shared, the New York Times, citing family spokesperson Erik Olvera, said cancer was behind her passing. Huerta, who campaigned for the rights of migrant laborers throughout the 1960s and '70s, is credited with coining the rallying cry “Sí, se puede”—“Yes, we can.” The phrase inspired generations and was famously adopted by former President Barack Obama, who presented Huerta with the Presidential Medal of Freedom in 2012. Upon receiving the award, Huerta credited the advice her mother had once given her: "If you see people that need help, you have an obligation to help them, and you shouldn't wait for people to ask." Huerta said that once she had acquired the necessary skills, she honored that obligation by teaching people "how to come together and be able to fight" for their rights. Read More: What To Know About Dolores Huerta's Trailblazing Life “One of the few women lobbyists of her era, [Dolores] became a formidable advocate for people who could not afford one,” reads the obituary shared by Huerta’s foundation. “Her legislative victories included a California measure allowing driver’s license examinations in Spanish, the extension of Aid to Families with Dependent Children to California farmworker families, and disability insurance protections for agricultural workers.” Huerta founded United Farm workers with Cesar Chavez in 1962 and served as its vice president for almost four decades. She worked closely with Chavez until his death in 1993. Earlier this year, Huerta came forward with allegations against Chavez, accusing him of having sexually abused her in the 1960s. “I have kept this secret long enough. My silence ends here,” said Huerta in a statement in March. The abuse led to two pregnancies, Huerta said, explaining that she "chose to keep [the] pregnancies secret and, after the children were born, arranged for them to be raised by other families that could give them stable lives." Huerta said she was driven to share her experiences after an investigation released by the New York Times revealed accusations that Chavez, a long-celebrated icon of the labor movement, groomed and sexually abused multiple girls and women who worked in the movement. Huerta said she had "carried this secret" for so long because she feared speaking out would compromise her life’s work and risk all the progress made for laborers. “The formation of a union was the only vehicle to accomplish and secure those rights and I wasn’t going to let Cesar or anyone else get in the way. I channeled everything I had into advocating on behalf of millions of farmworkers and others who were suffering and deserved equal rights," she explained. In April, Huerta was named on TIME's list of the "100 Most Influential People of 2026." Her long-time friend and fellow activist, Gloria Steinem, who died in September, wrote a tribute acknowledging the recognition. Celebrating Huerta for stepping forward, Steinem said: "We can all learn from the courage and conscience Dolores and others have modeled. The truth about Chavez reminds us to look honestly at the behavior of everyone, including our leaders, and to lend support to those who may be hurt by misused power... Dolores’ impact, like that of many other activists, will continue to guide us, regardless of the hurtful behavior of any one individual." Dolores Clara Huerta (née Fernández) was born on April 10, 1930, in Dawson, New Mexico. She was the only daughter of Juan Fernández and Alicia Chávez. Her parents divorced when she was 3 and she later moved to Stockton, California, with her mother. She went on to study at the University of Pacific’s Delta College in Stockton, now known as San Joaquin Delta College. Huerta married Ralph Head when she was in college. The marriage, out of which came two children, ended in divorce. She had five children with her second husband Ventura Huerta, whose surname she kept after they divorced. When Huerta was in her early 40s, she embarked upon a relationship with Richard Chavez, a brother of Cesar Chavez’s, and they were "life partners" until his passing in 2011. They had four children together, according to the New York Times. Huerta had 14 children, 24 grandchildren, 23 great-grandchildren, and seven great-great-grandchildren, a spokesperson told TIME. “Dolores was a deeply private person when it came to her family, and while all 14 of her children were part of her life, some parts of that story were never shared publicly,” according to the spokesperson. In her decades spent campaigning on behalf of laborers, as well as her work supporting the rights of immigrants and women, Huerta was arrested more than 25 times for participating in, what her foundation describes as, "non-violent civil disobedience activities and strikes." In 1988, when she was 58 years old, Huerta suffered broken ribs and a damaged spleen after being beaten by baton-wielding members of the San Francisco police force during a protest against the policies of presidential candidate George H.W. Bush. Huerta and others filed a civil rights lawsuit in the U.S. District Court in San Francisco after they were injured. Huerta's activism continued, and her foundation notes that "even in her 90s, Dolores could still be found at rallies, walking precincts, speaking to young organizers, and urging people to vote." She has been honored numerous times over the years on account of her contributions to marginalized communities. In 1993, Huerta was the first Latina inducted into the National Women’s Hall of Fame. In the late '90s, former President Bill Clinton presented her with the Eleanor Roosevelt Award for Human Rights. In 2022, she won the Clinton Global Citizen Award, in recognition of her "trailblazing lifetime of public service advocating for the dignity and fundamental human rights of women, children, and working class people worldwide." Huerta is survived by her family and also the foundation that bears her name. In 2003, Huerta returned to California’s Central Valley and started the Dolores Huerta Foundation, which cites its mission as "inspiring and organizing communities to build volunteer organizations empowered to pursue social justice.” The foundation has said it plans to open the "Dolores Huerta Peace and Justice Cultural Center in the Central Valley" in 2027. Huerta is presented with a Presidential Medal of Freedom by President Barack Obama on May 29, 2012. Alex Wong—Getty Images In a moving statement, former President Obama remembered Huerta as one of his heroes. Dolores spent "her life fighting for marginalized communities, even as she herself faced sexism and abuse—including, as she revealed earlier this year, from Cesar Chavez and others within her own movement," Obama said. "She also came up with a pretty good slogan, 'sí, se puede'—yes, we can—that I too often get credit for. But as usual, Dolores got there first." The Congressional Hispanic Caucus remembered Huerta as someone who "defied definition." Referring to her as a "national and international icon," the group said: "Her legacy is woven into the fabric of our history. Her fight for justice will continue to inspire generations. Rest in power, hermana." María Teresa Kumar, the co-founder and president of Voto Latino, an advocacy organization that aims to empower the next generation of Latino voters, celebrated Huerta's influence. “Dolores always saw the value and spirit in young people before the rest of the world was ready to," she said. "Because of her, our community has been empowered to be our own advocates.” Fellow co-founder Rosario Dawson said that Huerta had "centered her life around doing good in this world and around the deep-rooted belief in leaving it better than we found it. It is now up to us to carry her legacy forward." Championing Huerta's community work, California's Gov. Gavin Newsom said: "California—and the United States—would not be what we are today without her resolute spirit, her passion for justice and fairness, and her commitment to those overlooked and forgotten." He recognized Huerta as having "represented the best of us" and said her "fight for gender equity, workers' rights, and civil rights paved the way for generations to come." Former House Speaker Nancy Pelosi referred to Huerta as a "dear friend" and noted how her rallying cry had inspired "generations of Americans to believe in the power of collective action." Former President Joe Biden extended condolences on behalf of himself and his wife, Jill. "For more than seventy years, Dolores fought for working people. She organized in the fields, she walked the picket lines, and she sat across the table from people who never expected to have to negotiate with her," he said. "America is fairer and more decent because of Dolores Huerta." Alongside pictures of herself and Huerta, former Vice President Kamala Harris posted a personal tribute to social media. "I was blessed to know Dolores for many years and proud to organize alongside my dear friend to champion working families and immigrant rights. I will always be grateful for her support and friendship," said Harris. "We will honor her legacy by continuing the fight for the dignity of every person."
