What to Say—and Not Say—to Someone Having Intrusive Thoughts
2026-10-28

What to Say—and Not Say—to Someone Having Intrusive Thoughts

TIME | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Have you ever had a disturbing thought pop into your head out of nowhere—something you'd never consider actually doing? Intrusive thoughts are unwanted thoughts, images, or urges that can feel upsetting, confusing, or wildly out of character. They're more common in people with obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD) but can happen to anyone, and they can involve everything from violence and sex to religion, relationships, and the fear of having done something wrong. Many people are able to brush off the occasional intrusive thought and move on quickly. People with OCD, on the other hand, can get stuck trying to figure out what the thought means—and whether they might act on it. “We stop and we treat that thought as super important and meaningful,” says Alissa Jerud, a clinical psychologist and clinical assistant professor of psychiatry at the University of Pennsylvania. Intrusive thoughts can feel especially disturbing because they often involve someone or something the person cherishes. “We tend to have intrusive thoughts about the things we care most about,” Jerud says. Some of Jerud’s patients have kept their intrusive thoughts to themselves for 20 years before telling her. They might be thinking: “Why am I having these thoughts? What does it say about me? Maybe I’m a monster,” she says. A calm, compassionate response can make it easier to keep talking. “It can help them feel like there’s hope,” Jerud says. “Maybe I don’t have to beat myself up for having these thoughts in the first place.” Here’s what to say if a loved one tells you they’re having intrusive thoughts. Start by thanking your friend for trusting you. Skip responses like “How could you think that?” or “Don’t say that,” which can reinforce the shame they’re already feeling. It’s helpful, too, to make it clear that what they told you won’t affect your relationship: “This doesn’t change how I think about you,” suggests Madeline Thompson Smith, an Atlanta-based licensed professional counselor who specializes in OCD. Then acknowledge how upsetting the thoughts sound: “That sounds really scary to have stuck in your head.” Pay attention to how you sound and look, too. A warm invitation to explain more can feel like judgment if you’re staring intensely or using a sharp tone. If you look startled, acknowledge it instead of hoping they didn’t notice. Kimberly Leonte, a psychologist in Boston who specializes in OCD, suggests wording it like this: “I guess I wasn’t expecting that, but I want to hear more.” When someone you love is distressed by an intrusive thought, reassuring them can feel like the kindest thing to do. But if they keep coming back with the same question, answering it again and again can become part of the OCD cycle. Say your partner keeps asking whether an unwanted thought means they’re dangerous. You reassure them, they feel better, and you both get back to your evening. Until another question arrives: But what if you’re wrong? What if they didn’t explain the thought accurately enough? Next thing you know, they’re asking for reassurance again. A single reassuring response isn’t necessarily a problem, Thompson Smith says. The concern is when seeking reassurance becomes a compulsion—a behavior someone feels driven to repeat to relieve their distress. Each answer briefly helps them feel better, but it leaves them needing another answer when the doubt returns. Instead of debating what the thought means, acknowledge how they’re feeling and express confidence in their ability to get through the discomfort. Thompson Smith suggests: “I know this feels really uncomfortable right now. I’m not going to help you go down the rabbit hole, but I’m happy to sit with you while it’s hard.” If reassurance has become a routine between you, talk about changing it together, ideally with guidance from their therapist. Abruptly refusing to answer can leave your loved one wondering what happened. “It’s tough if the person’s used to getting reassurance, and you just stop giving it and they don’t know why,” Leonte says. Instead, discuss how you’ll respond the next time they ask—and make sure you both understand why you’re trying something different. Meanwhile, aim to avoid suggestions like “Just stop thinking about it” or “Replace it with a happy thought.” Besides being considerably easier said than done, trying to force an intrusive thought away can keep someone preoccupied with whether it’s gone yet. Find out what your loved one wants from the conversation. They might be seeking support in taking a step toward getting mental-health help, or they may simply want you to know what they’ve been going through. “Do you want me to just listen, or do you want help figuring out what to do next?” Thompson Smith suggests asking. If this is the first time they’ve said their intrusive thoughts out loud, they might not be ready to discuss next steps. If they’re already in therapy, ask how you can support what they’re learning: “What would your therapist recommend trying right now?” Leonte suggests asking. If they’re interested in learning more about intrusive thoughts but feel overwhelmed, you could offer to read about them together. Sometimes, they simply want someone to listen. Your loved one might be telling you because “they just don’t want to be alone in this, and you can help them hold it,” Thompson Smith says. Or they may need to know “you’re not gonna judge me or people aren’t gonna run away if I start trying to be open and get help.” If unwanted thoughts are causing significant distress or interfering with daily life, offer to help your loved one find a therapist. Keep it low-pressure: “If you ever want me to help you find a therapist, I’d be happy to do so,” Jerud suggests saying. For OCD, look for a therapist trained in exposure and response prevention, or ERP, Jerud suggests. It helps people learn to tolerate intrusive thoughts without turning to rituals like repeated reassurance. The International OCD Foundation maintains a directory to help people find one. There’s also an important distinction between an unwanted thought about harm and wanting or intending to act. Don’t assume that every disturbing thought is benign, Thompson Smith says. If you’re unsure what someone means, ask calmly and directly: “Is this a thought that scares you, or something you want to do?” If they describe wanting to act, ask about plans and help them get professional support right away. After someone opens up about their intrusive thoughts, keep treating them like you always have. Send the ridiculous meme, ask whether they’re still coming to dinner, and keep including them in the things you usually do together. People often fear that sharing their intrusive thoughts will change how others see them, Thompson Smith says. Pulling away—or asking about their mental health every time you talk—can leave them wishing they’d kept quiet. You might get frustrated or struggle to find the right words, Leonte says. If you react in a way you regret, apologize and let them know you’re still there for them. “No one’s perfect,” she says. “The goal isn’t to be perfect, it’s just to be supportive.”

The solution was, of course, full of holes, some of them quite serious, just not obvious. The holes were in the edge cases and how it handles and recovers from errors. These were exactly the kinds of mistakes I would expect a junior level developer to make (in at least one case I am thinking of, the task shouldn't have even been handed to someone so inexperienced, but the people making that decision didn't really know how advanced the task was and anyway the dev wanted to get real experience). So, our process worked because we had senior level human code review, which caught these. But the real risk here is that without such experienced senior level people to do that review this would have seemed like a "slam dunk" where AI saved a whole lot of time and eliminated the need for expensive veterans on the team (only to fail catastrophically in production as soon as something hiccupped). News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Why There’s a Real Risk of a Replay of the 2020 Election Come Tuesday
2026-10-29

Why There’s a Real Risk of a Replay of the 2020 Election Come Tuesday

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
This is Executive Dysfunction, a newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Click here to receive it in your inbox each week. The midterm elections are now underway across the country and as voters decide who should hold the levers of power in Washington, all eyes are on President Donald Trump. He’s spent the last decade baselessly questioning the integrity of U.S. elections—despite plenty of evidence finding they are safe and secure—with things coming to a violent head on Jan. 6, 2021. This year, as more and more polling is suggesting Democrats will sweep Republicans, even in solidly red districts, it raises the question: Will the president accept the results of critical House and Senate races, or won’t he? “I will, if they’re honest elections,” Trump ominously responded when asked this very question on Tuesday. Since Trump began his second term, he’s been laying the groundwork to try to nationalize U.S. elections and go after “voter fraud” through executive action and lawsuits against states, though most of these efforts have been struck down in court. Yet, the Justice Department has not given up. In fact, in recent weeks the agency filed at least two more dubious election lawsuits against California and Alaska, plus a series of cases against immigrants for alleged noncitizen voting. The legal challenges suggest the administration is planning ahead this time around, planting lawsuits that might create a foothold in the courts for the GOP to seize on if the midterms don’t end up going their way. To Charles Stewart III, a political scientist and professor at MIT who has spent decades studying American congressional politics, elections, and political development, the current situation created by Trump is a test of America’s democracy. “Not primarily because of fundamental shortcomings in American election administration, but because political actors associated with the Administration will attempt to delegitimize or overturn its work,” he wrote in a newly published paper, “The Day After: Threats and Guardrails in the 2026 Election.” Stewart believes that Trump’s efforts to thwart this year’s midterms should be viewed in three distinct phases: Before Election Day, during the vote counting and certification process, and when the new Congress convenes on Jan. 3, 2027. In practice, this looks like Trump beginning to seed baseless claims that losing Republicans can make after Election Day: mail ballots are suspect, voter rolls are contaminated with noncitizens, slow ballot counts are evidence of fraud, and any Democratic victories in close races shouldn’t be accepted. After Election Day, the lawsuits begin, harkening back to the days and weeks following Trump’s 2020 loss to Joe Biden, and finally things might culminate at the Capitol, where lawmakers could be pressured not to certify election winners. I spoke with Stewart to understand how this framework can help us see important red flags in the hours immediately following Election Day, plus the chances that Trump incites yet another angry mob to take over the Capitol. And is there any silver lining to hold on to? Here’s our conversation, lightly edited and condensed for clarity. Shirin Ali: In your paper, you drill down on the president’s efforts to undermine the midterms into three flashpoints: Before Election Day, during the vote counting and certification process, and when the new Congress convenes. Can you explain what each of these are? Charles Stewart III: The ideas behind those flashpoints come from what we’ve seen the Trump administration working on in the second term: two executive orders, activities at the Justice Department and Homeland Security, actions that are beginning to nibble around the edges of the election administration process. Particularly before Election Day, putting pressures on mail voting, highlighted by the executive order from this past March that basically directed the U.S. Postal Service to create a new series of procedures for handling mail voting, marrying that together with directives to DHS to create these lists of citizens. The second flashpoint has to do with pressures on election administration, especially election administrators and the institutions themselves. There, I think the pressures go beyond even what the administration has been doing. Certainly, a lot of the activity of the administration has been focused on the possibility that if election officials don’t do what the administration wants, they will not only bring civil complaints against them, but they may very well bring criminal complaints against them. Then after Election Day, that’s where we’re thinking about getting the final count in. We shift into the formal process of canvassing the vote, certifying it, and making sure that we’ve named the winners. I’m thinking broadly about a potential replay of what happened after the 2020 election, where the Trump campaign lodged a number of lawsuits in battleground states like Wisconsin and Pennsylvania. I’m still anticipating, even more now, that coming true. The final flashpoint is what happens when the 120th Congress convenes on Jan. 3, 2027, particularly the House. And there the question is whether there will be efforts to bar the seating of senators or members of the House who’ve been elected, specifically Democrats who would be the pivotal members of the two chambers that would flip control of the chamber to the Democrats. There’s another obvious parallel with 2020, when there were efforts to deny the certification of the presidential election. How do the Justice Department’s lawsuits against California and Alaska fit into this? Those are kind of jumping the gun on the timeline that I had suspected, because they’re really almost preempting post-election challenges and making them pre-election challenges. About a week ago I was saying, “Well, they’ve filed this lawsuit against California, it’s getting kind of close to the election. If there’s just California, it’s kind of a weak rule in terms of applying formal pressure on the states.” Then came Alaska, and this one makes me wonder what else the DOJ is going to do in the next week to bring early lawsuits against states and kind of do a shot across the bow. The California lawsuit is the sort of thing that we that we’ve long expected the Trump administration to do, and in fact in the first administration, there were rumors that they were trying to put together a national voter database and merge it with immigration records to begin to go after states for having noncitizens on the rolls. This has been a long dream of the administration and people associated with the administration, and it’s taken them basically a decade in order to act on it. It doesn’t look like they’ve done a particularly sophisticated job, both in terms of the actual job of database matching or on the legal side. The Alaska case seems to be the most frivolous election challenge that I can imagine, and in fact it’s kind of orthogonal. It’s unrelated to the things I had anticipated, because the lawsuit they’re bringing is the sort of lawsuit that you would expect a candidate to bring. Really, it’s up to Sen. Sullivan to bring these challenges under Alaska law and then potentially to bring a contest to the U.S. Senate. I only have to believe that the Alaska case is the classic case of pleasing an audience of one; Donald Trump wanted it and he got it. There’s just no way in the world this is going to go forward. It’s sort of like going after James Comey or going after the members of Congress who reminded service members they didn’t have to follow illegal orders. It’s really performative, to please the boss, and hard to take seriously, except the degree to which it’s predictive of what we’re likely to see after the election: the federal government putting the thumb on the scales of candidate-oriented post-election litigation. Next week, immediately after Election Day is over, what are you going to be watching for? I think there will be two things. One will be legitimate, and the other will be illegitimate. The legitimate thing is that we may very well see more losing candidates contest their elections and begin taking legal actions to try to save evidence, within the normal contest laws. Some of it may very well be performative to garner support from the administration or local political actors, but those are the ill-fated sorts of things that there’s a legal framework for doing. Plus there’s a legal framework for judges throwing these cases out, right? And that’s the way it should happen. What I think we need to be prepared for, and I don’t think it’s going to be widespread, are candidates supported by the administration going into court to attempt to impound voting machines and ballots and to remove them from where they’re normally housed. Basically, a repeat of the Fulton County event a year ago where the FBI removed ballots. Those are the things I think we should anticipate, and I think that the Department of Justice may very well take matters into their own hands that are quasi-independent of the candidates themselves. For instance, the Alaska lawsuit was just something that the DOJ did because the president wanted it. That’s a disturbing thing that I think we need to be prepared for after the election because we’ve already seen it happen before the election. That is the DOJ just going into places they don’t like and filing lawsuits. In your paper, you wrote, “The greatest danger is not that even a few challenges will succeed; it is that a small number of delayed certifications could matter greatly if control of either chamber is in doubt.” What did you mean by that? If you think about Jan. 6, 2021, in Congress and leading up to that, there was a whole lot of noise in the political system about whether the vice president could refuse to accept electors and whether Congress could impose their own view on what state electors they should accept, and all those sorts of things. In retrospect, traditional constitutional election lawyers had it right all along in terms of the law. The correct person was elected president, but the political ramifications of that whole episode and Jan. 6 itself were just catastrophic for American politics and certainly transformative in many ways. The law held, the right guy was elected president, but antidemocratic elements were energized and we had a riot at the Capitol. A social movement has arisen in the country that makes millions of dollars based on stoking a view that elections in America are illegitimate. It’s an extreme view, and a minority view even within the Republican Party at that level of extremity, but it’s really been politically consequential over the last six years. I think we could be in an Act 2 of that, and that’s what I’m getting at here. Imagine that this was a decade ago and we’re coming into a Congress where the Democrats were going to take over the chamber and have about a 20-seat lead. You wouldn’t have imagined anything major to come out of any contest, any seat. There might have been a couple of contests and it might have been highly politicized, but they would have been handled in the regular order. Imagine right now if Democrats have that same margin of success. I think we have to assume there will be at least a few members of the House on the Republican side who will make a lot of hay around refusing their being seated, refusing to allow them to vote for the speaker, pressuring the clerk not to accept their certificates of election, those sorts of things. All of those would be illegitimate. The rules of the House are very clear about the clerk of the House accepting the properly given certificates of election for members of the House for the purpose of the roll-call vote at the beginning and electing the speaker. There is a very prescribed way of handling contests in the House of Representatives, and there is no legitimate, legal way for the current Congress to be the judge of those elections. Now, one of the things I think will stoke the political fires, though, is the investigations into any contest that any member would want to bring in the 119th Congress. There will be pressures in the current Congress for House Speaker Mike Johnson to highly politicize those investigations, use them as opportunities to shine a spotlight on California and other states that are not cooperating with the administration over noncitizens on the rolls, for instance. Or on states with high levels of mail voting. It’s going to unleash this political theater in hopes of blowing up regular procedures that are very clear, much more clear than the Electoral Count Act was in 2020, into something that looks like a rejection of this election. And the closer it is, the higher the stakes and the higher the temperature. Despite having serious concerns about what the Trump administration will do in the event Republicans lose control of Congress, you’re optimistic that the 2026 midterms will be resilient enough to withstand a Trump bashing. Why? It starts with the decentralized nature of election administration. When I’ve been asked about whether the Trump administration is going to be successful in overthrowing this election, I’ve generally responded with two words: Feckless—they’ve not been very good at what they’ve done; and capacity—there are just not enough lawyers left in the Department of Justice, or enough immigration agents, there’s not enough of anything to take over the American electoral system. If it’s a matter of mobilizing the federal government, there are more local election officials running elections than there’s the capacity of the Trump administration to go after enough of them. That’s decentralization. The second thing is that we continue to see evidence that the facts-based institutions of our electoral system continue to hold. That includes state and local election officials who are nothing if not careful about following the law. And there are the county boards of elections; we’ve seen general election boards stand by the canvassed results. There have been several states that have passed laws over the last few years reinforcing the notion that the certifications of elections at the local level are ministerial and that the proper place for conducting contests, if something is seen to be amiss, is in the court system, not in the election boards. Finally, I think we continue to see the courts holding to what the law is, holding to the precedents that have been established in the past about what do you do when someone contests the results of an election. There’s established law there, and trial courts have, on the whole, held, and when they haven’t, they’ve been admonished or overturned on appeal. It would be nice if we didn’t have to say, “Well, at the end of the day, we’re relying on the Alaska Supreme Court.” It would be much better if it didn’t have to get that far, but we do still have a robust and independent judiciary that has been willing to stand up to nonsense. We hope you learned a thing or two from this edition of Executive Dysfunction, and if you enjoyed reading it, please consider supporting our legal journalism by becoming a Slate Plus member! In this week’s episode of Amicus, Dahlia Lithwick weighs the million-dollar question: To impeach or not to impeach? If Democrats gain control of the House and Senate, this will be one of the first issues they will need to grapple with, and Frank Bowman, law professor and former federal and state prosecutor, tells Dahlia that Trump’s actions are definitely impeachable. However, Congress is likely to face obstacles that may just be insurmountable. In the Amicus bonus episode, Dahlia is joined by Madiba Dennie of Balls and Strikes to discuss Supreme Court Justice Elena Kagan’s biting remarks about originalism, plus a flash of positive court news that includes a rejection of the Trump administration’s ability to harm transgender inmates in an emergency. States that have struggled to enforce strict abortion laws have found a new avenue to force their agenda, and it focuses on controlling speech. Mary Ziegler, law professor at the University of California, Davis, writes in Slate about a new frontier around reproductive rights that’s brewing for after the midterms. Ohio Republican candidate for governor Vivek Ramaswamy’s decision to run an ad accusing his Democratic opponent Amy Acton of lying about sexual abuse she endured at the hands of her stepfather gets at a dark and damning trend: We don’t believe children. Dale Margolin Cecka, associate professor of law at Albany Law School, explains in Slate the statistics around child sexual abuse and how Ramaswamy turned Acton’s vulnerability into a campaign tactic. Immigration is very much on the ballot this midterm election cycle, and Chika Okafor, economist and assistant professor of law at Northwestern University, conducted a new study that found voters are very much in control of what law enforcement policies get adopted and which don’t. In Slate, he argues that on Election Day, voters will get to directly decide whether more Americans will be killed by federal immigration agents. Thank you for reading Executive Dysfunction! My State’s Democratic Candidate Has Zero Rizz. He Could Decide Control of the Senate. Good!
2026-10-29

My State’s Democratic Candidate Has Zero Rizz. He Could Decide Control of the Senate. Good!

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Less than half an hour into the first debate between the two candidates for New Hampshire’s open Senate seat, the moderator put his foot in his mouth. “Do you think that’s important, Chris Sununu?” George Epstein, of the Conway Daily Sun, tried to ask one candidate, before immediately course-correcting: “Excuse me—Chris Sununu is definitely the wrong name to use.” It was a mistake that said more about the race than any of the candidates’ answers. Yes, John Sununu, Republican, is running against Chris Pappas, Democrat. But Epstein’s error wasn’t an unfortunate mashup of the two candidates’ names: It was a reference to John’s younger—and significantly more popular—brother, Chris Sununu, also the state’s most recent former governor. (John Sununu has served on New Hampshire’s behalf in both the U.S. House and Senate, but he’d been out of office—and out of politics—for almost 20 years, until he joined the race this year.) If you’ve read anything about the New Hampshire Senate race, you might have heard that this is the one where the Republican is trying to ride his brother’s coattails to flipping a seat. This is reflected in his campaign’s perhaps oversimplified yard signs, the majority of which carry only two words “Sununu, Senator,” prompting plenty of residents to wonder if he’s deliberately skipping listing his first name. Otherwise, on paper, the New Hampshire Senate race has been a bit of a sleeper, despite the fact that it’s a competitive contest in an every-seat-counts struggle for control of Congress’ upper chamber. There are plenty of reasons for that: The September primaries were extremely boring compared to other Senate primaries that demanded national headlines. And unlike other primaries that seemed to point toward where the Democratic Party might be heading in 2028, Pappas’ win hardly felt like a bellwether. He did face a challenger from the left, but Karishma Manzur never put up numbers that made her a real threat, and Pappas beat her handily. Sununu did the same with his further-right challenger, Scott Brown, the former senator who had previously (and briefly) represented Massachusetts. That brought Pappas and Sununu to face off against one another in a Senate race that feels almost like it could be happening back in 2012, with classic establishment candidates from each party promising Granite Staters that they’re the ones who will have New Hampshire’s back in Washington. By many measures, it should be Pappas’ race. New Hampshire may be purple, but it’s reliably voted Democrat for president every year since 2000, and the delegation it sends to D.C. has been blue over the past several cycles too. Despite a bit of panic earlier this fall (“New Hampshire Democrats Sound an Alarm: We’re Losing” blared a New York Times headline in September), Pappas has pulled ahead in polling in recent weeks. But I have become particularly interested in New Hampshire’s Senate race, in large part because I live here—I moved a couple of weeks after Trump retook the presidency, soon enough that my town hall was still displaying the 2024 vote count, when 800 residents went for Kamala Harris and 792 went for Donald Trump. And I have come to think of this race not as a sleeper, but as the flip side of the coin to whatever happens with Abdul El-Sayed’s bid for Michigan’s seat or James Talarico’s impressive attempt to flip Texas. This is a race of establishment candidates in a moment when both the Republican establishment and the Democratic establishment are beleaguered. I wanted to find out what the establishment version of either political party even looks like these days, and whether the most boring Democrat in a midterms year of stars might have something to teach the rest of his party. I also needed to understand whether anyone actually votes a split ticket in our wildly polarized times. Given my own progressive tendencies, I came away surprised by how much close observation of the contest changed my mind. It’s not that this race feels like it’s happening specifically in 2012, it’s just that it has a retro feeling about it, particularly thanks to the candidates themselves. Jason Barabas, a government professor and director of the Rockefeller Center for Public Policy at Dartmouth College, put it succinctly: “You have the first open Senate race since 2010, but you have two incumbents squaring off.” Chris Pappas served in the House of Representatives for four terms before the Dems nominated him for Senate this year, and Sununu, a former congressman and senator, comes from the state’s biggest political dynasty. (It’s not just John Sununu’s brother Chris—the boys’ father, also John, also served as governor of the state, and as chief of staff for George H. W. Bush, with a memorable legacy.) “If his name wasn’t Sununu, I don’t think he’d be in the position he’s in,” Nicholas Germana, a government professor at Keene State University, told me. Germana knows what he’s talking about not only because he studies government (primarily German politics, not American ones), but because, like so many New Hampshirites, he serves in the state government himself. New Hampshire’s House of Representatives has 400 members (though apparently no one sits in seat No. 13), making it the second biggest legislative body in the country, just after the U.S. House, and one of the largest governing bodies in the world. This is particularly silly when you consider the small size of the state—with under 1.5 million residents, it’s split into just two congressional districts. But it’s also a huge source of pride and a cause of political engagement here. While reporting this story, multiple state reps reminded me that New Hampshire was the first state to declare independence from England (six months before the country did), and even if the state is losing its first-in-the-nation primary status (Iowa was always a caucus), being demoted from second to third (South Carolina and Nevada are moving up for Democrats) in this upcoming cycle, New Hampshire will still take its task extremely seriously. Is it people’s political involvement that has given the state its purple nature, with a push and pull back and forth between the parties? Not really. Independents outnumber registered Republicans and Democrats. For years now, the state government has been red—and currently, it’s a united Republican governorship, Senate, and House—while the state votes blue for president and sends a full blue delegation to D.C. Which means that people are splitting their tickets: red for the state, blue for the country. “That’s something we talk about all the time—marvel at, frankly—is how much ticket splitting there is,” Germana told me. That’s partly because you have a much higher likelihood of actually knowing your House representative, which might get you to vote across party lines. But it’s also because there’s some cultural state pride in “balancing things out,” even in these times. New Hampshire had one of the highest rates of ticket splitters in the 2022 midterms, Barabas told me, and this year, 13 percent of voters have already declared a preference for splitting their ticket. (The likely way this will lean is to send Pappas to Washington while the current governor, Kelly Ayotte, stays in power.) But the times are making it harder and harder to do that. These midterms are seeing an active effort from Democrats across the state as they respond to some of the more extreme moves made by the united Republican state government. “Republicans in the state had been able to maintain a brand identity that is different than the national Republican Party—rooted in that traditional view of what a Yankee Republican used to be,” Germana told me. But as the Republican agenda has lurched right, that distance has evaporated. In recent sessions, the GOP here has passed culture-war legislation like banning trans athletes from sports. They’ve also taken more aggressive steps to throttle public schools, including voting in a school voucher program that has activated plenty of independent voters. These days, there’s “no daylight between the Republican brand in the state and the Republican Party nationally,” Germana told me, though it’s worth keeping in mind that Germana is not just an observer—he’s technically a member of the opposition party. All of this makes Sununu’s bid to flip a Senate seat a tough effort, and that’s before you take into account his weaknesses as a candidate. While Sununu says he’s not at all trying to fool anyone into thinking he’s Chris, multiple people told me they only realized which brother was running when the primary came around in September. (Their reaction was not positive—Chris had a lot of cross-party appeal thanks to being “a phenomenal retail politician,” Germana said, which John lacks.) John Sununu’s been out of politics since he lost to Jeanne Shaheen in 2008, and it’s Shaheen he’s looking to replace now. While his absence from politics affords him a certain distance from Trump, when he lost his Senate seat, Sununu resorted to a classic move for the establishment and politicians of both parties: He went into lobbying, something the Pappas campaign has made hay of in both ads and debates. John Sununu was a Never-Trump Republican who supported John Kasich in 2016 and Nikki Haley in 2024. And yet, he finds himself in a difficult position this race, as D.C. Republicans personally recruited him to run, after failing to get his brother to do so. It feels a bit like he’s trying to run this race as if he could be New Hampshire’s very own Susan Collins, a Republican, sure, but a reasonable one who would hold his party to account. The problem is, Donald Trump endorsed him during his primary race, and here, like everywhere, Trump’s popularity is plummeting. Recent polling found that the number of Granite Staters who approve of the president was the lowest ever—just 19 percent said they approved of Trump in September of this year (compared to the 41 percent who approved of Trump in 2018, during his first in-office midterms). People are mad about tariffs and the Iran war just like they are everywhere—but New Hampshirites get to be extra mad because we share a border with Canada and are heading into winter, where fuel costs will matter more. All in all, Collins and Sununu seem to be the last individuals trying to claim that it’s possible to be something of a New England Republican—conservative on fiscal issues while being socially liberal—as Trump’s presence and his overwhelming grip on the Republican Party make the viability of that brand more and more difficult. But even Collins is struggling with this message this cycle, and Sununu is not campaigning in the relentless way she is in neighboring Maine. “Sununu is running a low-key campaign with almost no public rallies, few appearances from Republican surrogates, and limited engagement with national media,” the Dispatch reported recently, and he dropped out of what was supposed to be the final debate. I myself tried to engage with his campaign in multiple ways—attempting to RSVP to a meet-and-greet I was told was closed to the press, emailing the campaign, and even eventually texting his campaign spokesman—all to no avail. The main action seems to be in the form of the millions of dollars pouring into the state, where the GOP does have a clear advantage. (In early October, Republicans had spent $39 million compared to Dems’ $23 million, though both campaigns said they have about $14 million on hand for the final few weeks.) The latest ad features Sununu trying to distance himself from Trump, repeating his line that he’s “never met the man.” Pappas is doing the exact opposite of Sununu. He’s been crisscrossing the state (well, driving up and down—you can’t really drive across in New Hampshire) with bigwig Democrats who are happy to add a stop here in anticipation of 2028. Raphael Warnock, Pete Buttigieg, and Gavin Newsom all came through, and late this month, Pappas was joined by Kamala Harris herself. Which is part of what has gotten Pappas into trouble with members of the left flank of his party: Donald Trump may be toxic, but the Democrats are also dealing with their own intra-party feud. Manzur, his primary challenger, took aim at Pappas’ establishment connections and big money—her signs said “Powered by People, Not Billionaires.” And like Abdul El-Sayed, she tried to make her weak fundraising (in the low hundreds of thousands, not millions) an asset rather than a liability. It didn’t work—Pappas beat her by a ratio of just about 2-to-1—but it doesn’t mean her messaging didn’t land somewhere. I spoke with a group of students at Colby-Sawyer College in New London who were all required by a class to attend a political event on campus, and when I asked what they thought of Pappas, they all groaned and told me he was a “corporate Dem”—basically as bad as Sununu, who they didn’t want to vote for either, mind you. Several were still deciding whether they’d bother to change their state registration to vote here this fall (one knew she wouldn’t because she was “excited to vote against Susan Collins”). College students will be college students, but they weren’t the only ones I heard lamenting that Pappas might not have what it takes. An AARP poll that found Pappas has the edge also showed that both candidates have underwater favorability ratings. And I have to admit, for a long time, I felt the same way. On primary day, I was personally conflicted about who I’d vote for. (I’m a registered Democrat.) Ultimately, I watched about 30 minutes of the debate between Pappas and Manzur, and decided that Manzur wouldn’t have a chance of making it out of our purple state. I pulled the ballot for Pappas, but really it was a vote for pragmatism, driven by concerns over the ultimate balance of power in the Senate. Since then, though, I’ve been following him much more closely, and I’ve come to feel differently about my pragmatism vote. That’s largely thanks to the actual campaign that Pappas has knit together—one that has closed the initial gap he faced and looks solid going into November. At one stop I watched him tour a small manufacturing plant, nothing bright or flashy, but Pappas was the picture of politeness, gamely asking questions about how they’d gotten a competitive edge in the niche grips they made. The visit demonstrated what a layup Dems are getting this time around: Tariffs causing this small business problems—check. The war in Iran increasing expenses—check. Healthcare premiums going up—check. Pappas, whose family has long owned the Puritan, an event and conference space with a restaurant that claims to have invented chicken fingers, was able to engage in the minutiae of their business, from a place of true empathy. An on-floor engineer remarked that they sell their scrap metal, and the price has skyrocketed—something they take as a sure indication that the economy is going down the tubes. “Just like when peanut butter sales go up,” the chicken-finger heir nodded solemnly. At that stop, I spoke with an employee who told me she’s never been into politics herself, but that she’s estranged from both her parents on account of Trump. She stopped talking with her mother the day Roe v. Wade was overturned, she said—she couldn’t take it anymore, and she wants to set an example for her son about how people should behave. Her story was specific, but the distaste for Trump was second only to “affordability” on the trail, and luckily for Democrats this cycle, the two are intertwined now. You can’t go to a campaign event in New Hampshire without running into at least a few state reps (or candidates for state rep), and many of these people told me that they’re hearing from a lot of independents who are fed up with Trump and prices and are voting blue, and even a few registered Republicans here and there. Many offered praise for Pappas, who is skilled at listening to all sides of an issue and takes his representation of a mixed district seriously, they said—he’s made it easy for right-leaning folks to switch over. “He’s a guy who knows how to win a purple district,” Germana had told me, and he’s calibrated his public persona and strategy around that. One thing that’s stood out to me is the way Pappas seems to actually decenter himself in these settings, which you don’t need me to tell you is unusual for a politician. If elected, he will be the first openly gay senator in American history. This is not something he ever brings up himself, which, sure, maybe is a nod to the fact that identity politics is truly dead, but I think is more of a representation of his style as a legislator. Also: You’d think the man whose family claims they invented chicken fingers, of all things—what is more American and universally beloved than a chicken finger—would toot that at least a little bit on the campaign trail. But it’s all pretty straight “as a family business owner, I understand the struggle” type stuff. (On a recent visit to the Puritan Backroom, I was struck by two things: how quintessentially New Hampshire it was, which I’ve realized is my code for “caters to Trump voters,” and how delightfully large their Puritan Backroom–branded sauces are—Pappas should be running on his record of ranch portion sizes.) He’s a politician who has done things methodically, which in this day and age means mainly that he paid his dues in both the New Hampshire House and the U.S. House, along with a stint on the state’s executive council, before he went for a bigger office. In his mid-40s, he seems young for the Senate, but that’s just because he has a boyish look and our standards for how old lawmakers should reasonably be are broken. He has a stick-to-the-message-ness that can make him seem rehearsed—or can be interpreted as discipline from someone who is taking his race dead seriously. At a canvass kickoff event on Oct. 17, the one where Gavin Newsom joined the New Hampshire delegates running for office, I heard more practical praise for Pappas. A state House rep who described herself as far more progressive than he is explained why she voted for him in the primary over Manzur: Pappas pledged to endorse his opponent if he was bested, and Manzur did not. This was not the moment for such games, the rep had concluded. There was a couple who had come up from Massachusetts to canvass who constituted the first real Chris Pappas fans I met in the wild (“I’ve always liked him,” the woman told me, with an almost mothering quality). OK, fine, it wasn’t really the wild—these were people who were giving up their Saturday to knock on doors for Democrats, and would most likely end up at a “No Kings” rally later in the afternoon. Just two days before, the Pappas campaign had announced a “Country Before Party” steering committee of more than 50 Republicans and independents who were endorsing Pappas for Senate. The big coup on that list was Don Bolduc, the Republican who has been defeated for that exact Senate seat in the previous cycle. “I think that one was kind of surprising, to tell you the truth, and that can be quite powerful,” Barabas, the Dartmouth professor, told me. At the canvass launch, Pappas attributed Bolduc’s endorsement to his military experience, and his recognition of the nightmare the military is currently facing, led by Pete Hegseth into the Iran war. It set Pappas up perfectly for his line that “everyone is welcome on this team: progressives, moderates, independents, Republicans.” It was this plain appeal that made me start to realize what feels good—almost calming—about Chris Pappas’ campaign. There was something fundamentally decent going on here—a representative who truly seemed to understand that the job of an elected official is to represent all of his constituents, not just the ones who agree with him. One of the most memorable pieces of political journalism I’ve read this midterms cycle was a New York Times Magazine feature from Robert Draper that reported out, via interviews with legislators in both national houses, why Congress is so broken. It turned the entire idea of the midterms on its head for me: “On Nov. 3, American voters will decide which party controls the House and the Senate for the remainder of Trump’s presidency. In theory, at least, the outcome of their decision should be enormously consequential.” In practice, though, Draper is not sure how much any of it really matters, as he goes on to outline exactly how much power the legislative branch has ceded during the mind-bending times of President Donald Trump. There are many reasons for that, including Trump’s astonishing willingness to break norms (see: setting tariffs, something Congress is supposed to have control over). But Trump is also the avatar for how our broader politics have broken over the past decade-plus. Members from both parties explained that the quality of those who choose to join the government has declined, by which they mean that they’re all narcissists now. “What you see now are too many people who come in viewing Congress as their green screen,” David Joyce, a Republican from Ohio, told Draper. “And they’re going to make a name for themselves and don’t give a damn about anything else.” I’m not trying to suggest that the old Congress was all sunshine and daisies. Bogged down by our resolutely two-party system, we Americans can end up fetishizing Washington bipartisanship (that is ultimately out of step with what most regular people want—there’s a reason both sides are turning on the establishment this cycle). But even compromise is getting a bad name these days. Work too hard to actually get things done, and you might end up primaried by an inexperienced challenger like Manzur who may have plenty of good ideas, but no ability to even make it to D.C. to try them. It’s not that Congress should be populated by only normies, but it’s good to have a solid bloc in the mix. They aren’t exciting, but they’re a decent bulwark against the kind of “exciting” we’ve been living through, with brief interruptions, for a decade now. In the absence of a Congress that passes legislation, we have a Congress full of grandstanders. Forgive me for my Pollyannaishness, but as I’ve tried to figure out what is happening in my own state, why this tight race isn’t getting as much attention as I think it should, I came to a pretty simple conclusion. Chris Pappas is running to legislate. In 2026, that’s pretty boring. But I found myself convinced by his pitch—that he is actually going to Washington to attempt to solve our problems, even if it means reaching across the aisle or compromising in favor of getting something, anything, done. He won’t do it with a lot of fanfare, and he certainly won’t do it in a tight black V-neck. But he’ll certainly try. Let’s just hope it’s still possible. How to Lose Your Party a Senate Seat: Alaska Edition
2026-10-28

How to Lose Your Party a Senate Seat: Alaska Edition

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Next week’s U.S. Senate election in Alaska could come down to one letter. Under the state’s unique balloting system, three candidates are running to unseat Sen. Dan S. Sullivan: Democratic former Rep. Mary Peltola, a bandana-wearing gadfly named Gerald Heikes, and … another Dan Sullivan. That other Dan Sullivan, whose middle initial is J, is a retired teacher who has previously voted Democrat, so his presence in the race quickly attracted suspicion. On Wednesday, the New York Times officially linked Dan J. Sullivan’s candidacy to Democrats, reporting that Peltola aides helped recruit him in a bid to siphon votes from the incumbent Republican. The subterfuge appears to be a blatant case of Democrats stooping to dirty tricks in a competitive race that will help decide which party controls the Senate. Yet as seamy as those tactics may be, they still don’t justify the Trump administration’s attempts to use the power of the federal government to help the original Dan Sullivan (the one who’s already a Republican senator, that is) win reelection. So like a salmon swimming up an icy Alaskan stream, let’s dive in. Last year, Peltola allies started looking for Alaskans named Dan Sullivan who might be open to running for Senate. The reported scheme, referred to internally as “the ballot composition project,” involved a Democratic operative named Jerid Kurtz clandestinely meeting with potential contenders. But Kurtz’s skullduggery appears to have paid off. In the small fishing village of Petersburg, he found Dan J. Sullivan, who apparently agreed to run if it would help Peltola win. (Dan J. Sullivan has previously denied any associations with the Peltola campaign, which in turn continues to deny any involvement with his candidacy.) Some of the Times’ information came from Elisa Rios, Peltola’s former campaign manager, who spoke to the paper after she was fired in August. Rios first claimed that Peltola knew about the gimmick before trying to retract her comments. Yet the Times also uncovered other evidence that the recruitment plot was real, including text exchanges going back months. Could having another Dan Sullivan on the ballot really help Peltola win? The theory goes like this: Confused voters intending to throw their support behind the Republican Dan Sullivan (who has a chance of beating Peltola) may accidentally vote for Dan J. Sullivan (who has no chance at all). Trump’s Justice Department cited that possibility in a lawsuit it filed last week at the president’s behest that aimed to block Alaska election officials from certifying a Peltola victory. Sounds like Democrats got caught in a pretty ugly attempt to confuse voters. Does that mean the administration’s suit has merit? Although the DOJ sued before the Times’ story broke, there are a few reasons to doubt that the Peltola campaign’s gambit merits federal intervention. For one thing, Alaska Republicans have engaged in similar hijinks. In 2024, the state party helped keep a federal inmate who had never been to Alaska on the U.S. House ballot in a bid to undermine Peltola, who ended up losing. The DOJ’s case also rests on some questionable assumptions. The feds framed the suit as a bid to “protect the integrity of the electoral process,” but the Constitution gives states the power to set election rules, and the Alaska Supreme Court decided months ago that the other Sullivan could stay on the ballot. The ballots also clearly label Sen. Sullivan as an incumbent Republican, reducing the chances of confusion. Attorney General Todd Blanche, a staunch Trump toady, has nonetheless argued that a narrow defeat for Sen. Sullivan could constitute evidence that voter confusion tipped the race, hence the preemptive lawsuit. “There are a number of scenarios where it could be obvious that a voter was confused by the way they wrote down candidates,” Blanche told CNN on Sunday. Except the U.S. guarantees the right to a secret ballot, which means it’s pretty much impossible to tell what someone was thinking when they bubbled in their preferred candidates. Rather than evidence of confusion, a narrow Sullivan defeat could just mean that Alaskans narrowly preferred Peltola as their next senator. Assuming a judge rejects the DOJ suit, how might Alaska voters respond to all this? One possibility is with a shrug. Without minimizing what the Peltola campaign reportedly did, there’s a difference between political trickery and lawbreaking election interference, and the Times story doesn’t assert that Peltola’s allies crossed that line. (Not that that’s stopped the president from claiming otherwise: On Wednesday, Trump cited the Times story in a Truth Social post arguing that Peltola “shouldn’t be allowed to run, and neither should the FAKE Dan Sullivan!”) Many voters may also have long assumed that the other Sullivan was a Democratic plant. Since June, when Alaska’s top court ruled he could stay on the ballot, most polls have shown Peltola narrowly winning. On the other hand, voters may resent the Peltola campaign’s attempt to confuse them, which is both underhanded and more than a little condescending. In what’s projected to be a close race in a state with fewer than 1 million inhabitants, turning off even a few voters could be a big problem—and a really stupid way to blow a Senate seat. Let’s turn our attention to my colleagues’ ideas about what to read and (not) watch this evening: A great book, a bad movie: Kazuo Ishiguro’s novel Klara and the Sun is strange and beautiful—but it might also be unfilmable. Laura Miller explains why Taika Waititi’s adaptation is shaping up to be a legendary box-office bomb. A reconsideration of Meta: It’s been five years since Facebook rebranded itself as Meta as part of Mark Zuckerberg’s pivot to the metaverse. That dream may be dead, but Meta lives on. Chris Stokel-Walker argues that the rebrand was the right decision—but made for the wrong reason. A new birth control option: Copper IUDs are a long-acting and nonhormonal form of birth control, but they do have some drawbacks. Luckily, if you hate your current birth control, Mia Armstrong-López is here to tell you about a new option that might be a better fit. The case for boring men: Dating in the modern era is a minefield, requiring you to deftly navigate the apps, tune out bad TikTok advice, and contend with the threat of ghosting. It’s not for the faint of heart. In a culture that pushes the promise of an exciting whirlwind romance, Alley Horn makes the case that boring men are a better bet. As a somewhat boring guy myself, I can’t say I disagree! See you back here tomorrow. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. I Wanted to Understand What Israelis Think of This Election. What I Learned Was Chilling.
2026-10-28

I Wanted to Understand What Israelis Think of This Election. What I Learned Was Chilling.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. ISRAEL—In Tel Aviv, one of Israel’s more liberal hubs, Ben Friedhaim sits with his young daughter in HaKnesset Square as waves gently lap onto the nearby beach just days before the election. Even during Shabbat, the boardwalk here is full of families basking in the sunshine, electric scooters whizzing past, and bars blasting American hits. Ice cream melts down his daughter’s hand as he tells me about his family’s recent return to the country. He came to vote. Friedhaim, who now lives in Prague, is one of more than an estimated 80,000 Israelis who returned to the country this week, having left after Oct. 7, when, he says, things got “scary.” Without the option of absentee voting in most cases, this cohort signals how important this election is. For Friedhaim, he wants to make Israel feel safe for his daughter to grow up here; this safety, in his mind, is provided by the Israeli military. “I think the army is very important when she turns 18,” he said about his toddler. The military is crucial to many here, given the country’s embattled status. “It is an important election, but Israel is always going to be at war,” he said. “There is no diplomatic solution.” Indeed, even as the election seems so critical that people like Friedhaim are returning home to vote in it, there’s a certain stasis to what’s to come. A largely right-wing Zionist vision appears entrenched across Israel’s political bench, and most candidates hoping to unseat Benjamin Netanyahu in this election offer little clarity on the future facing Palestinians in Gaza and the West Bank, or the broader region. The field has been crowded: 36 parties initially in the running. The main challengers to Netanyahu include Gadi Eisenkot of the Yashar party, the centrist former army chief of staff who opposes a two-state solution and backs some settlements in the West Bank. Former Defense Minister Avigdor Lieberman, of the right-wing Yisrael Beitenu party, is also in the running, and previously said the two-state solution is dead. And there’s Naftali Bennett, a nationalist businessman who rejects Palestinian self-determination—that’s the candidate Friedhaim is voting for because “he comes from a right-wing background.” There’s also the country’s left-leaning Democrats party, fronted by Yair Golan, who is more open to ending wars and reaching a two-state solution, though he believes that it’s not immediately realistic. Eisenkot—who also lost a son in Gaza—is essentially fronting this coalition bloc, hoping to oust Netanyahu. Then there are a handful of ultra-far-right figures, Orthodox parties, and the country’s Arab parties, none of which had a chance to win the majority outright. Just after 10 local time here on election night, the first exit polls were published: Many showed Eisenkot’s coalition initially holding a narrow lead over Netanyahu’s bloc, but no clear majority. By morning the results had already shifted toward Netanyahu, casting uncertainty over what comes next. Regardless of who wins the count, these factions will still have to work together to form a government and determine the next leader. Notably, it was a historic night for the Arab parties—but even now, it’s too early to tell what the next government will look like. The backdrop to this election has been one of tension, dissatisfaction, and fear. Even if a new coalition ushers in top-level change, it might do little to resolve the much deeper fractures beneath it. “Things have become very polarized here,” Friedhaim had said that Saturday before the election. “They’re turning people against each other.” In the time I spent in Israel in the week leading up to the vote, the election mood became visible, as did furious sentiment over what’s at stake. Looking out across a sea of Israeli flags during a massive anti-Netanyahu demonstration, one bystander bluntly says: “This country has been on the verge of civil war for years.” Still, this time feels different from past elections. The country is angry: some at Netanyahu’s cultish leadership, which they feel is running Israel into the ground; others convinced he’s the only one who can steer the country amid an increasingly hostile region. And yet, the options at the ballot box do little to address what lies ahead for Palestinians in Gaza and the West Bank, for Syrians living in the embattled Golan Heights, or for the residents of southern Lebanon who have been forced from their homes by yet another illegal Israeli expansion. Despite the differences in opinion on domestic policy, there is a pervasive sense that the Israeli identity is under threat and that its enemies must be defeated at all costs. Which means that real change in Israel—and the idea of peace—has been the hardest to envision. Hours after I spoke with Friedhaim, I witnessed a tense exchange break out among strangers at a bar discussing the need to protect Israel’s borders. They were debating the unnecessary loss of life—not of civilians from neighboring countries, but of the Israeli soldiers stationed in those occupied territories—with one person arguing that sacrifices were necessary. That same day, crowds carrying homemade signs marched to Hostage Square (“change is going to come,” said one), with attendees largely backing one of the opposition candidates listed above. Rallying for an inquiry into Oct. 7, the crowd suddenly erupted as Eisenkot gleefully passed supporters, shaking hands with potential voters eager to see a new direction for the country. Dani Schrire and his wife work in academia and had traveled from Jerusalem just to attend the Tel Aviv demonstration. “The division is not between Israelis and Palestinians,” Schrire said, though not everyone would agree. “It’s crazy fanatics who are Israeli and Palestinian on the one hand—and those who have some kind of vision of mutual acceptance, whether it’s a two-state, one-state, doesn’t matter.” The treatment of the Palestinians shapes the couple’s vote as well, though it wasn’t necessarily the only factor. As for their thoughts on Netanyahu after his latest clampdown in the West Bank, Schrire said: “I think the guy is a total nut.” Palestinians in the occupied territories—some 5.6 million people—cannot vote in Israel’s election. Among them is hostel owner Issa Nazzal, who sits in his office in the picturesque Palestinian town of Ramallah in the occupied West Bank, looking out the window at the encroaching settlement on the hilltop. For many Palestinians here, the focus hinges on surviving day by day under a terrifying occupation that has ramped up its violence in the run-up to the election. In recent days, the situation has reached a breaking point—violent settlers attack anyone in their way, including Palestinians and international journalists. People are particularly despondent about what plays out beyond the 30-foot concrete barrier surrounding the territory in the Israeli election. “I didn’t see anything that makes me feel they can change,” Nazzal said. Within Israel, change means different things depending on whom you ask. Not far from Israel’s seat of government, the Knesset, in Jerusalem, last-minute canvassing was prominent. A group of teenagers, not old enough to vote, pass flyers in support of Bezalel Smotrich, a far-right ultranationalist politician who models himself on policies including the total destruction of Gaza. Military fatigues are worn like a badge of honor in Jerusalem’s historic center, where young recruits sling automatic weapons over their shoulders. Trumplike “Make Israel Great Again” caps are flogged in tourist shops across the Old City, one of the world’s oldest continuously inhabited cities. Standing outside the family-run carpet business, Yal and his father—who didn’t share their last names—perch near a table featuring a poster of Netanyahu alongside Donald Trump, framed by fighter jets and Israeli flags. There’s no question about where Yal stands: “The Arabs here are the problem,” he says. “Israel is a small country, and everyone wants to kill Israelis.” Netanyahu’s party appeared to have been prepping the ground for claims of election fraud, and Israelis across the political spectrum raised concerns in passing about whether the country will accept whatever results emerge. At the time of writing, the mood in Jerusalem was calm, but Netanyahu tried to extend voting hours, which the elections committee rejected. It’s hard to ignore what Yal had told me days before: “I don’t want to think what’s going to happen [if Netanyahu doesn’t win].” Propaganda has undoubtedly framed this election, with the country’s most popular TV channels portraying contrasting polls over who will win. Few here appear to see—or want to see—the devastating images that continue to emerge from places like Gaza, footage that international audiences are more exposed to. Instead, campaigning hinges on the threats facing Israel, and the urgent need for more secure borders—even if that means expanding into other countries. More than 850 polling stations were set up in occupied outposts for Israeli soldiers currently serving in Palestine, Syria, and Lebanon, up from 710 in 2022. The highway heading north along the country’s Mediterranean coastline has been littered with campaign billboards in a recognizable Israeli blue for weeks. There’s also an image of New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani alongside Iran’s supreme leader, the chief of Hezbollah, and Turkey President Recep Tayyip Erdoğan. It’s an illustration of the alleged enemy, provided by Netanyahu’s Likud party. The shape of the new government could take weeks to sort out. Because of Israel’s proportional representation system, no party has ever won 61 seats outright to achieve a Knesset majority, meaning that the election will be followed by potentially long, drawn-out negotiations. Israel’s election committee tried to bar the country’s Arab parties from running, but the move was overturned. They might play a decisive role in who secures the Knesset—though few Israeli politicians in the opposition bloc currently appear open to working with the Arab parties to secure a majority government. Official election results, however, won’t be published until Nov. 4. In the oceanside city of Haifa, streets are quieter than in Israel’s major cities, but the mood in one of the Arab parties’ campaign offices was anything but. Some people here cling on to hope that Israeli politicians will have to pay attention to the Arab parties this time around. Historically, the Arab-led parties have only ever secured a record high of 15 seats, and only once did they join the governing coalition (in 2021). Then and now, they face considerable right-wing pushback. Before the election, I met with political activist Reem Hazzan as she was working on last-ditch canvassing for a party called Hadash. Here, she’s also running the campaign for the Joint List, an alliance of Arab parties that could have some determining influence in the overall election outcome. I ask her what makes this election different from others. Her answer is immediate: “The necessity to get rid of Netanyahu, Smotrich, and [far-right leader Itamar] Ben-Gvir.” She added: “If we do not get rid of them, apartheid will continue and be deepened, ethnic cleansing and Jewish terrorism in the West Bank will continue. The very slow genocide in Gaza will continue.” Hazzan worries that even of the Arabs eligible to vote, not everyone plans to: “A lot of people are worried that whatever we do, we will not be able to change anything; we will not be able to actually influence. “I wish people were much angrier, really, and much more furious about the situation, about what they are willing to sacrifice in order to change the situation,” she adds. “But I must also say that I understand why. We have been struggling for decades … and things are only getting worse.” The fear is different among this community, more visceral. Arabs in Israel are disproportionately affected by violent crime, and accounts of Muslims being targeted just for wearing hijabs or speaking Arabic are rife. “I’m afraid, regardless of these elections,” says Sally Abed, a Palestinian City Council member in Haifa. “I became a mother two years ago. I gave birth during a very active war. I am very worried as a woman, as a Palestinian, as a mother. … I really feel unsafe.” After spending a week in Israel—and witnessing a vote that seems as if it will not provide a definitive outcome and instead require more negotiation for a government to even form—I worried most about people like Abed. Israelis will argue over who actually takes charge in the weeks ahead. Palestinians will continue to live through the consequences. The fissures here run dangerously deep, and massive questions remain over whether the next government can pull people together—or push them even further apart. Microsoft breaks New Outlook attachment opening with Edge Update
2026-10-29

Microsoft breaks New Outlook attachment opening with Edge Update

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Attachments in New Outlook come undone thanks to Windows' fundamental interconnectedness of things Users of New Outlook wondering where the option to open an attachment went have a culprit to blame – an Edge update. The issue underscores how interconnected things are in the Microsoft ecosystem. According to the vendor, an update to Edge 156 "unintentionally enabled an unnecessary configuration," which means some New Outlook users are unable to open email file attachments. An error stating the file cannot be opened also appears for some, along with an instruction to restart Outlook. However, the "Open" option goes away after the error is encountered. A workaround is to use "Save As" to save the attachment, and then open the file manually. Microsoft confirmed the issue yesterday afternoon in the advisory EX1528992, and early this morning began rolling out a fix to disable the "unnecessary" configuration. It expects that it'll take 12 hours for the fix to be rolled out, and users will have to close and reopen New Outlook to feel the benefit. The problem raises some interesting questions. While we'd understand how something unexpected in Edge might cause rendering issues in New Outlook, losing the ability to open attachments because of something an Edge update did is concerning and may make some administrators reconsider the blast radius of software updates. We asked Microsoft for more information about the "unnecessary configuration" and will update this piece should it respond. However, the incident highlights connections within Windows that might surprise users. In addition, it shows that, despite a woeful showing on the desktop market share front – although Edge recently "surged" to almost 15 percent last month, according to Statcounter, up from around 10 percent for much of the year – Microsoft's browser can still capture user attention. It's just not the sort of attention the Windows maker might have hoped for. It is also worth noting that Outlook Classic does not appear to be affected by the issue (according to Microsoft's EX1528992 advisory for Exchange Online). Impacted users could therefore temporarily add opening attachments to the list of things the old software does that the new software doesn't. ® Attachments in New Outlook come undone thanks to Windows' fundamental interconnectedness of things Once more, with feeling: 'First they ignore you, then they laugh at you, then they fight you' PARTNER CONTENT: Connectivity at all times, abroad Cursing in login credentials can help keep the bad guys out, but not when the rest of your security is BS Keeping secrets is difficult in an open world, but we've done it before and must do it again PARTNER CONTENT: HPE Alletra MP X10000 cache offload delivers up to 20× faster time to first token in testing validated by Kamiwaza. Apparently, remembering things beats calculating them again. Matrix takes sovereign messaging into the realm of the tech giants
2026-10-29

Matrix takes sovereign messaging into the realm of the tech giants

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Once more, with feeling: 'First they ignore you, then they laugh at you, then they fight you' Decentralized messaging platform Matrix is no longer the upstart it once was. The organization is now a serious challenger to the status quo as concerns about sovereignty and independence reach new heights. At the organization's recent Malmo event, which grew attendance 30 percent year-on-year, the mood shifted from "yes, this is important" to "yes, we're rolling this out." Matrix is an open standard for communication. It's a federated system, so there are no central hubs or overseas control to worry about, and it can be used for anything from messaging to voice over IP. Video is also supported. The system is an alternative to services such as Microsoft Teams. It has become popular in the public sector as governments grow increasingly wary of relying on US tech giants for services that the Trump administration could switch off at will. However, that increased interest and subsequent roll-outs have brought a level of scrutiny Matrix was not fully prepared for. A recent report regarding anonymous reaction to a roll-out of the technology by the European Commission took Element - the company that puts the lion share of the money into Matrix - by surprise. One attendee at the Malmo conference observed, "First they [competitors] ignore you, then they laugh at you, then they fight you." Matrix remains an open standard, however, the open source foundation behind it would certainly welcome more paying members. "It's true that we're definitely getting more attention than we have had before," said Element CEO Matthew Hodgson. "We are seeing Matrix ending up on the critical path of digital sovereignty as almost the poster child of the whole thing. "I'm not sure that we necessarily do want to be the poster child," he added, "We didn't sign up to end up being ambassadors for digital sovereignty, roaming from government to government, but empirically that's where we've ended up, and yeah, that's going to attract attention from people who would rather everyone just kept in their traditional big tech systems." This comes amid worries about the reliability of international partners, from early projects including France's Tchap messenger for the public sector and Germany's BundesMessenger through to more recent users, such as the Swedish public sector. Indeed, in one memorable demonstration during the Matrix event, messages were sent between French, Swedish, and NATO entities. "I cannot overstate the strangeness," Hodgson added, "of having various national militaries phoning up every couple of days, saying, 'Look, we need to get on board Matrix too because we need to talk to this country and they're using Matrix, and we need to talk to that country, and they're using Matrix.'" "It could be for defense, could be national security, could just be healthcare or education, public sector. But that is real. That is why there is a feeding frenzy." Not that Element or Matrix is trying to build a better Teams than Teams. Patrick Alberts, Element's Chief Product Officer, said, "This is a digitally sovereign communication solution that, by and large, works like Teams in many aspects, but it's not set out to be the faster Porsche here..." As for the reported complaints from the European Commission over Element's software implementation, Diana Gheorghe, EC head of sector for Communication and Collaboration Services, addressed them directly and described the anonymous complaints as "the natural friction of [a] high-profile rollout that we have done at scale." Alexandru Vignide, EC service manager for Communication and Collaboration Services, who is very much at the  pointy end of making the sovereign communication tools work, noted the speed of the rollout, which began with a proof of concept in Q4 2025, and hit laptops between July and September 2026. The EC chose the Schengen Cloud Alliance (which includes OVHcloud) for hosting and, according to Vignide, "We wanted to provide a familiar experience for users." Not everyone is impressed with what is rolling out, but it's important to remember that change can be alarming, and this is still early days. It is, however, a significant one. Gheorghe said that, as well as increasing resilience and business continuity, "We wanted to have vendor diversity. We also wanted to have data control and ownership of the platform." Microsoft, as an example, would point to the sovereign capabilities of its system. As the Matrix conference kicked off, it announced new capabilities, including additional options for running AI, data services, and confidential workloads on customer-controlled platforms, and highlighted its sovereign partner ecosystem for customers who prefer local expertise. However, Microsoft (and other tech giants) cannot escape concerns about who ultimately controls the data and what administrations might do with it. In 2025, a Microsoft representative memorably confirmed the US giant "cannot guarantee" data sovereignty to customers in France. There is, however, a fly in the Matrix ointment. The platform is open source and, like so many open source projects, requires funding. Right now, Element accounts for much of the Matrix Foundation's funding. Hodgson finds this a little frustrating. "If every government who is operationally dependent on Matrix put the smallest amount of money in the Foundation, the Foundation would have enough money to really start investing in the ecosystem and not be dependent on Element basically picking up the tab." He acknowledged that things had improved in the last year, but said, "It's not good governance for an open standard to have one big player like Element effectively propping things up." This isn't uncommon in the open source world, and it's a little disappointing given the ever-increasing importance placed on sovereignty in communications. Alberts commented: "There is confusion in the market that the thing somehow creates itself. "No, it doesn't. It requires hardworking people. These hardworking people need to be paid, and that payment, in order to support government deployments going forward, ideally also comes from these governments. And some of them do a lot and do it in a great way, and that's amazing.
