Anduril now says its BlueChip laptop will meet Buy American standards at launch
2026-10-29

Anduril now says its BlueChip laptop will meet Buy American standards at launch

The Verge | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Palmer Luckey says it was always the goal, and now the company is committing to it. Palmer Luckey says it was always the goal, and now the company is committing to it. On Tuesday, defense contractor Anduril and chipmaker Qualcomm announced a partnership to build laptops entirely in the United States, starting with a single model in 2027. They also announced a “long-term goal” of pushing the computer industry to create “a fully domestic supply chain.” Even a single Made-in-USA laptop would be a surprise, because no major US computer company builds its laptops here — not Apple, not Dell, not HP, not Microsoft. We’re not aware of any company that does. But we are aware of companies that have briefly and strategically assembled computers here seemingly to appease President Donald Trump. We’re also aware of companies that have promised US jobs only to deliver empty buildings. And we’re aware of at least one fake “US” company that lied to the government about making product domestically. That’s why we now ask pointed questions when a company says it’s committed to US manufacturing. We asked TP-Link those sorts of questions just over a week ago. On Monday, we asked Anduril founder Palmer Luckey and Qualcomm president and CEO Cristiano Amon those kinds of questions. Over the course of two days, Anduril has changed one important answer. Anduril communications VP Shannon Prior now says the company’s laptop will have at least 65 percent of its components made in the US by the time it launches in 2027. It’s an important number, and one that Anduril did not commit to earlier this week. Luckey held a call with reporters on Monday, during which he was clear that he wants to make literally every part of a computer in the United States — and that Anduril and Qualcomm have already mapped out a plan. “We have a technical roadmap to get to 100 percent domestic over time,” he told journalists. Luckey also suggested his laptop would be built to comply with the requirements of the military and other government agencies — and that it would meet or exceed one specific US government standard that has an exact definition. “We are not just barely meeting the bar for Made in America. We are going hard to make this an all-American play, and not even the Buy American Act requires what we’re trying,” Luckey said. The Buy American Act dates back to 1933, but it was updated during the Biden Administration to require that at least 65 percent of the cost of a product’s components must be made domestically for it be considered a domestic product itself. That number is going up to 75 percent in 2029. Many products need to be made in America to those standards, or federal agencies can’t buy them without receiving a waiver — which agencies currently use en masse, Luckey himself argued on the call. “There’s a number of government agencies that are required by law to buy American-made computers [...] All of those procurements are happening under waivers of those laws,” he said. We also noticed that Anduril’s press release specifies that its first laptop will be “designed and assembled” in the United States, not necessarily “manufactured” here (though the release does talk about future manufacturing). This was also a sticking point with router maker TP-Link, which would not confirm full manufacturing even after suggesting it in an interview with The Verge. That’s why we asked Luckey what percent of his first laptop’s components would be made in the US. His reply was not 65 percent or 70 percent. It was “TBD.” You can read the exchange for yourself: The Verge: When it arrives in 2027, what percent of the components in the BlueChip laptop will be made in the US? Luckey: TBD. I’m doing my very, very best. It’s gonna be a whole mix of things based on when do certain memory fabs come online? When do certain CPU, GPU fabs come online? There’s a whole— Like, am I able to get a display line here or not? Do I have to do that after launch? I’m being totally open kimono with you here. Those questions are not settled. It is not clear if I will be able. I know the glass is gonna be made in the US, I know the frame is gonna be made in the US around that display. I know that parts of the ribbon component are gonna be made in the U.S. I don’t know if I will have successfully pulled that component in or a dozen other components. So it’s a little up in the air. I’m doing my very, very best. I think most likely the thing that we ship is not going to be 100-percent American. I think it’ll be a blend of US and allies around the world, mostly US, probably a few things from some allies, and then that’ll be in the context of conversations with those allies about bringing those components into production in the United States, and I’m being honest with the government on this too. I mean, I’ve had conversations where I’ve said, listen. I would love to give you guys an American, 100-percent American laptop right off the bat, but that is not the environment the US government has created or US industry has created. I can’t let the perfect be the enemy of the good. When we followed up by email to ask specifically about 65 percent, the answer was that the laptop prototype is currently at 32 percent and Anduril could not yet commit to 65 percent. Here’s Anduril spokesperson Stephanie Davis’ full reply on Tuesday: For the prototype today, approximately 32% is U.S.-origin by component procurement cost, and more than 90% is sourced from the United States and allied countries combined. We’re working to expand domestic sourcing and production ahead of planned availability in 2027, with the goal of eliminating foreign content and ultimately establishing an end-to-end American supply chain. The final domestic-content percentage at launch remains unsettled, so we can’t confirm above or below 65% today. That’s why we’re asking you to revise the assertion that it will be “well below” that threshold. Today, Prior writes that Anduril can indeed commit to 65 percent. Here is the most recent statement: Our previous response to The Verge was incorrect. The BlueChip laptop is indeed being developed to exceed the 65% U.S. domestic-content threshold at launch in 2027. Meeting that threshold is a fundamental objective of the program. The precise domestic-content percentage of the final product is still being determined as we finalize suppliers and work through prototypes, and we look forward to sharing more next year. Today’s statement is the first time Anduril has confirmed that we, and government agencies, theoretically shouldn’t need to wait until Anduril achieves its full goals to see a laptop that meets the Buy American standard. If everything works out, Anduril’s first laptop in 2027, with Qualcomm, should be that laptop. We invited Luckey to explain how Anduril would get to 65 percent domestic component production in a follow-up interview, but we were told he’s not currently available. On the Monday call, he told journalists that Anduril already has a US glassmaker lined up for the laptop’s cover glass, is programming FPGAs (field-programmable gate arrays) to serve the purpose of power management chips and other circuits it can’t normally can’t get outside China, and is developing made-in-America batteries as well. The press release adds that the enclosure is designed “for US machining” and the laptop will use metals “that US industry can produce and machine at scale.” As for the laptop’s Snapdragon processor, we asked Qualcomm CEO Cristiano Amon about that. “TSMC has a manufacturing facility in Arizona, and that’s where our chips are going to be made,” he told The Verge. A hacker stole more than 40,000 DHS employee records

Visit Original Website
The names, job titles, phone numbers, and emails of thousands of ICE, CBP, and other DHS employees were compromised in a massive hack.﻿ The names, job titles, phone numbers, and emails of thousands of ICE, CBP, and other DHS employees were compromised in a massive hack.﻿ A hacker stole a trove of personal information from the Department of Homeland Security (DHS). The breach exposes the names, job titles, phone numbers, email addresses, and office locations of more than 41,000 employees, including those in US Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) and Customs and Border Protection (CBP), according to TechCrunch and Wired. The data also linked the agencies to specific surveillance tools. As reported by TechCrunch, a hacker known as “Department of Peace” gained access to the data through Okta, an identity and access management service used by the DHS. Around 21,000 records pertain to CBP employees, and nearly 7,000 belong to staff at ICE. The data also includes records from the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA), US Citizenship and Immigration Services (USCIS), and Homeland Security Investigations (HSI). The DHS has worked to conceal the identities of CBP and ICE agents, who have carried out anti-immigration raids around the US, while also tracking down online critics. “They have our information, so it’s only fair that we have theirs,” the “Department of Peace” hacker said, according to TechCrunch. This leak comes just weeks after hackers stole data from the FBI on its employees and job applicants. The leaked DHS information was reportedly given to cybersecurity researcher maia arson crimew, who shared it with Distributed Denial of Secrets (DDoSecrets), a nonprofit organization that’s now hosting the leaked “Icepick” dataset. The leak also reportedly includes information about DHS groups that have access to tools that can be used for surveillance, such as the facial recognition app Mobile Fortify and PenLink, which can collect and analyze data from phones and online services. The DHS didn’t immediately respond to The Verge’s request for comment. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Readwise is taking on Kindle and Kobo for ebook supremacy
2026-10-28

Readwise is taking on Kindle and Kobo for ebook supremacy

The Verge | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
Taking on Kindle is hard. Beating Goodreads might be really easy.﻿ Taking on Kindle is hard. Beating Goodreads might be really easy.﻿ For the last several years, Readwise has been building tools for power readers. There’s the eponymous product, Readwise, which stores and categorizes your Kindle highlights to help you remember what you’ve read. There’s Readwise Reader, an app for reading newsletters, PDFs, and web articles you’ve saved for later. And now there’s Readwise Books, a full-fledged platform for buying, reading, and talking about books. Readwise Books is currently in beta, available through invite or to existing Readwise subscribers. I’ve been testing the app for a few days, and it’s a much bigger take on reading than most. Tristan Homsi and Daniel Doyon, Readwise’s cofounders, like to call the platform “Strava for reading” because it can track how long, how much, and how fast you read, and it can show that information to friends you make in the Readwise Books app. The app’s homepage is a feed of what other people are reading, with comments and recommendations laced throughout. “Strava for reading” wasn’t the original goal of Readwise Books, which has been part of the Readwise plan since the beginning. “Literally, Dan and I talked about this when we first met,” Homsi says. “We feel like Kindle could be so much better. We really didn’t think it would take nine-plus years to be able to get to this point.” But it turns out, Doyon adds, that for all their ideas about innovation in books, “no one sits around wanting a better Kindle.” But there is one thing they do wish for: a better Goodreads. “Everybody hates Goodreads.” In a sense, then, the plan for Readwise Books is to take on Kindle by first taking on Goodreads. The Readwise Books app offers a nice way to track books you want to read, are reading, and have read; it even syncs with your Kindle account to let you track books you’ve bought from Amazon by taking pictures of your starting and ending pages. You can rate and review books and see what others have said. It’s a little Strava, a little Letterboxd, and a lot nicer to look at than Goodreads. The long-term goal, of course, is to get people to stop buying Kindle books and start buying from Readwise. Homsi and Doyon have spent the last several years working to reach deals with all five of the major US publishers in order to have a competitive library of books available. That meant endless negotiations, security reviews, DRM debates, and more — both cofounders seem exhausted just talking about the process. But eventually, by several small miracles, they got the deals done. And since those deals govern the price of books, and many things about how they’re sold and presented, they bring Readwise to a nearly even playing field almost immediately. It all took a lot longer than expected, but Doyon and Homsi think their timing might actually be pretty good. Digital book reading continues to grow, particularly among young people; a huge amount of that reading happens on phones and tablets. Meanwhile, a new crop of E Ink devices like the tiny Xteink and the Boox Palma line have expanded the universe of available hardware for a company like Readwise. Not long ago, Doyon says, they would have had no choice but to build an e-reader of their own. (And he says they still might.) Now, they can just make sure it runs on an E Ink-friendly flavor of Android. After a couple of days using Readwise Books, I can say confidently I already like it better than practically all of its competition. It’s a much nicer-looking app than anything you’ll get from Kindle or Kobo; it has lots of ways to customize how books look; and at least for now, I’m finding the tracking features compel me to read more instead of annoying me. (Homsi says some early versions of the gamification features were substantially more annoying.) I’ve found a few bugs, and the half-integration with Kindle is a little confusing in spots, but it’s already a really nice place to read. Of course, I, like millions of other people, already own a Kindle and lots of Kindle books. Switching ebook platforms is hard, and Doyon says he’s not counting on everyone to do it right away. He says he’s hopeful Readwise can persuade existing users to switch, in order to sync their notes and progress more seamlessly. He’s also hoping that the advent of AI, and the ways in which AI reliance may rot our brains, will get a lot of people back into the idea of reading more deeply again. And that a bunch of those people might be looking for a nicer, more social place to do so. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Our climate models have a big, dangerous blind spot
2026-10-29

Our climate models have a big, dangerous blind spot

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Climate change itself is increasingly fueling climate change. Rising concentrations of greenhouse gases are driving up global temperatures, setting off dangerous feedback loops that we have not fully accounted for in our climate models—or our emissions-reduction goals. Scientists have long warned that warming would propel further warming in various ways, but a growing number of observations and studies are at last providing a sharper sense of just how powerful these cascading effects could be. And, by extension, they’re showing how much faster the planet may warm in this century. A study in Environmental Research Letters in September, synthesizing the results of previous papers, found that the additional carbon dioxide and methane released from thawing permafrost, intensifying wildfires, hotter lakes and reservoirs, and wetter, warmer wetlands alone could drive global average temperatures up 0.2 °C to 0.4 °C this century. That would come on top of the 2.6°C of warming above preindustrial levels that modelers are already expecting through 2100. The additional heat could subject many of the world’s cities to monthslong heat waves and expose hundreds of millions of people to flooding. None of the models that inform the major UN climate reports, which assess how much the planet will likely warm under various emissions scenarios, account for all these feedback effects. Many of them don’t incorporate any. That leaves a huge blind spot in our understanding of how much the world could actually warm in the coming decades—and how rapidly nations will need to slash emissions to stay within critical boundaries. A separate analysis published in One Earth in March, which examined what the feedback problem means for our international climate goals, concluded that the world may be much further off track than is widely believed. It found that just three regional sources of warming-driven emissions—thawing permafrost, increasing fires in boreal forests, and warming tropical wetlands—may shorten by as much as 25% the time before the planet consistently breaches the 1.5 °C and 2 °C warming thresholds. Our climate models and the UN’s reports clearly need to be updated to better represent these effects. Nations must also step up their efforts to reduce emissions to reflect this improving understanding of the dangers we face, says Sam Abernethy, the lead author of the paper and a climate scientist at Spark Climate Solutions, a San Francisco–based nonprofit research group. At least some of this is starting to happen. Spark kick-started an initiative, called the Warming-Induced Greenhouse Gas Emissions Model Intercomparison Project, through which more than 20 international teams are running the same set of analyses on different models to develop greater understanding of feedback effects’ potential power. Researchers involved hope that the UN will incorporate those results into its next major climate assessment, due out in 2029. That report will update estimates of the remaining emissions that can be released before the world crosses key temperature thresholds. But what is already clear is that the planet is rapidly warming and that nations haven’t done nearly enough to cut fossil-fuel pollution. And even if we did manage to halt it, warming-driven emissions could continue. So a number of the research groups involved in the recent studies have started to explore what nations could do to get feedback emissions under control. Scientists at the Woodwell Climate Research Center in Massachusetts are, for example, investigating the possibility of preventing fires or rapidly detecting them and putting them out when they threaten to burn the vegetation and soil that help insulate particularly carbon-rich wedges of permafrost (a subject I explored at length in a recent feature story). In addition, the Environmental Defense Fund’s Climate Innovation Initiative has backed lab research on the potential of adding sulfate, iron, or other compounds to wetlands to counteract fast-rising methane emissions. Several startups are also exploring possibilities. They’re looking into suppressing the lightning strikes that are igniting more and more fires in high latitudes, placing synthetic mats above permafrost to prevent it from melting, and deploying autonomous drones to quickly suppress potentially high-emissions fires before they burn out of control. Investigating all these ideas is worthwhile. But researchers are only in the earliest stages of figuring out how big a dent in emissions any such efforts could achieve across sprawling global ecosystems undergoing massive transformations. The surest path to reducing the effect of warming-driven warming remains the one we’ve known all along: breaking society’s addiction to fossil fuels. This article is from The Spark, MIT Technology Review’s weekly climate newsletter. To receive it in your inbox every Wednesday, sign up here. MIT Technology Review
Creating AI value with enterprise context
2026-10-28

Creating AI value with enterprise context

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
Across a business, any number of AI platforms, systems, and tools may be at work simultaneously—for example, AI-powered customer platforms, conversational assistants, and workflow and process automation tools. Each one is sophisticated, advanced, and quick to respond. But, when set to work, the outcomes and actions that emerge may be wrong, confused, or lacking a proper understanding of the business. Many organizations find themselves in this situation—and it could be one of key reasons they struggle to extract returns on their AI investments. An MIT Technology Review Insights poll finds that while every organization surveyed is using AI today to some degree, fewer than one in five (16%) are extracting transformative value from their deployments. The largest percentage of respondents (40%) say they are seeing moderate gains in specific tasks or teams, while 21% report meaningful gains, leaving 23% that are seeing minimal returns or none at all. Companies are mostly still piloting AI use cases (according to 38% of respondents) or using it to assist employees’ work with tools like co-pilots or chat assistants (35%). Just 12% of enterprises have AI agents in production and 3% say they are using AI to do most of the work in key processes. “There’s a big gap between what the models are capable of and how the models are actually being useful inside organizations,” says Prukalpa Sankar, co-founder of Atlan. “For every real-life intent and purpose, artificial general intelligence is already here. It’s just not really useful in most companies today.” The missing layer, Sankar says, is context, or how well AI understands a business. A context layer is the architectural infrastructure that sits between an organization’s business systems (for example, systems of record and data, and its AI platforms). It translates enterprise intelligence into governed, machine-usable context for AI agents or models and helps knowledge compound. “Without a context layer for AI, it’s almost like you’re hiring the smartest intern in the world, blindfolding them, and throwing them into your organization,” Sankar says. It was researched and written by humans, with any AI tools that may have been used limited to production processes under human oversight. MIT Technology Review
Pairing industrial AI autonomy and human control
2026-10-28

Pairing industrial AI autonomy and human control

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
Living digital twins are helping industrial plants run more like living systems, and setting the stage for industries that work in sync. Most people associate industrial environments with cold steel and clockwork machinery. Functionally, though, a plant or factory is more like a living thing: It has a pulse, even a metabolism. It goes through wear and tear as it ages, and signs of deteriorating health often show up as barely perceptible irregularities. Digital twin technology has long aimed to mirror that dynamism and model anomalies that may be symptomatic of larger problems. But earlier implementations were a few beats behind physical reality; they were incapable of real-time analysis and unable to proactively address any issues detected. They focused more on simulation, observation and what-if scenario planning, rather than closed-loop execution. Rather than serving as a digital mirror of reality that can spot anomalies or suggest optimizations, a living digital twin combines a dynamic, high-fidelity digital representation of complex operations with the capacity to intervene in the physical world through operations and control systems. AI is at the center of this change. Rob McGreevy, chief product officer at AVEVA, likens newer, AI-infused digital twins to organisms that grow and evolve. These “living” versions are adaptive, evergreen models of a plant’s assets and processes. Because they keep pace with live conditions and connect systems that once ran in isolation, they provide a previously missing layer of visibility and orchestration that better reflects the organic rhythms of the facility itself. Now, agentic AI is also enabling industrial systems to move beyond diagnostics or suggestions and act of their own accord. As the technology progresses toward greater autonomy, it’s starting to develop the reflexes for a kind of industrial homeostasis, in which small, constant adjustments keep a plant or factory healthy and humming. All that said, human judgment and operator oversight still play an instrumental role by defining what healthy looks like to begin with—and flagging when AI systems are falling out of alignment. Living digital twins provide the means for human engineers to monitor what AI is doing and ensure execution is on track. Living digital twins link engineering and design information with real-time operational data through a digital thread, or a connected record of an asset that follows it through its lifecycle. A couple of notable technological shifts have helped string this thread together: Sensors (which have gotten more affordable in recent years) now stream telemetry from IoT devices and industrial control equipment, and software platforms bring that information into user-friendly interfaces. Predictive and physics-based models can also provide AI-enriched insights into how a machine or process might behave. The lenses through which operators view digital twins have evolved in turn. “You can look at the digital twin through the lens of sustainability: What are my emissions?” says McGreevy. “Or, through the lens of maintenance: Is this asset performing in a healthy way? Or, through a performance metric: Is it producing the number of widgets I need?” The next phase is letting AI act on select insights those lenses illuminate. For their part, manufacturers are eager for this chapter: In a 2026 Manufacturing Leadership Council survey of manufacturers (opens in new tab), 66% said they are using or plan to use agentic AI in their operations, and 40% expect some degree of factory autonomy by 2030. At the same time, they’re wary of the potential risks: In Deloitte’s 2025 smart manufacturing survey (opens in new tab), 65% of executives ranked operational risk, including the potential for business disruption, among their top concerns. Source: MIT Technology Review Insights with data from Manufacturing Leadership Council (opens in new tab), 2026 “There is a lot more refinement to be done, a lot more proof points, testing, and validation needed,” McGreevy says about the ongoing process of making AI autonomous. In the near term, McGreevy expects agentic AI to follow the path seen in other industrial technologies like SCADA systems. “The ‘S’ in SCADA means supervisory, and supervisory means there’s a human in the middle,” he says. He sees AI working mostly in that advisory mode for the foreseeable future, with full autonomy limited to narrow, highly repetitive tasks. As for the role human operators play, the day-to-day is evolving. Workers who once specialized in a single process line or maintenance task can now take on multiple disciplines. They can spend less time and effort approving routine actions one by one, and more on defining acceptable operating limits. Because AI can reinforce patterns in the data it receives, catching missteps early is a core part of the job. Human knowledge and experience in interpreting what AI is seeing and doing is instrumental. Governance on a company- and industry-wide scale, too, needs to adapt to the times. Organizations must establish clear guardrails for which decisions AI can make on its own and how its actions are monitored and audited. Still, many have yet to set up comprehensive safeguards: In a recent study from AVEVA and IMD (opens in new tab), 43% of organizations said AI has changed decision-making and accountability across ecosystem partners only slightly or not at all, while just 23% reported a significant or fundamental change. (Only 27% reported sharing data substantially or extensively with partners.) In the meantime, regulators are starting to define some of the rules. The EU AI Act classifies certain AI safety components used in critical infrastructure as high-risk and imposes requirements for human oversight. Companies are setting up their own structures as well; AVEVA, for one, has a responsible AI governance board and a published set of principles for how AI is developed and deployed. Inside the semi-autonomous systems already running, that oversight looks different from one deployment to another. In some cases, an operator approves every action; in others, AI acts on its own while people oversee and define the boundaries it can operate within. McGreevy cites several examples of what machine autonomy and human-plus-machine collaboration looks like among AVEVA customers: The same synchronization that was once missing from individual plants’ technology systems has also been lacking on a larger scale. “Manufacturing and critical infrastructure have operated as siloed systems for the last 50 years,” says McGreevy. But certain emerging technologies, he suggests, are creating opportunities for more holistic coordination. On an enterprise level, this might involve the integration of physical AI: systems that pair robotics and sensing technologies with AI-powered decision-making. Drones that fly over pipelines and power lines, for instance, can feed visual inspection data to algorithms that spot gas leaks and other hazards. Not only does this create a more accurate and interconnected flow of information, but it spares humans from needing to physically enter potentially dangerous environments. “The robotics-meets-software convergence will mean fewer incidents, fewer outages, and a more sustainable way of operating,” McGreevy predicts. A bigger-picture version extends beyond any one plant or process to entire industrial networks, something McGreevy dubs the “integrated value chain.” “Living digital twins make digital manifestations of the entire value chain possible,” he says. “This allows companies to react quickly to supply chain changes, demand changes, tariffs, and material availability.” Amid today’s realities of turbulent geopolitics and shifting regulatory demands, such resilience will be “an absolute necessity,” adds McGreevy. “Without it, it will be very hard to run an effective business.”

Industrial Intelligence: The next growth opportunity for manufacturing
2026-10-27

Industrial Intelligence: The next growth opportunity for manufacturing

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Every manufacturer has its own industrial intelligence. Some is documented, and some resides in the knowledge of experienced workers. Consider an experienced operator preparing to restart a line after a changeover. The machine settings matter, but so does knowing how a particular material behaves, what happened during the previous shift, and when an apparently minor variation deserves a closer look. A procedure can describe the sequence, but successful execution depends on the judgment that comes from experience and the organization’s accumulated know-how. That combination is industrial intelligence: a manufacturer’s accumulated know-how, operating practices, production data, and understanding of its own business. The opportunity now is to make more of that intelligence available wherever decisions are made, then connect it to the physical world of manufacturing. The strategic destination is not more agents, but rather to turn the existing business intelligence into an engine for profitable growth. Expertise has limited reach when it stays with one person, inside one system, or within one plant. Leaders now must determine how to extend that reach without stripping away the context that makes the knowledge useful. For example, an engineer evaluating a process change needs more than a production record. They need to understand the circumstances behind it: the equipment configuration, the quality requirements, and the trade-offs an operator made. Connecting those elements can help people apply experience beyond the place where it originated. When individual knowledge becomes widely accessible, innovation can spread to the maintenance workshop, the planning office, the engineering team, and the production floor. More people can contribute because more people can work with what the organization knows. But access to knowledge is only part of the opportunity. Manufacturing ultimately is physical, and it depends on materials, machines, and products. Physical AI describes systems that can perceive conditions, reason about them, and take action within the physical world. By connecting machines and processes, manufacturers get a clearer view of what is happening on the factory floor. Agents can then reason on top of that information to identify patterns, predict problems, and determine the best course of action. Physical AI brings business intelligence into the physical process, allowing machines and systems to respond to changing conditions. Consider a robotic-handling operation encountering variation in the parts arriving at its station. Vision can help the system assess a part’s position. A model informed by the material and task can help determine an appropriate movement. Connected controls can then execute an adjustment within validated limits. The value is not merely seeing the variation; it is responding appropriately. The same principle applies to quality operations. When Toyota Industries connected previously fragmented paint shop data, engineering and operations teams were given the context needed to understand defect drivers. The company reports that analysis cycles fell from five days to under four hours, while its pilot reduced relevant paint defects by about 25%. The breakthrough was not simply identifying another signal; it was helping people understand why defects were occurring and respond before the outcome was locked in. This is the type of situation where industrial intelligence has become essential. Data may reveal what is happening, but industrial intelligence provides the context for what should happen, which constraints cannot be breached, and when uncertainty requires human judgment. Simulation offers a way to examine proposed actions before applying them to equipment. Manufacturers can explore how a change might affect a process in a digital environment. That does not remove the need for physical validation, but it creates room to learn without treating the production line as the first experiment. Responsible deployment, therefore, begins with operational discipline. Leaders need to establish clear authority or what systems may change, reliable connections to equipment, defined safety boundaries, and a record of decisions and actions. People must be able to recognize uncertainty, intervene, and bring equipment to a safe state. Trust rests on those practical arrangements, not on confidence in a model alone. Manufacturing has long combined human ingenuity with increasingly capable tools. Physical AI extends that relationship. Intelligent equipment can take on suitable repetitive or physically demanding tasks, while agent-assisted operations can help people navigate information-intensive decisions and changing conditions. The aim is not to make machines smarter; it is to see what people can build when knowledge can travel, intelligence can act, and ambition has more room to become reality. Rockwell Automation illustrates the point. At its Singapore plant, technicians use a maintenance copilot that combines manufacturing data, machine manuals, and knowledge captured from experienced workers. This system has reduced the time required for new workers to learn how to troubleshoot hundreds of machines from nine months to three. An engineer could explore more process alternatives before committing to a change. An operator could help improve the system’s response rather than repeatedly managing the same exception. This type of progress requires investment in equipment but also in people: training, time to learn, and playing a meaningful role in shaping how systems work. Machines become more capable so people can become more capable. Industrial intelligence connects what a manufacturer knows: the experience of its people, the signals from its operations, and the lessons accumulated over time. Physical AI gives that intelligence a way to act in the physical world, turning knowledge into decisions and decisions into action. The next challenge for industry is not simply to produce more, but to grow without adding complexity at the same rate. Intelligence can help manufacturers turn experience into scalable capability—improving productivity, accelerating innovation, expanding capacity, and enabling people to solve problems that were previously difficult to tackle. The next frontier of industry is not smarter machines or more automated factories; it is businesses that can continuously learn, adapt, and act. The opportunity is to turn the intelligence embedded in every operation into a compounding advantage for growth. Watch the video series to learn how industrial customers are unlocking intelligence across the value chain, and join Microsoft at Microsoft Ignite to see what’s next for Industrial Intelligence. Nasdaq futures fall as Alphabet slides and Microsoft rises on results
2026-10-29

Nasdaq futures fall as Alphabet slides and Microsoft rises on results

The Next Web | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
US tech stocks were set to open lower on Thursday after mixed results from Alphabet and Microsoft. Investors are weighing how much Big Tech spends on AI against how quickly that spending pays off. Nasdaq 100 futures were down 1.2% early on Thursday, Reuters reported. S&P 500 futures fell 0.4%, while Dow futures rose 0.35%, as money moved out of technology shares and into other parts of the market. Alphabet shares fell 10.5% before the market opened. The Google owner’s total revenue of $127.6bn fell just short of forecasts, as search advertising disappointed. It also burned through cash as it spent heavily on AI data centres. Its spending on property and equipment reached a record $51.4bn in the third quarter, and free cash flow was negative $9.1bn, according to its quarterly results. Revenue at its Google Cloud unit more than doubled, however. Microsoft moved the other way, rising 3.5%. Revenue at its Azure cloud business grew 48% to $33.2bn, according to the company’s results, and it forecast growth of 49% to 50% for the current quarter. Total revenue rose 19% to $92.3bn, ahead of forecasts, and its cash flow also beat expectations, Reuters reported. “We still have demand that exceeds supply,” said Amy Hood, Microsoft’s chief financial officer. Options traders had expected a big move in Microsoft shares around the results, as scrutiny of AI spending grew. “Some investors are no longer giving all hyperscalers a ‘free pass’ to spend without limit,” Jeremy Saltzberg, senior investment officer at Check Capital Management, told Reuters. Instead, he said, investors are now comparing each company’s spending with how soon it can turn AI into revenue. Software stocks also came under pressure. Cybersecurity firm Fortinet fell 25.4%, and ServiceNow dropped 19% after its own quarterly results, while Salesforce and Zscaler were also lower. Chipmakers Nvidia, AMD and Intel all slipped. Meta, which reported last week, is among the companies facing the same questions. Its latest filing showed about $825bn in future commitments, mostly for AI data centres and cloud computing. Amazon reports its results after the US market closes on Thursday. The moves come a day after the Federal Reserve held interest rates steady. Traders see a more than 81% chance of a rate rise in December, according to the CME FedWatch tool. Inflation remains high, driven partly by an oil price shock linked to the conflict in the Middle East. Investors are now waiting for September’s PCE inflation figures, the Fed’s preferred measure, and the first estimate of third-quarter economic growth. Hamburg’s MuRay raises €7.1M for muon imaging of rocket engines
2026-10-29

Hamburg’s MuRay raises €7.1M for muon imaging of rocket engines

The Next Web | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
MuRay co-founders and co-CEOs Andi Hektor (left) and Kristjan Põder. Hamburg startup MuRay has raised €7.1M to look inside dense metal parts, such as 3D-printed rocket engines and gas turbines. It does this with muons, tiny particles that pass through materials that block X-rays. The total combines a €3.5M private round with €3.6M in public funding, including a grant from the EU’s Horizon Europe research programme. First Momentum Ventures led the private round, co-led by Caelora, a laser company that spun out of German fusion startup Marvel Fusion this year. Superangel VC, Nordic Science Investments, Mätch VC, Arcas VC and Tiny VC also took part, MuRay said. X-rays can only get through a few centimetres of steel. Muons, by contrast, can pass through more than a metre of steel and other dense metals, according to the company. MuRay generates a controlled beam of them and uses its own detectors and AI software to build 3D images of what is inside a part. The company says its method can also tell apart materials that look the same on an X-ray, while exposing the part to less radiation. It aims to let manufacturers check parts for hidden flaws while they are still on the production line, with scans taking minutes rather than hours. “X-rays reach their limit at a few centimetres of steel, yet much of modern industry, from reusable rockets to gas turbines, is built from metal far thicker than that,” said Andi Hektor, MuRay’s co-founder and co-CEO. MuRay grew out of DESY, the German particle physics research centre in Hamburg. Co-founder and co-CEO Kristjan Põder led DESY’s work on laser-plasma accelerators for industrial and medical use, while Hektor previously co-founded GScan, which uses naturally occurring muons to scan large structures. The pair launched the company in 2025 and came out of stealth that November with €325,000 in early funding. “This funding will help us continue proving the physics whilst delivering our first service to industrial customers,” said Põder. MuRay plans to open its first imaging service for industrial customers in 2027, using DESY’s existing electron beam in Hamburg. Its first full-scale system, powered by a compact laser-driven accelerator, is due in 2029. With the German Space Agency at DLR, it has modelled how to inspect a rocket engine. It plans its first real engine test in 2027, as demand for space hardware grows. The company is also in talks with international ports about using the technology to spot nuclear materials in cargo. The company, which also has a branch in Tallinn, plans to grow to about 25 staff by the end of 2027. It is now hiring seven physicists and engineers in Hamburg, including founding leads for its AI and muon source teams. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Lovable opens its first London office with 70 staff
2026-10-29

Lovable opens its first London office with 70 staff

The Next Web | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Lovable has opened its first London office, giving the Swedish AI app builder a second home for its engineering, product and design teams. Around 70 people are already working there, and the company plans to increase that number to 100 by the end of the year. The company announced the opening on Thursday. Its London team spans nearly every part of the business, from AI research, security and infrastructure to sales, marketing and finance. The office will also house the design team led by Nad Chishtie, along with forward-deployed engineers who work directly with business customers. Staff from Radish AI, the startup Lovable acquired to strengthen its work on AI agents and infrastructure, are based there too. For Lovable, the move puts it closer to a growing pool of users and potential hires. The UK is its third-largest market by paying users, and one of the countries with the most founders building with its platform, the company said. Lovable lets people create websites and apps by describing what they want in plain language, an approach often called vibe coding. London’s talent pool was one reason for choosing the city, the company said. Its existing community of users was another. Having teams on the ground means Lovable can work more closely with the people building products on its platform. The opening follows a broader push by AI companies into the UK. During London Tech Week in June, fast-growing firms including Cursor and Legora announced plans to expand their presence in the country. Lovable’s growth has been driven by people using the platform to build everything from websites to internal business tools. Users have created more than 70 million projects, with over 1.2 million new projects added each week. Websites built with Lovable receive more than 900 million visits a month, according to the company. British businesses are among those putting it to work. At meal-replacement brand Huel, around 300 employees have built more than 200 internal tools using the platform. Lovable says this has helped the company cut more than £85,000 a year in software spending. The expansion comes two months after Lovable raised $400m in August at a $13.3bn valuation. The funding round was co-led by Menlo Ventures and the EU-backed Scaleup Europe Fund. At the time, Lovable said it planned to increase its workforce by 50% to around 450 people this year. Google Cloud revenue doubles to $30.7bn, beating forecasts

The Next Web | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Google Cloud more than doubled its revenue in the third quarter, beating analysts’ forecasts, Alphabet said on Wednesday. Weaker growth in search advertising, however, meant the group as a whole fell short of expectations. Cloud revenue rose 103% from a year earlier to $30.7bn, according to Alphabet’s quarterly results. Analysts had expected $29.57bn, Reuters reported, citing LSEG data. The unit’s operating profit climbed to $11.1bn, from $3.6bn a year earlier. Google Cloud is the world’s third-largest cloud provider, behind Amazon Web Services and Microsoft Azure. Use of its AI models is also growing. Pichai said Google’s systems now process nearly 32 billion tokens a minute, more than four times the level a year ago. The Gemini app has about 1 billion users, he added. Cloud customers have also signed up for much more work ahead. The unit’s backlog of contracted revenue reached $561bn, and Alphabet expects to recognise more than 55% of it within two years, according to Reuters. “We had a strong quarter and are now on a half-trillion dollar revenue run rate,” said Sundar Pichai, Alphabet’s chief executive. Alphabet’s total revenue grew 25% to $127.6bn, slightly below the $128.2bn analysts had forecast. Advertising brought in $82bn against expectations of $83.4bn, as more people used Google’s AI Mode in search, Reuters reported. Spending on AI infrastructure also kept rising. Alphabet spent a record $51.4bn on property and equipment in the quarter, more than double a year earlier. As a result, its free cash flow was negative $9.1bn for a second straight quarter. The company kept its forecast for this year’s capital spending at $195bn to $205bn, the range it set in July. Its finance chief said spending would rise significantly again in 2027 but gave no figure. Net income fell 25% to $26.2bn, mainly because of an $8.7bn loss on equity investments. Alphabet shares dropped about 10% in after-hours trading. Demand for Google’s own AI chips is also part of the cloud story. Atlassian people chief Avani Prabhakar calls AI mandates a ‘very bad start’
2026-10-29

Atlassian people chief Avani Prabhakar calls AI mandates a ‘very bad start’

The Next Web | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
TNW’s Cristian Dina (left) and Atlassian Chief People Officer Avani Prabhakar at HumanX in Amsterdam, 24 September 2026 One AI “super user” in a team of five to seven people lifts the whole team’s AI adoption and usage by 56%, Atlassian’s Avani Prabhakar told TNW. Atlassian counts someone as a super user if they use AI’s most sophisticated features more than 40 times a week to change a workflow, she said. It tracks who they are. Prabhakar is Atlassian’s chief people and AI enablement officer, a role it announced in April, after she served as chief people officer. She joined the company seven and a half years ago and is based in Sydney. She started her career at GE in the US in 2001, in the business rather than in HR. She later spent seven years at British Airways and worked at Fuji Xerox. She spoke to Cristian Dina at HumanX in Amsterdam for the TNW Podcast, after episodes with Boomi’s Steve Lucas and Remote’s Job van der Voort. The full conversation is in the video below. Companies should treat AI as a people transformation, Prabhakar said. Deploying it is the easy part, and so is getting people to like it. Big companies get into trouble on security and governance, and on cost. So every team at Atlassian gets what it calls an AI working agreement. It sets out where AI can be used in that job and where it cannot. A product researcher cannot put company information into an AI tool, for example. A recruiter is told which decisions are theirs and which are the AI’s. On cost, every employee gets an AI wallet, a monthly allowance that depends on their role, she said. Once they hit the limit, they need approval for more and must explain the usage and the workflow it improves. TNW reported on the engineers’ AI budget in July. “Do not reward token usage, just monitor token usage, because that drives very wrong behavior.” Avani Prabhakar, Atlassian The bigger saving came from the Teamwork Graph, Atlassian’s internal context layer, she said. It pulls knowledge, conversations, people, assets and code into one graph. Sending Claude to search only that graph cut token use by 48%, and accuracy rose by 44%, she said. Making AI use a mandate tied to performance is “a very bad start,” Prabhakar said, because people start gaming it. Nor should companies build a separate chief AI officer team on the side, the way they once set up digital transformation offices. And asking for a business case before anyone can try AI kills experimentation. “ROI is an outcome. It’s not an entry fee.” Avani Prabhakar Atlassian works through tasks, skills and roles, in that order. For now, everyone sees AI’s benefits at the level of tasks and workflows, she said. Anyone claiming AI has replaced a role is producing “a lot of AI slop.” On skills, Prabhakar runs a team of about 13 behavioural scientists, and Atlassian has partnered with Stanford on experiments. One found that graduates make decisions based on AI 19% more effectively than senior managers, she said. Atlassian has also set up about 30 greenfield projects run by teams of five or six. In those teams, engineers build prototypes and designers commit code. On roles, it has created a new job family, the design technologist, for designers whose work has changed with AI. Atlassian has about 12,000 employees in more than 3,000 cities, and no office mandate. Staff can work from home, from an office or from anywhere. It writes more and presents less, and it sends Loom recordings rather than always holding meetings. That written, asynchronous habit gives AI the context to read and write better, she said. Prabhakar now spends 60% to 70% of her time with customers. Her brief is to build a “customer zero” story: run the experiments inside Atlassian, write the playbook, and take it to large companies. Her advice to graduates comes from talks with her daughter, who wants to study law. Follow what gives you joy, she said, and learn AI alongside it. “Don’t make your career choices out of fear.” Avani Prabhakar Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Samsung posts record $80bn quarterly profit on AI memory demand
2026-10-29

Samsung posts record $80bn quarterly profit on AI memory demand

The Next Web | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Samsung Electronics made a record operating profit of 107.4 trillion won (about $80bn) in the third quarter, the company said on Thursday. Demand for the memory chips used in AI data centres kept rising. The figure confirms the preliminary results Samsung published earlier this month, when it said profit was about nine times higher than a year earlier. Revenue also hit a new high of 195.3 trillion won, up 14% from the previous quarter, according to Samsung’s full results. Almost all of that profit came from chips. Samsung’s semiconductor business earned 106.5 trillion won on revenue of 147.1 trillion won. Its memory unit set new quarterly records for both sales and profit, thanks to strong server demand and higher prices. Sales of HBM4, the latest generation of high-bandwidth memory that sits alongside AI processors, grew significantly during the quarter, the company said. Its contract chipmaking arm also did better, helped by sales of the base chips used in HBM. Other parts of the business struggled. The mobile and networks division, which makes Galaxy phones, posted an operating loss of 0.6 trillion won as costs rose, while TVs and home appliances lost 0.2 trillion won. The display unit made a profit of 1.1 trillion won. To keep up with demand, Samsung spent 29 trillion won on new plants and equipment in the quarter, more than double the previous three months. It expects to spend about 84.4 trillion won across the whole year. Shareholders will also benefit. Samsung declared a quarterly dividend of 4,601 won per share, worth 30 trillion won in total. It also repeated its plan to return 90 to 110 trillion won to shareholders this year. For the fourth quarter, Samsung expects memory sales to keep growing as new CPU and GPU platforms reach the market. It also forecasts double-digit revenue growth at its foundry business. Rival SK Hynix also reported record profit this week and said it expects the memory shortage to last until 2030. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks!
